# Blood Money and Blue Diamonds

Book I — Version D

The main revision — Aiden's recollection

## 1. The Funeral Had a Two-Drink Minimum

“Nigga, it’s my motherfucking funeral,” I told the man taking tickets.

He sat behind a folding table with a cash box between his knees and a rubber stamp beside his elbow. Velvet curtains covered the ballroom entrance behind him. Through them came bass, laughter, and the smell of hot butter. I stood there hearing a roomful of people enjoy my death while this motherfucker prepared to charge me for access.

“Forty dollars,” the ticket man said, holding out his hand.

I waited. The memorial banner above him had my name on it. The photograph beside him had my whole face. I gave him another clue anyway.

“I’m Aiden.” I pointed at the picture.

He checked the photograph, then checked me. Whoever had made the poster had started with a picture of me getting arrested. They had cropped out the cuffs and added angel wings but left the officer’s hand on my shoulder. I had a police escort into heaven.

“You look better in the picture,” the ticket man decided.

“Nigga, I’m alive.” I spread my arms so he could inspect the evidence.

The ticket man tapped his cash box. “Then you can pay.”

This was Vegas, after we came home. I had been back long enough for my problems to acquire a cover charge. Beneath those gold letters, I wondered whether a genuine corpse got in free or whether somebody checked its pockets.

I stepped out of the line and called Tank. A fork tapped a plate on the other end. I could hear a faint version of the same sound through the curtains.

“Why there a cover charge at my funeral?” I asked my brother.

“The shrimp was expensive,” Tank said around something he was chewing.

I watched a woman come through the curtains with cocktail sauce shining at the corner of her mouth. “Why do I have shrimp?”

“You died successful,” Tank told me.

I listened to him chew again. “Nigga, are you eating?”

“Grieving,” Tank said.

I hung up, paid the forty, and offered my wrist. The ticket man stamped DECEASED above my watch. I looked at the wet purple letters while he put the stamp away.

“That gets you one well drink,” he said.

I pointed at the sign beside his table. “The sign says two-drink minimum.”

“Second one’s your responsibility.” The ticket man reached around me for the next person’s money.

A man waiting behind me leaned forward, looking from my wrist to the picture.

“Hold up,” that man said. “Ain’t that the deceased?”

“Paid deceased,” the ticket man answered after checking my stamp.

The man in line brought out his phone. “Get a picture? People'll think I knew you personally.”

“Meeting personally right now,” I told him. “Both our hearts fucking beating.”

He turned the phone upright and shook his head.

“Yeah,” he told me, “but the funeral picture hit different.”

He put his arm around my shoulder and raised the phone. I held my own memorial program against my chest while he took the picture. On the screen, he looked delighted to have lost me. I left him beside the poster checking whether my death needed a filter and went in.

The ballroom opened around rows of round tables. Along the right wall, a mirror ran behind the bar; at the far end, two shallow steps led to a low stage. The band occupied its back half: drums, piano, two keyboards, and enough brass to invade somewhere with confidence. My coffin stood on a wheeled stand at the front of the platform, covered in flowers. A small card resting on the flowers reminded mourners to tip the staff.

I stopped beside the chocolate fountain and looked for my people.

Tank stood near the stage with a plate. Porsche moved behind the musicians carrying our duplicate silver case. Mercedes waited below the steps in a red suit, pressing her thumb into her palm. I had watched her do that all week.

Then I found Shawna coming toward me, and the rest of the room became something I could check later.

My wife wore black, with a slit up one leg and a diamond at her throat. I had watched her arrange the hat at home. She tilted it, considered the mirror, and tilted it farther. By the time she reached me at the funeral, I would have paid to be the dead man who got to disappoint that widow by coming back.

I held up a program I had picked off a table. “Why this say I’m survived by eleven children?”

“Those are sponsors,” Shawna said, putting a finger beneath the small print.

I followed her finger. “Nigga, one of them’s Jeremiah.”

“Jeremiah’s Collision Repair,” she read to me.

I folded the program and slipped my hand around her waist. “You enjoying widowhood?”

Shawna looked up at me beneath the brim. “The parking is excellent.”

I pulled her closer. She let me, then put two fingers against my mouth. Her smile stayed where the room could see it. The pressure of her fingers told me to listen.

“Eight minutes,” she said. “Kitchen. Stay there until we get the case.”

The kitchen door stood left of the stage. I could see its round window above a waiter’s shoulder. Beyond the piano, another doorway led backstage, where Mercedes was supposed to inspect the recordings Silas Gold had stolen. Porsche would switch the case during the inspection. I had attended the planning conversation. My contribution, according to the women who loved me, was to remain out of fucking sight.

“Soon as he walks in, I could take it,” I told Shawna.

She turned her eyes toward the crowded tables. “And his men could start shooting into everybody who paid to eat shrimp.”

“Apparently excellent shrimp,” I said, following her glance.

“Aiden.” Shawna took her fingers off my mouth and put her palm flat against my chest.

I stopped arguing.

Silas had the original recordings of Mercedes’s first album. She had told me exactly what was on that drive: the takes, the musicians, the late nights in a studio that had since been demolished. She could sing the songs again. She couldn't put those people back in that room on the same night. In the final chorus of one track, her voice had cracked. She had kept it because, she told me, that was where she sounded most like herself.

I had seen her explain this without crying, her thumb pressed so hard into her palm that the skin changed color. Watching her beside the stage now, I wanted to get the drive into her hand before she had to explain a damn thing again.

Silas usually conducted business from behind bulletproof glass. He sent other men out to be unpleasant. But when Shawna invited him to a private auction of my belongings after the memorial, he agreed to come. My watch, my cars, pieces of the collection: she had described the offer to me afterward, including the way his voice warmed up when she mentioned the cars. He would bring Mercedes’s recordings so everything could be settled together.

Apparently my death offered the motherfucker a compelling reason to leave his aquarium.

Mercedes looked up, met my eyes, and came over. She adjusted my collar. It had been straight when she arrived, but I held still beneath her hands.

“Don’t do the thing,” she told me.

“You gotta narrow that down,” I said. “I got a substantial range.”

Mercedes looked around my funeral. “The thing where this becomes about you.”

I nodded toward the entrance. “My photograph is six feet tall.”

“Exactly,” she said. “The nigga has enough publicity.”

I stopped smiling and covered her hand where it rested on my shoulder.

“You’ll get your music,” I told her.

Mercedes squeezed my shoulder. “I know. I want to enjoy getting it.”

I took my hand off hers. For once, I had managed to promise something without deciding how she'd have to enjoy it.

She returned to the stage. Shawna kissed me, put both hands on my shoulders, and turned me toward the kitchen.

“In there,” my wife said. “Be a beautiful secret for eight minutes.”

“Y’all asking a lot of secrecy,” I told her, but I went.

The cook gave me a place beside a steel prep table. An old speaker above the shelves carried the stage microphones; a little service microphone beside it allowed staff to make announcements. I could see the stage through the round window in the door. The cook kept peeling potatoes while Tank climbed the steps with a plate in one hand.

My brother took the microphone in the other. “We gathered here because Aiden would want these niggas supporting the bar.”

Glasses rose across the ballroom. I watched Tank wait for them to come down before he continued.

“My brother was generous,” he said. “Gave me the shirt off his back.”

I leaned toward the service microphone. “Police was looking for a man in a red shirt.”

The laugh reached me through the wall before it came through the speaker. I pushed the door open to appreciate my work. Shawna raised one finger without turning around. I went back inside, and the cook moved a tray of shrimp away from my elbow.

Tank let the room settle.

“But my nigga came back,” he said.

I put my palm against the door.

He told them about the station. I had said he hadn't done shit, paid what they demanded, waited six hours, and bought him breakfast when they finally let him out. As he spoke, I remembered the hard chair and the cold coffee. I remembered Tank coming through the door and seeing me still there. His shoulders had dropped before he put the smile on.

My brother sniffed into the microphone. “Then the nigga asked for his shirt.”

The drummer put a small roll beneath the laugh. In the kitchen, I wiped my mouth with a napkin I hadn't needed a moment earlier.

The laughter hadn't quite stopped when Silas arrived.

I saw the white fur coat moving between the tables. Four men followed. The smallest carried the silver case against his leg. The largest walked with his elbows out, although his shoulders had already taken responsibility for blocking most of the aisle.

Silas held up a bottle as he approached the stage.

“Miss the good part?” he asked Tank.

My brother looked at the coffin. “Depends how this speech goes.”

Silas laughed. His men joined a beat later. From the kitchen, I watched that little delay and filed it with the other things I disliked about him.

Shawna met him at the steps. He bent over her offered hand to kiss it. She moved just far enough for his mouth to meet his own thumb. I pressed my lips together to keep from making another contribution through the service microphone.

“The recordings,” Shawna said, holding her hand out again.

“After my tribute,” Silas told her.

My wife kept her hand where it was. “Mercedes inspects them first. That was the agreement.”

Silas gestured at the crowd with his bottle. “Look at this room. Let a man have his moment.”

He climbed onto the platform, took the microphone from Tank, and put the bottle on my coffin. Tank picked it up and handed it back. Silas put it down again. Tank returned it again. The drummer followed the bottle with his eyes and supplied a little tick each time it changed hands.

Silas turned toward my brother. “You got somewhere to be?”

“Eventually,” Tank said, still holding the bottle out to him.

Silas took it and faced the room.

“Aiden and I had differences,” he announced.

I remembered one of those differences very clearly. I had owned my boat. He had stolen it. I waited to hear whether he would mention that part.

Instead, he talked about my appetites. Then he looked at Shawna and suggested I had left people unprovided for.

“I would never presume—” Silas began.

“Then don’t,” my wife told him.

A woman near the front slapped her table. “That nigga got instructions!” she called.

Silas stood through the laughter with his teeth showing. I could see his fingers tighten around the microphone. When the room quieted, he called Mercedes’s name.

She stayed below the steps until the smallest guard carried the case onto the platform and opened it. Silas put his bottle back on the coffin, took out the drive, and held it by one end. He waved the guard away. The man came back down the steps with the open, empty case in his left hand. Mercedes’s eyes followed the drive. Each time Silas moved his hand, her eyes moved too.

“Sing us a tribute,” Silas told her. “Then you can apologize for what you’ve been saying about me.”

Mercedes put one foot on the bottom step. “You promised an inspection.”

“Sing first.” Silas offered her the microphone, then pulled it back before she could reach it. “Ask nicely.”

Through the window, I saw the biggest guard move across the backstage doorway. Porsche stopped behind him with the duplicate case. She leaned to see past his shoulder, but he shifted with her. The route we had planned was closed.

Mercedes took her foot off the step. Her empty hand opened at her side.

Her heel settled on the carpet. That little movement stays with me more clearly than the gunshot that came afterward. Silas had got her to step away from her own music. My hand closed around the button of my jacket.

I took my jacket off.

The cook looked up from his potatoes. “You want me to hold that?”

“Please,” I said, handing it to him.

He hung it clear of the steam. “You coming back?”

I picked up the shrimp tray and checked the window one last time. “Apparently I do.”

I came through the kitchen door without hurrying. Shawna saw the tray, looked toward the stage, and pulled her chair clear of the aisle. Tank put down his plate. I saw both movements and kept walking.

A woman beside the aisle turned from me to the coffin.

“Lord,” she whispered.

Her companion began crossing himself. The woman caught his forearm.

“Don’t do that,” she told him. “He got shrimp.”

“Could still be spiritual,” the man answered, watching my tray.

His companion pulled at his chair. “Move your chair, nigga.”

He moved it. I went past with the shrimp level and stopped beside the smallest guard at the foot of the stage.

“Shrimp?” I asked, tilting the tray toward him.

The guard took one. I offered the sauce, and he dipped carefully. I watched him get it all the way to his mouth before climbing the two steps onto the platform. If he was going to have a bad evening, I could at least make sure he ate first.

Silas heard me speaking to his man. He spun around so fast the fur collar slapped his mouth. His eyes went to the coffin, then back to me.

Forty dollars. Worth it.

“Nigga, it’s my motherfucking funeral,” I told him.

He shoved the microphone into its stand and reached inside his coat. I saw the pistol clear the fur. I drove the edge of the tray up beneath his wrist.

The shot went into the ceiling. The gun flew out of his hand, struck the stage, and skidded under the piano. Plaster burst over the musicians. The trumpet player ducked with both arms around his instrument, and shrimp scattered across the platform.

People screamed. Tank jumped down the steps and hauled two guests behind a table. Shawna stood in the aisle holding the chair she had moved. I saw her settle both hands around its back as I caught Silas by his coat and pulled him away from the edge.

“Everybody else brought flowers!” I shouted at him.

Silas grabbed the bottle from the coffin and swung. Glass broke against my shoulder.

Pain ran down my arm. My grip loosened, but a fistful of fur stayed caught in my fingers. For one breath I couldn't tell whether the warmth spreading beneath my shirt was liquor or blood. Then I could tell. The improvement in my understanding did nothing for my fucking disposition.

I hit him with the tray.

His head went sideways. The drive flew from his other hand, and Mercedes stepped into its path, catching it against her chest.

“Got it!” Mercedes shouted as she climbed the last step onto the platform.

The big guard left the backstage doorway and hurried toward me. Porsche came through behind him. At the top of the steps, he planted a foot to turn. She caught up and swung the duplicate case into his ankle.

His foot stopped. The rest of him went down the steps into Shawna’s chair.

Wood broke across his mouth. He landed sitting among the pieces, touching his teeth with one hand. Shawna stood above him holding what remained of the chair back.

“Stay,” my wife told him.

He looked up at her and stayed.

I still held Silas’s coat. In the mirror behind the bar, I saw another guard draw behind me. Mercedes had tucked the drive inside her jacket. She followed my glance toward the mirror, seized the microphone stand with both hands, and swung it as I ducked.

The stand caught the guard across the chest and drove him into the drum kit. A cymbal folded against its mount. The drummer stood up, looked at it, and then looked at the man tangled underneath.

“Nigga, that’s vintage,” the drummer said.

He picked up his stool and brought the padded seat down across the guard’s shoulder and back. The man collapsed over the bass drum with his arms covering his head. I had never seen a percussion section make its complaint so directly.

Tank came back up the steps. At the same moment, Silas wriggled out of his coat and ran straight toward him. My brother caught him around the waist, lifted the coffin lid with his free hand, and tipped him inside.

The lid banged shut. Something squealed underneath it.

Tank froze.

I knew that look. I had seen it on him before an important detail arrived much too fucking late.

The lid flew open. A little man in a gold tracksuit sat up underneath Silas. He wore my spare sunglasses, knocked sideways across his face. He shoved one lens back over his eye and pointed at Tank.

“NIGGA, I TOLD YOU NINE-FIFTEEN!” the little man shouted.

I let Silas’s fur fall out of my hand. “You hired a corpse?”

Tank looked at his shoes. “Resurrection package.”

“A resurrection package,” I repeated, looking at the man climbing out from beneath Silas.

“It included the dance,” Tank explained.

The performer lifted a finger. “And fog.”

My brother pointed at the dust hanging above the stage. “We got atmosphere.”

“That’s ceiling,” the performer told him.

“Work with the venue,” Tank said.

The little man climbed over Silas’s stomach, stepped onto the platform, and straightened his tracksuit. He explained that he did the surprise, he did the dance, and he left. Nobody, he informed us, had mentioned wrestling.

Silas’s fourth man began edging toward the entrance. Tank saw him and pointed without lowering his voice.

“Sit your ass down. The nigga hasn’t danced,” my brother told him.

The man sat. Below the steps, the smallest guard lowered the empty case to the floor. He still had a shrimp tail in his other hand.

“I’m with catering,” that guard announced.

The cook leaned out of the kitchen. “No the fuck you are not.”

It took us seven minutes to gather the guns, tie up the men, and convince the room that the bar remained open. I watched people come out from behind tables, feel around for bags and shoes, and begin telling their neighbors what those same neighbors had just seen.

Silas kept trying to sit up in the coffin. Each time, the performer eased him down with a gold sneaker against his forehead.

“Stop touching my face,” Silas complained.

“Stop being in my office,” the little man told him.

Mercedes carried the drive to the sound desk. I followed far enough to see her take it out of her jacket and hand it to the technician. He connected it, moved aside, and gave her headphones. Files filled the screen.

She chose one. Porsche took her hand beneath the desk. I started closer, and Shawna caught the back of my belt.

I stopped beside my wife. The cut in my shoulder throbbed, but I could keep it bearable by holding the arm still. I watched Mercedes close her eyes. None of us spoke while she listened.

She removed the headphones and wiped beneath one eye.

“All here,” Mercedes said.

Tank let out a breath beside me. “Then play one, my nigga.”

Mercedes looked around the room. I followed her glance across fallen flowers, memorial napkins pressed to bleeding faces, and my huge photograph leaning crookedly near the entrance. Then she went toward the stage.

The drummer was fitting a spare cymbal. Mercedes stopped beside him.

“You good?” she asked him.

“You doing something slow?” the drummer asked, holding the wing nut in place.

“No,” Mercedes said.

He tightened the nut and sat down. “Then yeah.”

She gave the technician a track number. A hiss came through the speakers, then saxophone, warm and rough. I knew from what she had told me that I was hearing the room they couldn't rebuild. The band listened for a bar and joined the recording.

Mercedes pointed toward my photograph.

“Turn that nigga around,” she told me. “I need the room.”

I turned it myself.

She began low, almost speaking to the nearest table. Then the bass came beneath her. She planted one heel against the monitor and raised her chin. Her next line reached the back of the ballroom. People stood between the fallen chairs, reaching for one another’s hands.

The little man closed the coffin, stepped onto it, and checked the time. Silas pounded beneath his shoes.

“Get off!” Silas shouted from inside.

“Nine-fifteen, bitch!” the performer answered.

Then he danced. Tank threw both hands up.

“That nigga earned his deposit!” my brother called.

I watched Porsche laugh with her face lifted toward Mercedes. The cook came out with my jacket over his shoulder and a tray in his hands. A woman took the tray, put it on a table, and caught his hand before he could retreat.

“Nigga, I’m working,” the cook protested as she pulled him toward the open floor.

“Not anymore,” the woman told him.

Shawna slipped her fingers into my good hand. I looked down and found her studying the blood on my sleeve.

“You’re bleeding,” my wife said.

“Little bit,” I told her.

She touched near the cut. I winced, and she took her hand away.

“We’ll get it looked at,” Shawna said.

I turned toward the stage. “After the song.”

She stayed against my unhurt side.

The last chorus came. Through the speakers, Mercedes’s younger voice caught for half a breath. I heard the crack she had described. Onstage, Mercedes sang beside her own recording and smiled. Porsche stopped laughing to listen. I saw Mercedes look toward her and keep singing.

I loosened my grip on Shawna's hand. Mercedes had her heel on the monitor, her voice in the room, and no need to ask anybody nicely.

Shawna leaned close to my ear. “Eleven sponsors is a lot of mouths to feed.”

I laughed, hurt my shoulder, and laughed again. Shawna smiled against my cheek. She had caught me with that kind of straight-faced bullshit plenty of times, and I still walked right into it.

The ticket man pulled the entrance curtain aside for a new group.

“Sixty dollars,” he told them.

I pointed across the room. “Nigga, it was forty!”

He gestured at Mercedes, the coffin dancer, and my upright, bleeding ass.

“Live show now,” the ticket man explained.

By the time the police took Silas away, the band had demanded overtime and Tank had begun negotiating for the leftover shrimp. I stood near the sound desk with Shawna, reluctant to leave the room while it still held the music.

The young technician brought two clean glasses. His headphones hung around his neck. He kept looking between Shawna and the DECEASED stamp on my wrist, trying so hard not to ask something that I could almost hear the question.

“Can I ask something?” the technician said.

I held out my stamped wrist. “Apparently you can charge for it.”

He smiled and looked at Shawna. “How’d you two meet?”

My wife laughed before I could start. “Let me tell it.”

“Baby, I was there,” I reminded her.

“So was I,” Shawna said. “One of us remembers who saved whom.”

The technician pulled up a chair. Tank called for somebody to get him a plate.

“I’m on the clock,” the young man told my brother.

“So am I,” Tank answered. “I organized a death.”

Mercedes handed the technician a napkin. Porsche moved the bottle closer. I watched him loosen the headphones around his neck and sit down. I pulled up a chair across from him, lowering myself carefully so my shoulder wouldn't argue. Tank dragged over a chair for himself. Shawna sat beside me, against my good side, with one of the clean glasses.

I looked at the man who had asked the question. “The night I met her in Vegas. You want the whole thing?”

He nodded. My wife made herself comfortable.

“Start before you became reasonable,” Shawna told me.

“We still waiting on that chapter,” I said.

Tank laughed into his shrimp. I took a drink and let the ballroom quiet around the edges. The musicians were packing their instruments. Somebody swept plaster off the stage. My wife’s knee rested against mine.

Before the house, before the ring, I'd have seen a man holding hands at his own funeral and wondered what the fuck he'd done to his life. Shawna moved her knee against mine. I let myself remember how little I'd known when I first took her hand.

The bass had been shaking a different wall that first night. Big T had his hand on my shoulder. Eleven other men waited beside the cars.

I was twenty-four, and I still thought I could see all the trouble in front of me.

## 2. First Night in Vegas

The night I met Shawna, cocaine had settled in the fold of my collar and somebody else’s blood had worked its way inside my watch. It had dried beneath the rim of the glass. Every time I checked the hour, there it was: a little red reminder that somewhere in this city I either owed a man an apology or needed to finish the job.

I was twenty-four, and I thought damage was a way of keeping score.

My last car had burned outside my ex-boyfriend’s apartment. He told me that meant I wanted him back. I had reached the point in my life where even my insurance claims were being interpreted romantically. That afternoon I had eaten a gas-station sandwich in a borrowed car. Before the sun came up again, I would be carrying three million dollars, running with a woman half of Vegas wanted delivered to them, and bleeding around a bullet I would keep for the rest of my life.

But all that was still ahead of me. Behind the nightclub, under the yellow security light, twelve men were preparing to beat my ass, and I wanted them to like me when they finished.

Big T had walked me out with his hand on my shoulder. He kept it there while the door closed and the music became a deep thump on the other side of the brick. The rear lot smelled of hot engines and spilled beer. Cars stood at angles around an open patch of concrete. Men leaned against the hoods or rolled their shoulders beside the doors, blue rags showing from pockets, chains flashing when they moved beneath the light.

I knew some of them. Two had borrowed money from me. One still owed me ten dollars and had apparently decided paying it back would be easier if I lost the ability to remember numbers.

T drew me into the middle, his fingers pressing through my shirt.

“Once a nigga in, that nigga in,” he told me. “You understand what I'm saying?”

“I understand,” I told him.

T kept his hand on my shoulder. “Summer ends. Somebody drops. Suddenly a nigga got somewhere else to be. That you?”

“I got nowhere else to be,” I said. “That’s why I’m standing here instead of enjoying the fucking music.”

A laugh escaped from behind a Cadillac. T didn’t join it.

“This funny to you?” he asked.

I looked around the ring of men assembled to determine my sincerity through orthopedic damage.

“I think twelve is a committee,” I told T. “Which one of you takes the minutes?”

T drove me backward into the Cadillac. My spine hit the edge of the hood and the breath jumped out of me.

That answered it. T took the minutes.

The other eleven came in with amendments.

A fist split my lip. Something hard caught behind my ear before I had turned toward the first blow. I tried to get my elbows in, but a kick knocked my leg out and I hit the ground on my palms. Grit bit into the skin. Shoes filled the little space I could see beneath the cars.

I got up because I had come there to get up. That was about the size of my thinking at twenty-four. I had spent too long being somebody’s temporary guest, somebody’s good time, somebody’s useful man until the useful part was over. T was offering me a place where my name would stay after I left the room. I was willing to pay for that place with whatever these motherfuckers could reach.

Blood ran into one eye. I swung toward a shoulder with the eye that still worked, felt my knuckles connect, and heard somebody tell me to stay down.

“Y’all just told me to stay IN,” I shouted. “Pick an instruction!”

Then Tank hit me in the ribs.

He was twenty-eight—I learned that later—almost twice my size, and broad enough that his shirt had given up pretending the buttons were a permanent arrangement. The punch bent me around his arm. I caught a fistful of that failing shirt before he could draw back and drove with my legs, taking him into the next car.

The alarm went off beneath us.

“Not the paint!” somebody yelled.

I hit Tank again. “You should’ve thought about the paint before you started throwing people at it!”

He tried to turn me off his chest, but I was too close and too angry to assist him. I kept my fist in his shirt and used the other hand wherever I could find room. He called for somebody to get me off him. T laughed. The others watched. Through the alarm I heard Tank shout T’s name. He kept looking past my shoulder toward him while nobody moved to help. I wasn't the only nigga finding the induction process disappointing.

“You said he was little,” T reminded him.

Tank caught another blow on his forearm. “Nigga, I have new information!”

T finally hauled me away by the shoulders. I stood in the open with my fists still up. My legs shook. My mouth tasted of pennies and whiskey, and I couldn't uncurl my fingers even after the men stopped coming.

“Enough,” T said. He grinned as he looked around at them. “Twelve niggas. Look at him. Still shopping.”

I told him I was done. When he asked whether I was sure, I looked at the fists I was apparently still offering to the room.

“I’m waiting for my hands to hear it,” I told him.

T laughed, and several of the others joined him. Then he took a gold chain out of his pocket, opened it wide, and lowered it over my head. I bowed automatically to let him. A small cross dropped against the sore place above my ribs.

The men went quiet before I did.

“My brother wore that,” T said. “Real nigga.”

I lifted the cross. It was warm from his pocket. I asked where his brother was so I could thank him.

“Dead,” T said.

The car alarm chose that moment to stop. I heard T draw a breath through his nose, and because he was so close I could see the dampness at the edge of his eye.

“Don’t make me bury you too,” T told me, keeping one hand on my shoulder.

A minute earlier, he had been hitting me. Now he had found a way to make me stand there trying not to cry. I closed my hand over the gold as if accepting it required me to keep it warm.

“Not this nigga,” I promised.

T kissed my forehead. Then he turned me toward the club and called for the men to make room for his son.

I remember believing him. I won't improve the story by pretending I was cleverer than I was. He had hurt me, stopped the hurting, and called the space after it love. At the time, that felt enough like a home for me to walk through the door.

Tank held it open. Out of some bruised instinct toward manners, I reached to help him. He moved his shoulder away from my hand.

“Walk through, nigga,” Tank said, holding the door away from my hand. “I can still defeat a door.”

Inside, blue light lay across the smoke. The back room was warmer than the lot, crowded around a glass table buried in money and white bricks. Men separated bills into stacks. Bottles of Hennessy stood wherever the cash left room. Three women in diamonds drank beside a man with two pistols tucked into one belt, as if his trousers had enemies on both sides.

One of the women looked at my face and asked whether I had survived a war.

“Yeah,” I said, taking the chair somebody pushed toward me. “And I won.”

They cheered. A bottle arrived in one hand, a burning blunt in the other. I drank, smoked, and wiped my mouth against my wrist before I remembered what was already on the watch. The pile of cash beside my elbow was bigger than any amount I had ever been trusted to sit near.

The woman wanted to know if I was the one who had beaten Tank. T said I was. She folded a thousand-dollar bill into my shirt pocket. Ninety seconds back inside and somebody was paying me to be handsome with a broken face. I began to think the committee’s compensation package deserved another look.

Tank came in holding ice against his jaw. He took the bottle from me, drank, and gave it back. I waited to find out whether the next thing between us would be another punch.

Tank pressed the ice against his jaw. “Who taught you to fight dirty like that?”

“Same nigga taught you to fight unconscious,” I told him, keeping the bottle ready in case this resumed.

The room broke open. Tank stared at me while everybody else laughed. Then his face changed. He began laughing too, blood showing between his teeth, and dragged a chair up beside mine.

We drank out of the same bottle. I passed it over; he passed it back. I stopped watching his free hand quite so closely. Before the ice against his jaw had melted, I would have handed him my heart to hold while I fought with both hands.

T tossed a set of keys across the table. I caught them against the bottle.

“Black Mercedes,” he said. “That’s yours.”

I turned the keys over, feeling the little badge beneath my thumb. An hour earlier, I had wanted a place in his crew. Now he was telling me the west side needed a man and the west side had me.

I asked whether he was serious. He nodded toward the keys, as if there was no point discussing an answer already in my hand.

I sat taller. My ribs objected. I ignored them. The woman’s thousand dollars crackled in my pocket whenever I moved, adding its own applause.

Then T showed me a photograph on his phone.

The woman in it stood beside a pool at night. The Strip burned behind her. She wore black, diamonds at her ears, dark hair over one shoulder. She wasn't smiling for whoever held the camera. I looked at her face and forgot to admire the city.

“Shawna,” T said. “Twenty-six. Works for Red.”

I asked what sort of work. Whatever Red wanted, he told me, and moved on before I could decide how much I disliked the answer. She would be at the Saint, in the penthouse. At eleven she would bring down a case. I was to take her and the money to T’s house in Henderson.

Across the table, Tank watched me looking at the photograph.

“Why's she leaving Red?” I asked.

“Finally got smart,” T said.

“Red know about this little family pickup?”

T looked at me across the photograph. “If he knew, we wouldn't be having the conversation.”

Then he took the back of my neck and drew me close enough that his next words were only for me.

“Don't fall in love with her,” T said close to my ear.

I laughed into his face. He kept his hand on my neck until I looked at him properly.

“Laugh when the job's done,” T told me. “I'm serious.”

“Look at me, nigga,” I said. “Do I look like the nigga falls in love?”

He released my neck. I sat back with the keys and believed every word I had just said. I had managed to become wrong about my whole future before I had even learned the radio in my new car.

The Saint rose over a gold fountain and a line of waiting vehicles. Men I knew from the clubs had told me how Red ran it: he owned the penthouse, the nightclub beneath it, and enough people between those floors that a man could disappear into a service elevator without anybody at the desk looking up. At the top, bulletproof windows reflected a city that was encouraged to admire from outside.

I parked the Mercedes at the entrance. The valet saw the drying blood on my collar and asked whether I needed a doctor.

“My car needs you first,” I told him, putting a hundred in his hand. “Nothing happens to it.”

The car still had all its windows when I said that. I like to remember we had those few minutes together.

Upstairs, the penthouse opened into music, glass, and more money being treated as if it had grown there. Men gambled with bank-wrapped bundles. Cocaine dusted tables between drinks. Women in dresses worth more than the cars outside laughed at men who introduced themselves with names I wasn't about to verify.

A man in a white fur coat bent over a trophy to do a line. It was September in Vegas. He had brought both the heat problem and the trophy himself. When somebody reached past him for a drink, he hugged the award to his chest. I never learned what he had won, but he was having a hell of a time retaining the title.

Mercedes stood near the bar. She was thirty, and we already possessed a little history: she had once left me naked in a hotel room and taken my shoes after I called her by her sister’s name. She looked down at the car keys in my hand and smiled before she looked at the damage to my face.

“You finally got a Mercedes you can afford, baby?” Mercedes asked, touching the key ring with one finger.

I invited her to come see it. She reminded me that I owed her flowers.

I counted five hundred out of my new money and put it in her hand. “Buy the whole fucking garden.”

She kissed my cheek, cautiously avoiding the cut, and told me I was bleeding on the good side of my face.

“Both sides good,” I told Mercedes, turning my face so she could check.

She laughed. I left her beside the bar, pleased with myself, a dead man’s gold on my chest and another man’s gift in my fist. I had been wanted by men and women across that city and thought I knew the forms trouble liked to take when it dressed beautifully.

Then I saw Shawna at the window.

The silver case stood beside her ankle. She held a cigarette low enough that its smoke curled past her bare arm. A little scar marked the skin above one eyebrow. A man was talking beside her, but she wasn't following his mouth. Her eyes had found me across the room.

I went to her. My lip hurt. My ribs hurt. The air seemed to have become a thing I needed to move through deliberately.

“T sent me,” I told Shawna.

“I know,” she said. Her eyes had already dropped to the cross on my chest.

I asked how she knew. Her cigarette stopped halfway to her mouth.

“Where'd you get that?” Shawna asked, pointing at the cross.

“T gave it to me,” I said.

“Tonight?” she asked. She hadn't looked back up at my face.

I nodded. She reached out and turned it over without asking.

Three tiny letters had been engraved in the back: D. B. M. I hadn't noticed them. Shawna’s fingers closed around the cross, pulling the chain gently against the back of my neck.

“You know what those mean?” she asked.

I told her no.

She let it fall. “Drive before you find out.”

I lifted the case. She took my free hand and led us away from the window. The man beside her stopped talking. As we crossed the party, I watched the small changes travel ahead of us: a smile lost, a phone raised, a man turning his shoulder to speak into somebody else’s ear. We were leaving a room that had not given permission to lose her.

At the elevator, one of Red’s men stepped across the doors. He asked where the fuck I was taking her. I told him she was coming with me. Did he need a picture?

“I’m asking her,” he said, reaching toward the front of his jacket.

I hit him in the throat before his hand reached the inside of his jacket. He folded into the wall. I shoved him lower and stepped across his shoes as Shawna pressed the elevator button.

Another man came around the corner. Shawna brought a small silver pistol out of her purse and aimed it at the middle of his chest.

“You get one chance,” Shawna told the man coming around the corner.

He took it by standing perfectly still.

The doors shut. She kept the pistol in her hand all the way down. In the quiet box, with the music shrinking above us, I could hear how carefully she was breathing.

“Niggas chasing you out of every party,” I asked, “or I got the special one?”

She watched the floor numbers descend. “Only the ones I survive.”

I wanted to make her laugh. I wanted it badly enough that the desire frightened me before I recognized what it was.

The valet had the Mercedes waiting. I put the case on Shawna’s lap, got behind the wheel, and pulled away from the entrance. I had barely started learning where the controls lived when she looked in her mirror and said my name.

“Don't look,” Shawna told me, holding her eyes on the mirror.

Naturally I looked. I had owned this Mercedes for eleven minutes. I was inspecting everything that approached it.

A black Charger followed with its lights off. Two men sat in front. In the back, a third leaned across the seat with something long in his hands.

The rear window burst.

Shawna folded over the case. I ducked, jerked the wheel, and clipped the edge of a fountain with the right fender. Water rose over the hood and washed across three men in suits standing with their chests out at the entrance. Their posture became considerably less formal. We struck the street still moving. A horn screamed beneath our own engine.

I shouted to ask whether the men were hers.

“You think I'd ride with you if those were my niggas?” Shawna shouted back.

“You met me tonight!” I reminded her, dragging the wheel straight.

“I'm trying to meet you tomorrow!” She pointed at the open road. “Drive!”

A yellow Ferrari slid out of the next street and joined the Charger. It came level on my side. The driver smiled at me while his passenger raised a pistol. That smile bothered me. There should be a limit to how comfortable a man gets while doing this shit.

Shawna had braced one hand against the center of the wheel when the glass broke. I pointed at it.

“Baby, move your hand,” I told her. “The horn. You're leaning on the horn.”

She lifted her wrist. The screaming stopped. In the sudden space it left, I heard the Ferrari’s engine climbing beside mine.

I turned toward it.

The gunman pulled his pistol up against his chest. Beside him, the driver's hands jerked on the wheel. Yellow metal scraped our side, caught the street pole, and spun away across a valet entrance. Sparks flew beneath the car. Men in expensive shoes climbed onto the backs of other men in expensive shoes to get out of its path.

Shawna stared after it. “That’s a Ferrari!”

“That’s one Ferrari,” I told her. “Keep up.”

The Charger still filled the mirror. The man in the back shifted his long gun into another position, and before I could decide how much road I had to work with, a red convertible burst out of a hotel drive between us.

Mercedes stood in its passenger seat, holding her heels together in one hand.

“I told you about my fucking flowers, Aiden!” Mercedes shouted from the red convertible.

Porsche drove. Dark red hair, red dress, eyes fixed on the lane ahead: the car seemed to be following an argument she had already won. We had met at a party where a rich man bought her a sports car and cried when she actually left in it. She kept the car. Later, she showed me the apology he sent. She had kept that too.

Porsche shouted for Mercedes to sit down. Mercedes shouted that I owed her.

“He owes the whole street right now!” Porsche answered, steering across the Charger’s nose.

The black car braked. Shawna turned to me with her mouth open.

“Your friends?” she asked.

“Good people,” I told her.

“Your good people are going to get us killed,” Shawna said.

“Look behind us,” I told her, keeping the Mercedes ahead of the convertible. Porsche was keeping the Charger from passing. Whenever its nose appeared on one side, the convertible moved across it. Mercedes had dropped into the seat, but her arm kept appearing above the door with a shoe in it, contributing what she could to traffic management.

Shawna laughed. One bright sound, short enough that I turned to make sure I'd heard it. I can still hear it more clearly than the gunshots that came before.

Then the Charger hit the convertible’s rear bumper.

The red car swung toward the wall. Mercedes grabbed the windshield frame. One silver heel left her hand, caught the streetlight in a flash, and disappeared into somebody’s outdoor dinner.

I braked hard enough to drag Shawna forward against her belt.

“Not leaving them,” I said before she could finish my name.

The Charger surged toward us. I saw the driver properly now: a white scar over his eyebrow, teeth showing, his head turning between the two cars.

“Choose me, nigga,” I said, looking at him in the mirror.

I put the Mercedes behind the red convertible. The Charger hit us instead.

The impact drove my head into the seat. Metal folded at the rear. Shawna caught my arm, and for an instant the world narrowed to the grip of her fingers and the work of keeping the wheel from being torn out of mine.

Porsche recovered before she reached the wall. She lifted one finger as she pulled clear. I took it as something stronger than a thank-you.

Ahead, the boulevard opened across an intersection. A transport truck moved through it carrying a silver speedboat over the front of its long trailer, with the back portion empty. Beside the intersection, a closed hotel’s delivery ramp climbed out of the street. I saw the rising concrete, the boat crossing beyond it, and the patch of road on the far side.

Shawna looked from my face to the ramp.

“No,” she said, catching the door handle.

“You don't know what I'm thinking,” I told her.

“I know exactly what you're thinking,” Shawna said. “Your hands already voted.”

I brought the car toward the foot of the ramp. “Then hold the case.”

We hit the incline with the engine roaring. The hood lifted. For a moment the tires hammered over the ridges in the concrete, and then that sound stopped completely.

The car left the ramp.

My stomach rose against my ribs. Through the windshield the Strip spread out below us, bright and impossible, while the boat’s mast and flag slid beneath the nose. Shawna screamed my name. I heard terror in it and hoped that somewhere inside the terror there was affection. Whatever else it contained, the volume would have put an ordinary marriage ceremony out of business.

We cleared the boat by less than the height of its flag.

The landing drove my teeth together. A tire burst. The Mercedes lurched, leaned, and found enough of the street to continue. A collision behind us reached me through every missing window. I glanced in the mirror and saw the Charger sideways against the bare rear of the trailer.

Shawna looked back too. In the mirror, a black wheel rolled across the intersection by itself.

Our pursuers had become a collection of separate transportation problems.

I slowed at the next light. The steering pulled against my hands, and my ribs were beginning to remind me that I had started this adventure with twelve men’s worth of damage already installed.

Porsche came alongside. Mercedes still held the surviving shoe. She looked as if she intended to find whoever had invented heels and make him build her a replacement under supervision.

“You jumped a fucking boat!” Porsche shouted.

“Had to get over it somehow,” I called back to Porsche.

She leaned across the seat toward my window. “With a car, Aiden!”

On the sidewalk, a man began clapping. The couple beside him joined in. Then the taxi line took it up, people lowering suitcases to applaud. I could already imagine how badly I was going to hear that story repeated. I raised my hand to accept what the city had finally recognized.

Shawna caught it and returned it to the wheel.

“They can admire you moving,” Shawna told me, keeping my hand on the wheel until I took hold again.

Mercedes pointed her remaining shoe at Shawna. “Keep him. He’s expensive, but he comes with a show.”

The light changed. Porsche kissed the air toward me and turned away. The two women left together, carrying one shoe. I watched them go and knew I owed them more than a replacement heel. I drove toward the desert on a damaged tire and the sort of pride that will keep a young man from hearing the noises his car is making.

Shawna touched my cheek. She told me I could have kept going when the Charger hit the women.

“Wouldn't have been me,” I told Shawna, keeping my eyes on the road ahead.

She watched me for a moment, then rested her hand on my leg. I felt that small weight more powerfully than anything the men outside the club had done to me.

We passed the last casinos before she asked me to pull over.

I said T’s house was farther out. Her hand left my leg.

“I know where his fucking house is,” Shawna told me. “Pull over.”

I turned off behind a shuttered wedding chapel. Half the sign had burned out. Gravel rasped beneath the ruined tire as I took us into the empty lot, out of sight of the main road. For the first time since the Saint, there was no engine chasing ours.

Shawna opened the case on her lap.

Money filled it. Tight bundles, edge to edge. Three million dollars, she told me. I stared at it and started to repeat what T had said.

“T says a lot,” Shawna said, lifting an envelope out from beneath the cash.

She opened the envelope. The photograph inside had a crease across its middle. It showed her standing in front of this same chapel three years earlier, wearing a white dress. A man stood behind her with his arms around her waist.

The cross against his chest was the one against mine.

“Dante,” she said. “His brother. My husband.”

I looked at the chapel, then the photograph, then at her. The whole ride changed shape behind us. Her fingers closing around the cross at the window. The way her eyes had gone somewhere else when I said T had given it to me. I had been carrying her dead husband through the night and talking as if the world began wherever I happened to park.

She put a cigarette between her lips. The lighter struck twice without catching. I took it gently, lit it, and held the flame steady while she leaned forward.

She told me how she had come to Vegas at twenty-three. A suitcase, eight hundred dollars, and a number a girl in Phoenix had given her. She wanted to sing. The number belonged to Valentino.

I knew Valentino's name from the clubs. I had seen his white suits and the diamond in his tooth, watched men laugh around him and women keep their eyes on his hands. What he had done to Shawna was new to me.

Shawna told me he had bought her clothes and found her a room. He had promised introductions to people in music. By the time she understood what he demanded in return, she said, her clothes, her room, and the life she had started building in them belonged to him.

She blew smoke toward the broken side of the windshield and kept the photograph flat against her knee.

“Dante came into the club on my birthday,” Shawna told me. “Everybody else wanted something. He asked what kind of cake I liked.”

One corner of her mouth rose. She looked down at the photograph before continuing.

“He came back with a whole carrot cake,” she said. “Not a slice. A whole fucking cake. Put it between the bottles like we had all night.”

Shawna told me what happened next. Valentino leaned over the cake, laughing at the man who had brought it, and pinched a piece of icing between two fingers. Dante caught his wrist before he could lick them. Valentino pulled. The cake stand tipped; Shawna caught it with both hands. Dante used his free hand to hit Valentino in the mouth.

The diamond tooth landed in the icing.

“Told me not to eat that part,” Shawna said, looking at the photograph. “Like I was going to forget where the tooth went.”

“My nigga,” I said before I remembered whose story I was hearing.

Shawna looked up from the photograph. “That’s what I said. Exactly what I fucking said.”

She wiped hard beneath one eye, then told me they had married here six weeks later. T stood beside his brother. Red paid for the room. Everybody smiled in the pictures.

I asked what happened. She watched the chapel while she answered.

Dante had taken three million out of a deal. He said they were leaving. He said he had a brother, so he didn't have to be afraid.

I lowered my eyes to the cross. The back had turned outward against my shirt.

Shawna turned the photograph over. Written there were the words behind those initials: Damn, boy. Magnificent.

Shawna described Dante looking at himself in the chapel mirror after their wedding, saying those words as if God had just completed the finishing work. She told me she had nearly dropped her flowers laughing. The next morning she had the words put on his cross.

“Nigga was right about you,” I told her.

“He was talking about himself,” Shawna said, looking up at me.

I looked down at the gold. “Then he finally got it on the right neck.”

The laugh escaped her so suddenly she covered her mouth. For a second the woman from the photograph looked out through the woman beside me. I didn't mistake that for a victory. I was grateful to have seen her.

Then she held the picture to her chest and told me they had left Dante in the desert. Valentino brought her his watch. He said Dante had died begging for somebody to take care of her.

I wanted to kill a man I hadn't met. By then Valentino was already dead, but anger doesn't always concern itself with the schedule.

Shawna told me she had waited eight months. She returned to Valentino and let him assume she had nowhere else to go. By the end, she said, he was telling her she loved him.

“Last Tuesday, I shot him in his car,” she told me. “He was talking when I did it. Kept talking after the first one. So I shot him again.”

Then, she said, she took Dante's watch out of his glove compartment.

She held her wrist where I could see it. The watch was too large. It had turned toward the side of her arm, and she pushed it upright with her thumb.

She told me Red had taken her in afterward and promised protection. Tonight, she said, she had taken the three million back.

I asked where T fitted into this. He had promised to get her out of Vegas, she said.

“You believe him?” I asked Shawna.

Shawna slid the photograph inside her dress. “I believed his brother.”

She closed the case. Then she leaned across the space between our seats and kissed me.

I felt her hand against my face and remembered the man in the photograph. I was wearing his cross. I had just repeated his joke. Part of me wondered how much of that kiss belonged to what she remembered and how much belonged to the man sitting beside her. I hoped she had seen something in me when I went back for Mercedes and Porsche. I didn't ask. Her fingers stayed against my cheek. I wanted her so badly I couldn't make myself pull back.

I tasted smoke. I forgot about the fortune on her lap.

When she pulled back, her fingers stayed against my cheek.

“Don't take me to that house,” Shawna said, still holding my face.

“Then this nigga ain’t taking you,” I said. “Give me somewhere else.”

My first night in the family, and I had already found something I was willing to lose it for.

I took her to a motel outside town. It had an outdoor stairway, six rooms on the upper floor, and a blue sign that blinked as if electricity was being rationed by a man with a personal grievance. I paid for all six rooms and told the clerk nobody was to come upstairs.

He checked the money twice before asking whether I really meant all six. I told him yes. He pushed the keys across the counter without attempting further hospitality.

In our room, Shawna set the case on the second bed and put her purse and pistol on the nightstand. She took Dante's photograph from her dress and laid it beside the lamp. I locked the door and set my pistol on the other side of the bed. Trucks passed on the highway beyond the lot. Downstairs, a couple were arguing beside a car, their ordinary fury rising through the window while the rest of our night waited outside it.

Shawna unbuttoned my shirt to look at my ribs. The bruising had darkened. She touched beside it and said my name softly, without a joke in it.

“I'm good,” I told her.

“You're bleeding,” Shawna said, touching the dark edge of a bruise.

“I've been bleeding all night,” I explained. That seemed a useful distinction to me. She went into the bathroom and came back with a wet towel, which was a fairly complete review of my argument.

She cleaned my face. Her hand steadied my jaw. Whenever the cloth caught the split in my lip, she apologized. I kept telling her not to. It had been a long time since somebody's touch made a hurt easier to carry.

When she finished, I lifted the chain over my head and put the cross in her hand.

She looked down at it. Her thumb found the back. Then she began to cry, bending over the little cross until I moved close enough for her to lean against me.

I held her while she cried. When her breath caught, I loosened my arm until she could draw it in. The little cross pressed between our hands. I hadn't known Dante. I kept the things I couldn't know about him to myself and stayed beside her on the bed.

After a while she lifted her head.

“Stay,” Shawna said.

“I'm here, baby,” I told her, keeping an arm around her.

“Tonight,” she said. “I mean tonight.”

“I'm right fucking here,” I promised. She pulled me down to kiss her.

She laid the cross beside the photograph and turned the lamp off herself.

We spent what remained of that little piece of night together, with the money on the other bed and our guns close enough to reach. Afterward, she rested against my chest while the blue light from the motel sign moved over the ceiling. There was nothing elegant about the room. The air conditioner rattled. A spring complained whenever I shifted. I remember it as one of the first places where I wanted morning to find me exactly where I was.

Shawna asked what I wanted. I told her I wanted her.

“You don't know me,” she said, her head still against my chest.

“Then tell me more,” I answered.

So she did. She talked about the songs she had wanted to sing. She said her mother in Phoenix still believed she worked in a restaurant. She told me Dante had wanted a house near the water, though neither of them knew how to swim.

I told her we would buy it. I told her we would go wherever she wanted.

I had known her less than three hours. A wiser man might have offered something smaller. At twenty-four, with her head on my chest, I would have tried to carry a fucking coastline into the room if she had said she liked the view.

My phone lit up on the nightstand.

T’s name filled the screen. I let it ring. A moment later a photograph arrived: the Mercedes parked below us, taken from close enough to show the damage along its side.

Under the picture, he had written: Come down, son.

I put my clothes on and slipped my pistol back into my waistband. Shawna sat up behind me. Before I opened the door, I saw her put the photograph and cross inside her purse.

The empty swimming pool lay beyond a metal fence at the far side of the motel lot. T waited beside it with Tank and two men I didn't know. He wore the same shirt he had worn behind the nightclub. My blood still marked one cuff.

I came down with my hands visible. Behind me, our room door closed. I was aware of that sound in a way I wasn't aware of the trucks or the light buzzing over the stairs.

“Where's the money?” T asked.

“Upstairs,” I told him.

“The girl?”

“Her name's Shawna. She's upstairs too.”

T nodded slowly. He looked me up and down before speaking again.

“One thing,” he said. “I gave you one fucking thing to do.”

I told him she had spoken about Dante.

Tank looked away. That frightened me before T answered. My new brother couldn't look at me while I asked about the dead one.

“Dante stole from his family,” T said.

“He was your brother,” I said to T.

“Then the nigga should've known better,” T answered.

I had brought a little hope downstairs with me. It was embarrassing how little I required of him: a denial, another name, anything I could hold between the man who had kissed my forehead and the man who could let his brother die. He did not even bother giving me a lie.

T pointed toward our room. “She put Dante in the ground. Now you ready to follow.”

“You put him in the ground,” I said.

T stepped close. I smelled the same cologne that had been against my face when he gave me the cross. He asked who had made me. Who had given me the car, the money, the name people would now say across the west side.

“You think she would've looked at you without all that?” T asked, pointing toward the car he had given me.

I didn't answer. It hurt because I had spent the first part of the evening believing the gifts had made me worth looking at.

“She’s upstairs with my three million,” he said. “That’s what she’s in love with.”

Tank shifted behind him. I looked toward the sound; T moved closer, filling my sight again, and gave me one last order: go get her.

Above us, the room door opened.

Shawna stood at the balcony rail holding the case. She had come out barefoot. The blue motel light moved across her face while she looked down at T.

“You want your money, T?” Shawna called from the balcony.

He looked up.

She swung the case over the rail and let go.

It struck the empty pool below us. The latches gave, and cash burst across the cracked blue tiles. Bundles split on the slope toward the drain. A few loose bills fluttered long enough for everybody to watch them land.

“There,” Shawna said. “Now tell him what I love.”

T drew his gun toward the balcony.

I struck his arm as he fired. The shot broke a window above the stairs. Tank caught me from behind, locking one arm across my chest. One of the other men came toward me with his fist up. I drove my forehead into his face and felt a bright red pain spread through my own skull.

Tank’s hold loosened. I twisted, drove him into the pool fence, and heard the wire rattle behind his back.

Shawna fired from above. The man beside T dropped.

T shoved his pistol against my side.

The shot felt like an enormous hand closing inside me. Sound came after it, or seemed to. I couldn't take a breath. My knees wanted to fold, and the front of T’s shirt filled my sight because I had fallen against him without letting go.

“Should’ve listened,” he said.

I caught his gun wrist with both hands. Blood ran beneath my shirt. I could feel it moving, could feel my strength trying to follow it out. But the balcony was above his shoulder, and Shawna was on it, and I would not give him that angle again.

Tank hit me. The blow reached me from somewhere far away.

I drove a knee into T, pulled one hand free, and got the pistol from my waistband. Even then, I wanted him to stop. I wanted the man who had called me son to look down at what he had done and become somebody else before I had to answer it.

He kept holding the gun.

I fired. He struck the fence. I fired again, and he went down with his hand still reaching toward me.

The wire shook after he stopped moving.

Tank stared at him, then at the gun in my hand. He began to say my name.

“Back up.” I could barely get the breath behind it. “Tank, back the fuck up.”

He moved away. I tried to remain standing long enough to understand what I had done.

Shawna ran down the stairs with the pistol in her hand and her purse slung over one shoulder. When she reached me, she put the pistol inside the purse and pressed both palms against the blood spreading over my shirt. I heard her tell me to look at her. I tried to tell her I was good. My mouth made the shape; nothing useful came out.

She turned on Tank. “Get the fucking car!”

He looked at T one more time. Then he ran toward the Mercedes.

That was how the west side became mine. Not with the keys landing in my hand, not with T announcing it over the money. With a man I had wanted for a father dead beside the pool and a woman still wearing another man’s watch trying to hold my life inside my body.

We left the cash where it had fallen. I remember blue tiles, money everywhere, nobody bending to pick it up while Shawna got me into the car. She eased my pistol out of my fingers and put it in the passenger footwell before pulling the towel against my side. There wasn't a dollar in that pool that could buy me another minute standing beside it.

She drove. One hand on the wheel, the other holding a towel against my side whenever the road allowed. The sky had begun to lighten over the desert. Behind us, the casinos kept shining, indifferent as a room full of people who hadn't heard their names called yet.

“Stay with me,” Shawna said.

“I'm here,” I told her, forcing enough air into my chest to make it audible.

“Then keep talking,” Shawna said. “Tell me about that house.”

So I talked about the house I had promised at the motel. I told her there would be a room just for her music. We would learn to swim. She asked where the windows would face, and I told her the sunrise. I was bleeding through a motel towel and selling waterfront property to keep the woman driving from hearing how hard I was working for each breath.

She kept asking about the windows. I kept giving her more of them.

At the edge of town, she pulled over. I thought something had gone wrong with the car until she took out the cross. She put the chain around my neck and settled the gold against my chest.

“Don’t,” I said. “That’s his.”

“I know,” Shawna said.

She closed my fingers around it. Then she kissed me and returned to the wheel. The meaning of that would take us longer than one drive to understand. At the time, I held on because she had asked me to.

The sun rose beside her face. My phone kept ringing. Some numbers I recognized from T's people or Red's. Others meant nothing to me. On the third call, I answered with my name.

There was a silence at the other end. Then a man asked what I wanted them to do.

I looked at Shawna. She watched the road. Dante’s watch had slipped around her wrist again, but she couldn't spare a hand to turn it back.

“Some nigga better find me a doctor,” I said. “And tell everybody the girl is with me.”

I had started the night looking for somewhere to belong. By sunrise, belonging had stopped meaning the men who would let me stand beside them. It meant the woman beside me, the people we had stopped for, and whatever I would have to stand through now that I had chosen them.

Ten toes down. All the way to the end.

## 3. A King with a Hole in Him

At the hospital, a doctor asked Shawna who she was to me.

“His wife,” she said.

I heard that. I had missed pieces of the drive: whole stretches of road, questions I must have answered because she stopped asking them. My mother’s kitchen kept appearing where the dashboard ought to be. Then I would see the empty swimming pool again, and hear T calling me son, and want to tell him he had lost the fucking right.

But wife came through clean.

I tried to live harder when she said it. Whatever blood I had left in me received a promotion.

The doctor asked for a name. Shawna gave her own.

“His name,” the doctor repeated.

“Aiden,” Shawna said. “His name is Aiden.”

They had brought a bed alongside the one from the entrance. Hands reached beneath me to move me across, but Shawna had both fists caught in my shirt. My blood had dried brown over the front of her black dress. One earring was missing. Her bare feet stood on a floor polished enough to show her reflection, in a hospital that smelled of flowers and probably charged extra for letting them die naturally.

The doctor told her to let go.

I managed to say, “Baby.”

Shawna looked down at me. I wanted to tell her something that would stay if I didn't. Instead, I felt the chain against my neck and saw the scissors in somebody’s hand.

“Don't let them cut my chain,” I told Shawna, trying to lift my hand toward it.

She opened her fists. Her fingers shook while she lifted the gold over my head, but she did it herself. She kissed the cross, put it around her own neck, and stepped back just far enough for the waiting hands to take me.

The ceiling moved. Then it went away.

When it returned, a woman in a white coat stood beside the bed studying a chart. I had a tube in my arm, a dry mouth, and the impression that somebody had removed the middle of my body and returned it without consulting the instructions.

“Dr. Marisol Vega,” she said when she saw my eyes follow her. “You’re alive.”

I asked for Shawna. Vega pointed toward the window.

She was curled in a chair with my bloody shirt gathered against her stomach. Somebody had put hospital socks on her feet. The cross lay against her collarbone, and Dante’s watch had slipped down over the narrow part of her wrist. In the morning light, I could see the darkened skin beneath her eyes. At the Saint she had held her back straight even while she stood still. Asleep, she curled around the shirt. I wanted to keep the room quiet around her.

I told Vega not to wake her.

“She’ll wake herself,” the doctor said. “She has been doing it every eleven minutes.”

I tried to sit up. Pain drove through my side so hard the room lost its edges. I fell back against the pillow and said the only word I had ready.

“Fuck,” I said into the pillow.

“Yes,” Vega agreed. “That. You will be doing quite a lot of that if you continue.”

She waited until I stopped gripping the sheet, then explained what I needed to know. They had treated the damage. The bullet was still inside me; taking it out would create risks I didn't need. What I required now was time, which was inconvenient because I had never considered time a serious participant in my plans.

I stared at the ceiling. T was dead and had still found a way to move in with me.

“How much time?” I asked Vega.

“More than you want,” she said, holding one hand beside the bed until I stopped trying to rise.

I told her there were men waiting downstairs and a whole west side I couldn't run horizontally. She said they could keep waiting. She checked the dressing, looked at the monitor, and continued talking to me as if owning territory did not exempt a man from the ordinary problems of having organs.

I couldn't lift my head without making myself dizzy. Vega held the cup while I drank. Beyond her wrist I could see towers, whole floors of bright windows. I might have owned every one of them and still needed her to tilt the fucking straw.

I tried to take the cup from her. My hand slipped. Water hit the sheet; the cup struck the floor.

Shawna came out of the chair before it stopped rolling.

Shawna said my name and reached for me, touching my face, my shoulders, the hair above my ear. Her eyes passed over the bandages without stopping. I held still under her hands.

“Right here,” I told her.

“You scared me,” Shawna said, pressing her forehead briefly against mine.

I apologized. She kissed my forehead, slapped my arm, and told me not to be sorry—to stop doing it. Then she kissed me again. Her tears reached my mouth. I could taste them when I tried to speak.

Vega set a fresh cup within reach and left us alone.

I watched Shawna wipe her face with the back of her hand. The cross moved when she breathed.

“You told them you're my wife,” I said to Shawna.

“They weren't going to let me in,” she told me.

“Could've said sister,” I suggested, watching her wipe her cheek.

She looked at me over her wet knuckles. “I wanted them to let me in. I didn’t want to make it weird when I kissed you.”

I laughed and regretted it physically. The joke was excellent. My side was simply a hater.

“Say it again,” I asked her.

Shawna brushed water off my wrist. “Which part?”

“What you told the doctor. About being my wife.”

She sat on the edge of the bed, careful of the tubes.

“Nigga, you trying to die married?” Shawna asked.

“Trying to live that way,” I told her.

Her hand stopped against my cheek. Her eyes widened, and she didn't answer. I wondered whether the question frightened her. I was asking for a life before we had finished surviving the night that introduced us. I meant it. Meaning it didn't make it a small thing to ask.

She lowered her head onto the pillow beside mine.

“Let me get you home first,” Shawna whispered.

That afternoon, Tank appeared in the doorway with flowers.

The suit was fresh. His shoes had dirt around the soles. A bruise marked his forehead, and he held the bouquet out from his body with the uncertainty of a man who could lift an engine but hadn't been briefed on stems.

“These for her,” he said.

Shawna stood beside my bed. She didn't reach for them.

“Put the flowers down,” I told him, “before we both start feeling strange.”

He set them on the table by the door. I watched his hands come away empty. Those hands had held me while T tried to kill me. They had also gone for the car when Shawna told him to. I wanted to hit him again. I wanted him close. I wanted a man that size between this room and the people looking for us, and I couldn't yet tell whether he was one of them.

I asked whether he had come alone. He said the men downstairs were waiting. T’s men, or what had been T’s men. They wanted to know whose men they were now.

Before he answered that part, he glanced at Shawna.

“I’m not leaving,” she said.

“Didn't ask you to,” Tank told Shawna.

“You looked,” she said, keeping herself between his flowers and my bed.

Tank spread his empty hands. “I got eyes.”

I held out my hand to her. She took it without taking her eyes off him.

T had gone to the funeral people, Tank told me. Of the two other men at the pool, one was alive and one wasn't. Shawna looked down. I felt her fingers stiffen around mine and squeezed them gently. There would be time for what she needed to say about the man she had shot. I wasn't going to decide for her whether that meant relief, guilt, or a silence we sat through together.

She asked about the money.

Tank studied the foot of my bed before answering. “Pool was empty when I got back.”

“Three million doesn't walk,” Shawna told him.

“Plenty people got legs,” Tank answered, still watching the foot of the bed.

She let my hand go.

“Look at me when you say it,” Shawna told Tank.

He did. He told her he had looked, that people moved fast when nobody was watching, that the money wasn't there.

I believed him. I wanted the man who had brought the car to be the man I could keep, and I was lying in a bed without enough blood to investigate every wish I had.

“Leave it,” I said. “She threw it away to save me. It saved me. That’s money spent.”

Tank looked from me to the monitors.

“You always this rich?” Tank asked me.

“I woke up,” I told him.

He stayed quiet for a moment. Then he nodded. “Yeah. You did.”

Tank told me what was happening outside. Suppliers were waiting with questions. Men who owed the club money had started asking whether their debts died with T. Red wasn't answering calls from the west side, and T's office remained locked. I could imagine what that looked like to men I knew: respect for the dead until somebody found a way to make it pay.

T had announced my place publicly. Keeping it was another matter.

“You keeping me there?” I asked Tank.

He worked his jaw and reminded me that he had driven the car.

“That ain't what I asked,” I said to Tank. “I'm asking about now.”

Shawna moved to the window. Sunlight caught Dante’s watch. Tank looked at it longer than he needed to, then looked back at me.

“I ain't putting another man in the desert,” Tank told me.

He went downstairs to bring the others up. I watched the doorway after he had gone. It wasn't the promise I had asked for, but it was the one he had managed to give me.

Vega returned to find nine men in my room, two in the hallway, and a duffel bag occupying the visitor’s chair. Shawna had stepped into the adjoining bathroom. I could hear the tap running. The men stood around my bed. Holsters showed when their jackets opened, and their faces stayed blank in a way I had learned to be careful around.

“Absolutely not,” Vega said.

The older man holding a stack of papers stopped halfway through a sentence. Nobody else moved.

Vega pointed at the door. “Two visitors. The rest leave.”

They looked at me. I could feel the little test taking shape: whose authority counted when the woman in the white coat contradicted the men in expensive shoes?

“She said move,” I told them. “You want the woman who kept me alive having to step over you?”

They moved so fast the last two reached the doorway together. Each stopped to let the other through, then stepped forward again. Vega pointed once more. They abandoned manners and fitted themselves through sideways.

Tank stayed, along with the older man from T’s club. The papers came onto the bed beside my uninjured hip. Money would arrive Friday, he explained, but people needed paying today.

I asked what was in the bag.

“Yesterday,” the older man said, nodding toward the duffel.

Tank opened it. Cash reached the zipper. Less than a day before, I had been counting what I could spend on food. Now men were describing a fortune by the day of the fucking week.

I told the older man to pay the people who had worked. He wanted to know whether I meant from this money.

“Unless you got some magic beans,” I told the older man. “They worked. Pay them.”

He made a mark on the paper. His pen sounded absurdly small in the room.

Then he said Red wanted a meeting. Face to face. I told him Red could call, because if he looked at my face he might notice I was busy trying to keep it attached to the rest of my life.

The man swallowed. Tank closed the duffel.

For a moment they both waited. I had seen T sit silent until the men around him stopped talking and began looking at their hands. I didn't know how to be him. I was twenty-four, leaking under a dressing, with Shawna’s lip gloss on my forehead. All I knew was that the chair T had given me came with people attached to it, and I wasn't giving him the last word about how they were treated.

“Nobody touches Shawna,” I said. “Nobody says her name to Red. Anybody got a problem can leave the keys on the bed.”

The older man looked at Tank. Tank looked at me.

“You heard him,” Tank told the older man.

The keys stayed in the older man’s pocket.

After he left, I sank back against the pillows. The conversation had emptied me. I could feel dampness beneath the dressing and couldn't hide the effort it took to draw a full breath.

Tank moved the bag closer and offered to count it.

“I want you watching that door like your brother in here,” I told Tank.

“Who am I watching for?” he asked, his hand on the duffel.

“Every fucking nigga,” I answered.

Before carrying the chair into the hall, Tank set a bundle of clean clothes on the low table beside my bed and laid my pistol across them.

“Floor of your car,” he told me. “Nearly stepped on it.”

“Car's got features,” I said.

He looked at me for a moment, then took the chair outside. That was the first night he guarded me.

Before it got dark, I heard a woman beyond the door. Then I saw the red velvet coat, the bags, and a face I would come to associate with some of Shawna's oldest stories.

“Where the fuck is she?” a woman shouted outside my door. A moment later I would learn her name was Rosa.

Shawna said her name and crossed the room. Rosa dropped what she was carrying and caught her. They held each other in the narrow space between my bed and the window while Tank quietly brought the abandoned bags inside.

Rosa had copper hair and a broken-heart tattoo beneath her ear. I came to know the rest through the two women in that room: she was thirty-four, sang at the Saint on weekends, and took paying men upstairs when the singing didn't cover what life demanded. She had known Shawna when the suitcase from Phoenix still had its tags. Watching them hold each other, I was seeing a piece of Shawna's life I had only begun to hear about.

“Why didn't you answer your fucking phone?” Rosa demanded into Shawna's hair.

“Broke it,” Shawna said.
