“Borrow one. Send a pigeon. Don't let some nigga in a rhinestone hat be the one telling me you're dead.” Rosa tightened her arms around her.

“I’m here,” Shawna said.

“I see you,” Rosa told Shawna. “I'm mad anyway.”

Rosa held her at arm’s length and checked her face. Only then did she turn toward me. She asked whether I was the one who had shot T.

I said yes.

“Good,” Rosa said.

She came to the bed and kissed my forehead, putting another mark over the one Shawna had left.

“Stay alive,” Rosa told me. “She doesn't have another black dress in her.”

Shawna’s face crumpled. Rosa turned back and brought her close before I could see the tears properly.

The duffel sat beside me, full of answers to questions nobody in that room was asking. I pushed it farther down the bed so Rosa could sit beside Shawna.

Rosa unpacked clothes, food, and an old songbook. She told us she had kept it after Dante died. Shawna opened it in the middle, looked down at the page, and shut it fast enough for the paper to snap.

“Later,” Shawna said, keeping her hand on the cover.

Rosa put it on the table where she could reach it. “Whenever.”

That evening Rosa painted Shawna’s nails by the window. Tank ate in the hallway, where I could hear him negotiating with a plastic fork. More money came from downstairs, and I told them to leave it there. My room held two women talking softly, the smell of real food, and a man outside who kept shifting and making the chair creak but hadn't left it. I wanted to keep that arrangement for a while.

Later, Shawna fell asleep against me. Her hand rested near the bandage without touching it.

She whispered Dante’s name.

I heard it. Of course I heard it. I had been listening to every breath she took.

I lay still until morning. I didn't know yet how to make room for a man who was gone without feeling him take space from me. The only decent thing I knew to do was let her sleep.

On the third afternoon, somebody delivered a coffin.

The nurse called from the private entrance. She sounded offended, which I respected. She worked in an expensive hospital, and someone had rolled a direct criticism of the establishment through the doors.

She said there was an item for me. I asked whether it was flowers. Larger than flowers, she said. I tried piano next.

“It is a coffin, Mr. Aiden,” the nurse said over the phone.

Across the room, Tank stood so fast his food hit the floor.

I told the nurse not to open it.

“I wasn't planning to try it on,” the nurse told me.

Shawna took the phone from me and began telling her to move everyone away. I reached for my trousers. Tank told me no. I told him to get my shoes. He pointed out that I already had hospital socks.

“Then get shoes worthy of the fucking socks,” I told Tank.

Mercedes and Porsche were visiting. They had brought fruit, champagne nobody was allowed to open, and an enlarged photograph of the Mercedes in the air above the boat. Somebody on the sidewalk had caught the jump. You could see Shawna screaming and me concentrating with tremendous visual appeal.

Mercedes went to the window. I pulled myself upright and joined her, one hand against the wall. Through the glass entrance below us, I could see two men beside a black lacquer coffin on a trolley. Silver handles gleamed along the sides. My name crossed the lid in letters big enough to make dying seem like a sponsorship opportunity.

“Even your enemies know you’re expensive,” she said.

Porsche stood beside her counting the men near two white sedans across the private drive.

“Four more by the cars,” Porsche said. “Those two aren't delivering shit.”

I looked beyond the coffin toward the white sedans. The men outside kept watching the entrance instead of the street. One adjusted his jacket without taking his eyes off it. The men by the coffin reached beneath theirs.

Tank dragged me back as the first shot broke glass below us.

The hospital changed in a breath. Alarms started. Somebody screamed beneath the balcony. A man ordered people onto the floor. In the hall outside our room, the brisk sounds of ordinary work became running feet.

I took my pistol from the clothes piled on the low table. I told Shawna to take Rosa into the bathroom and lock the door.

She started to argue. I caught her eyes.

“Be alive when I come back so you can cuss me out,” I told Shawna.

She pressed her lips together, caught Rosa's hand, and went into the bathroom. Mercedes followed them with a heavy vase in both hands, taking a piece of the hospital’s expensive taste into the defensive plan.

Porsche stayed by the door and pointed at the wheelchair.

“The man can't walk fast,” Porsche told Tank. “He can roll.”

I had spent most of the argument defending my feet. Porsche had found me wheels. I appreciated that.

Tank helped me into the chair and pulled my shoes on over the socks. Porsche pushed me into the hallway while he checked the corner ahead. A nurse was struggling with a bed farther along. The patient had one leg in a cast and was trying to hold the back of his gown closed while she steered. The bed kept catching the wall because every time he reached for the rail, his ass became a public matter.

I told Porsche to help them. She wanted to know who would push me.

“I got hands,” I told Porsche, setting them on the wheel rims.

She caught the foot of the bed before it struck the corner. I laid the pistol across my lap, gripped the wheel rims, and followed Tank. Each turn pulled at my side. The chair traveled about as fast as a man pretending the pain wasn't deciding the route. Whenever Tank stopped, I took the gun up again.

A gunman came through the stairwell door at the far end. He stopped facing Tank. Then his eyes dropped to my chair, and he smiled.

“That's the king?” the gunman asked, pointing at me.

“That’s the nigga,” Tank said.

The man lifted his gun. Tank threw himself against the wall, leaving a clear line between me and the weapon. I leaned past the arm of the chair and fired. The gunman fell backward through the stairwell door, and his weapon skidded across the polished floor. More feet sounded on the stairs behind him.

Porsche and the nurse got the bed around the corner. The old man had both hands clamped over his gown. I told him not to look down.

“Wasn't planning to, son,” the old man told me, gripping his gown.

“Good,” I answered. “Your nigga's got this.”

A shot struck the sprinkler above us. Water burst across the ceiling and fell over the corridor. Tank seized the back of my chair and ran.

The wheels rattled over seams. Water hit my face. I held my wounded side with one hand and kept the pistol up with the other. I twisted in the chair and saw two men come around the corner behind us. The man I had shot carried one arm low; the other held his pistol out in both hands. Their shoes slid as they tried to chase across the wet floor.

I shouted Tank’s name. He said he saw them.

“Then quit sightseeing and push!” I shouted up at Tank.

We struck the swinging doors to the atrium and went through onto an upper gallery overlooking the entrance. The nurse and Porsche brought the bed after us. To our left, stairs curved down beside the wall; a service lift stood in a recessed opening beyond them. Both routes emerged near the main floor’s far exit. Below, the gunmen at the coffin controlled that stretch of open floor.

Below us, one delivery man shoved the coffin trolley across the lobby while the other kept his gun raised toward our gallery. The front wheels hit the fountain's low stone rim. The cart tipped. Black lacquer struck the water with a slap, pulling the bent trolley in beneath it. The first man crouched behind the coffin. The other stepped into the shallow water, tracking us with his gun.

I fired before he did. His raised arm dropped, and he fell against the fountain's rim. His gun hit the water beyond his reach. The man behind the coffin shot back. Tank pulled my chair away from the edge as the shots struck the tiles and cracked a section of the rail. The chair tipped. I caught the arm, went over with it, and landed on one knee. My lungs emptied so completely I couldn't make a sound about what my side was doing.

Tank fired back through the gallery doors, forcing the men pursuing us into the corridor. Porsche stood in front of the nurse and her patient with a chair raised across her chest. The lift was only a few yards away, but I could see the gunman below watching its doors. He could catch them when they reached the ground floor. Staying here meant waiting for the men behind us to come through again.

I looked down at the coffin with my name on it.

“Who sent it?” I shouted.

The man using it for cover looked up. He actually answered.

“Who you think?” the gunman below called back.

“I think your taste is fucking awful,” I told him.

I kicked the overturned wheelchair toward the broken section of rail. It scraped across the wet tile, tipped into space, and fell into the fountain. The frame hit the coffin and shoved it sideways through the water. Its trolley buckled. The gunman sprang out from behind it, and Tank fired from above, driving him behind a marble column.

That opened the route to the lift. It didn't keep it open. The man could come back around the column before the bed reached the floor, and the gunmen in the corridor were still trying to get through behind us.

I got my feet under me and stood. The pain stood too. That motherfucker was committed.

Tank followed my gaze over the broken rail.

“Don't you dare,” he warned me.

“Get them down,” I told him. “Catch up.”

I went over.

The coffin lid took my feet in the middle of my own engraved name. Black wood split. Water burst over the sides. My legs bent farther than I wanted, and a white shock ran up through the wound. I stayed upright by putting a hand against the broken lid and refusing to consider what the other option would cost.

People lay behind furniture all across the lobby. They stared at me standing in a coffin in a fountain with a gun. I had come to this hospital for rest. The reviews were going to be complicated.

The man behind the column charged.

He didn't fire. I never learned whether his gun was empty or he had simply decided the wounded man ought to accept delivery. I tore a loose length of the lid upward and swung it into his shoulder.

The wood cracked. He fell against me. We went into the fountain together, his hands reaching for my throat while the water closed over my mouth. My side opened into a kind of pain I couldn't separate from the struggle. I drove him away with my forearm, came up coughing, and brought the pistol close enough to his face for him to stop.

I asked whether he was finished. He nodded, lying half against the broken coffin.

“Use your words,” I told the man in the fountain.

“Finished,” he said, keeping his hands where I could see them.

“Then stay fucking finished,” I told him.

Tank came down the stairs while Porsche and the nurse used the service lift with the bed. He reached the fountain first, kept his gun on the man, and gave me an arm thick enough to trust my full weight to. I had no strength left for making the climb look good.

I put my pistol back in my waistband while Tank held me upright.

The lift opened across the lobby. Porsche checked the way before the nurse pushed the bed toward the exit. The old man lifted a fist at me as they passed.

“That's my boy!” the old man shouted from the bed.

I had never seen him before that morning. I heard pride in his shout and took it anyway. I was collecting fathers faster than the bad ones could die.

Through the broken entrance I saw one of the men from the upstairs corridor burst out of a side door and run to the second white sedan. Another man already had the first sedan running. Both tried to leave. The lead car reached the gate just as a police cruiser struck it broadside. The second stopped behind it with nowhere to go. A woman came out of the cruiser with a long gun. Her dark glasses stayed exactly where they belonged while she shouted for the men to show their hands.

Every hand in those cars appeared with remarkable enthusiasm.

Chief Gloria Knox came through the broken entrance a couple of minutes later, with silver at her temples and a blue uniform so crisp it looked personally disappointed in every wet surface around her. She checked the fountain, the coffin, the broken gallery rail, and finally me.

She asked whether I had jumped from there.

“Nigga was in my way,” I told Knox.

“And that solved it?” she asked, looking from me to the broken coffin.

The man in the fountain groaned behind Tank.

Knox looked at him. “Apparently.”

I heard an officer call that the corridor was clear. Then Vega arrived with Shawna, Rosa, and Mercedes. My doctor saw my wet trousers and the blood spreading beneath my shirt. She said my name in a tone that made the bullet feel almost conversational.

I told her everybody around us was alive.

“You were in bed,” Vega said to me.

“Didn't have wheels,” I explained, leaning harder on Tank.

Porsche raised a hand. “I tried a chair.”

Vega shut her eyes. When she opened them again, she looked past me at the nurse beside the bed. The nurse was shaking now that the movement had stopped.

“You saved my nurse,” Vega said.

“She saved my ass first,” I told Vega, nodding toward the nurse.

The nurse began crying. The old man demanded to shake my hand. I offered the one that wasn't braced against Tank and tried not to let him see how little grip remained in it.

Knox put a card where I could take it.

“When you're tired of people sending coffins, come see me,” Knox said, holding out the card. “I'd rather give you a badge.”

Tank stared at her. “This nigga?”

“Especially this nigga,” Knox told Tank.

Before I could decide what to say to that, Shawna got her arms around me. She laughed, called me an impossible motherfucker, and kissed my face in front of the chief of police. Her hands moved carefully around the dressing, though she kept telling me what she ought to do to my ass. I stood with fountain water running out of my clothes while the people around us watched. I felt as if I had arrived from somewhere higher than the gallery.

They put me in another chair. Vega supervised that part personally.

That evening, I watched from my window as the broken coffin left on the back of a truck. Two of the captured attackers sat beside it in handcuffs. The man from the fountain kept looking up at my window. I raised my hand in a small wave. He looked away before I had finished it.

Then I let Vega put me back in bed. I had run out of arguments and, more seriously, out of the ability to remain upright while making them.

The days that followed did not resemble a victory montage. I slept. I woke sore. I learned that crossing the room and sitting down again could constitute a full morning’s work. By the time five days had passed in the hospital, Vega allowed me to leave. She made it clear that leaving meant she trusted my people to keep me reasonably still. It did not mean my body had forgiven me.

“No heroics,” she said at the door.

Tank waited behind my wheelchair with two duffel bags and Rosa’s red coat. Before we left the room, Shawna lifted Dante’s chain off her own neck and settled it around mine again, easing the cross flat against the clean shirt. Then she promised Vega I would behave.

Vega turned to her. “You either.”

Shawna nodded. Vega held her eyes until she nodded again. I watched their agreement take shape and felt several of my preferred methods leave the building without me.

Downstairs, six black cars waited under the entrance canopy. Men straightened beside them when Tank rolled me out. One opened the Mercedes door and welcomed me back.

Back. As if I had possessed a kingdom before I had a woman. As if these men hadn't learned my name while I bled into somebody else’s sheets. The word carried expectations I wasn't strong enough to lift, so I concentrated on the smaller task directly in front of me.

I stood up.

I wanted Shawna to see me do it. When my knees trembled, she took my arm and held it firmly against her side. I hoped the men by the cars would mistake the help for simple affection.

“Where we going?” Shawna asked, keeping hold of my arm.

Beyond the canopy, the city moved in bright afternoon traffic. We had people, cars, money, enemies, and a bullet that would apparently be joining all future plans. The one ordinary thing we did not possess was somewhere that belonged to both of us.

I looked down at her.

“Home, baby,” I told her. “We just gotta get one.”

## 4. The Palace of the Woman I Just Met

I bought Shawna a house before I bought myself another shirt.

We went to Lake Las Vegas after the hospital. I had managed to stand for the men by the cars, sit through the drive, and convince myself that looking at property counted as resting because the property generally stayed where you put it. Then I got out beside the lawn and discovered my side had objections to real estate as well.

The agent came toward us in heels. One sank into the grass and stayed there when she took her next step. She caught herself against Tank’s forearm. He steadied her, pulled the shoe out of the lawn, and handed it back with a plug of earth still attached.

She looked at the size of his hand, the line of black cars, and the dressing visible where my borrowed shirt fell open.

The agent cleared her throat. “Will anybody else be joining the viewing?”

“You want MORE of us?” Tank asked.

I told her we were fine. “Show my woman the water.”

She stepped onto the stone path before describing the house. Five bedrooms. Private dock. Heated pool. The piano could be negotiated separately.

“Why?” I asked. “The nigga in a band?”

The agent explained that the owner might wish to retain it.

I waved a hand at her property folder. “Tell him to wish for a smaller instrument.”

Shawna squeezed my hand. “Baby. Let her do her job.”

I let the woman get the key into the lock without offering further terms.

The foyer opened beneath a curved staircase. Light fell through a chandelier large enough to make me wonder whether the ceiling had applied for financing. White curtains moved at the far end of the living room, where glass doors stood open to the patio. Beyond the pool and the grass, the lake lay bright and almost still.

The black piano sat beside those doors.

Shawna let go of my hand and walked toward it slowly, looking between the water and the keys. I stayed by the last step down into the room and watched her find the bench. She placed two fingers on the ivory without pressing. Her mouth opened, then closed. I remembered touching the hood of a clean car when I was little, then taking my hand away before the owner came out. I wanted her to play the fucking thing.

“Aiden,” she said.

I went to her. “You can't buy a house because I touched a piano,” Shawna said.

“Touch a smaller thing next time.”

She looked up at me. I could see her trying not to laugh.

I touched the end of the bench. “You like it?”

Shawna looked through the doors again. “Yeah. Look at this light, baby.”

I turned toward the agent. “We have a yes. Do whatever paper follows yes.”

The agent opened the folder she carried. “We haven't discussed the price.”

I held up a hand. “Don't say it louder than she said yes.”

Tank wandered outside to inspect the dock while the woman went to fetch the details. I lowered myself onto the piano bench beside Shawna and got halfway through pretending it was easy before she caught my arm.

Shawna guided my elbow down. “Slowly, you ridiculous nigga.”

I settled onto the bench with my teeth together. “That is slowly. Normal speed, we’d own next door already.”

She laughed, and I concentrated on breathing through my nose until the pulling beneath the bandage eased. The room carried sounds differently from the motel. A footstep had space to travel. Water touched the dock in small, regular strokes. There was no argument below the window, no engine idling for somebody who might be coming upstairs.

Shawna looked from my bandage to the room around us. “We doing everything backward.”

I patted the space beside me. “Then turn around.”

She swung one leg across the bench to face me. “We barely know each other.”

I nodded toward the open doors. “I know you wanted water.”

Shawna took her fingers off the piano. “Dante wanted water.”

My smile stayed in place a moment after it should have left. Outside, Tank put one shoe onto the dock and tested his weight. A duck stood on the far end watching him. Neither seemed prepared to recognize the other’s claim.

“You said it to me,” I told her.

She put a hand on my knee. “I know.”

I wanted to stand up and buy a different lake. One he hadn't thought of. Instead I sat there with her hand on my knee while Tank sized up a duck. After a while, Shawna pressed one of the keys. The note hung in the empty room, and I stayed to hear it finish.

The agent returned with the price. I sent her to the men handling T’s accounts. The club and its income had survived him. His house was promised elsewhere; this one would carry Shawna’s name. I wanted her to have a door that was hers to close, a place nobody could take back because she stopped pleasing him.

The agent asked where my name would go.

“My nigga lives where I live,” Shawna said before I could answer.

I turned toward her. Shawna met my eyes. “That the arrangement?”

I laid my hand over hers. “That’s the whole constitution.”

Shawna faced the agent again. “Then stop negotiating over my piano.”

I told the agent to put the keys in Shawna's hand. “Piano stays too. My wife said.”

I caught Shawna's raised eyebrow and tried again. “My future wife. Look, lady, leave the fucking piano.”

She leaned across the bench and kissed me hard enough that I needed to catch the edge with my good hand. Outside, Tank committed his full weight to the dock. The duck stepped off into the water and swam away, surrendering the property without meeting his asking price.

By that evening, the house had begun filling with the objects and people that would make it ours. Rosa arrived in the red velvet coat, stopped under the chandelier, and kept her sunglasses on while she examined the height of the room.

Rosa lowered her sunglasses just far enough to look at us. “This bitch gets shot at one time and comes home with a staircase.”

Shawna screamed laughing. I had heard her laugh in the car. This time she bent forward, braced a hand against the wall, and let the sound fill both floors. I wanted the agent back so I could tell her that was what I'd paid for.

Shawna took Rosa's hand and tugged her toward the first step. “Come see upstairs.”

Rosa looked up along the curve of the banister. “You got upstairs too?”

Tank, standing near the first step, gestured at the evidence. “You looking at the stairs.”

Rosa pointed at him without breaking stride. “And furniture that talks.”

He looked at me for support. I gave him none. I was sitting down at the time and had become very selective about wars.

Within an hour Rosa came down to tell us she'd chosen her room and found a bathroom light that respected her cheekbones. She carried a painting she said was depressing everybody. She referred to the previous owner as if he had died in it. I told her he had moved to Arizona. She leaned the painting against the wall facing inward anyway. Apparently the location of his remains was not central to her criticism.

Tank sat across from me in the living room. I held an ice pack wrapped in a towel against my side and listened to the two women moving above us. A drawer opened, laughter traveled down the stairwell, and Rosa shouted a question about towels nobody had found yet.

“You sure about this?” Tank asked.

“If that chandelier comes with a monthly payment, no.”

He looked toward the stairs. “Her, Aiden.”

“Watch it,” I said.

Tank kept his voice low. “I been watching.”

He kept his eyes on the stairs. I sat through several seconds of silence, deciding whether I wanted him to finish. I told him to say it.

Tank leaned forward, elbows on his knees. “She loved Dante.”

I pressed the ice pack harder against my side. “He’s dead.”

Tank looked back at me. “That’s what I’m telling you.”

I threw the ice pack at him. He caught it without rising, which denied me even the small pleasure of inconveniencing his ass.

I pointed at the dressing below my shirt. “I got a bullet in me. You want me jumping up to fight you?”

Tank shook his head. “I want you sitting right there.”

I gripped the arm of my chair. “Then stop saying shit I can't sit down for.”

He came over and put the ice pack back against my side. Gently. The care made me angrier. It was harder to dismiss him while he was helping me breathe.

Tank adjusted the towel beneath the ice. “Some nigga gotta tell you something.”

Shawna laughed upstairs. We both looked toward the sound.

I asked him where T’s funeral would be. It was the first change of subject I could find large enough to hide inside.

The housewarming began before we had answered the towel question.

Mercedes arrived with twelve women, two men, and a box of champagne whose origins she described as a gift with enough emphasis to discourage witnesses. Porsche followed in a white Bentley with the top down. A man sat in the back wiping his eyes.

Tank leaned toward her window and asked who he was.

“His car,” Porsche said. “My evening.”

The man lifted a hand. “We’re friends.”

I told him to blink if she had bought him too. He laughed so hard he stopped crying and got out to shake my hand.

He introduced himself as Leon, owner of a steakhouse. While he shook my hand, Porsche told me he'd proposed over appetizers.

“I said you could drive,” Leon reminded her, his hand still in mine.

Porsche tapped the Bentley's hood. “And here we are. Keep up, baby.”

She told me she'd declined the ring. Looking at Leon's damp eyes, I thought he might still be hoping the evening came with a second answer.

Mercedes took a champagne bottle from his free hand before he could set it down.

“That’s growth,” she told him.

Twenty minutes later, the patio had people in every available space. Women went into the pool wearing diamonds that nobody had tested for recreational use. A dealer tried to impress Rosa with his watch. She held his wrist beside her ear, listened, and told him she couldn't hear the personality inside it.

Tank laughed from the door. The dealer turned toward him with a sharp little movement of his chin. Then his gaze traveled upward over all that Tank and stopped at his face.

“Funny lady,” the dealer said, and joined in. He laughed so enthusiastically I thought Rosa might have to charge him for it.

I rested in a chair with Shawna beside me and a clean shirt open over my bandages. Gifts accumulated around us: flowers, liquor, a horse statue nobody claimed, and enough steaks in a refrigerated truck to suggest somebody believed ownership of a yard was a public catering commitment.

Shawna leaned close and gestured across the patio, taking in the house, the gifts and the lights coming on along the water. “All this yours?”

The lake lay beyond the dock. Lights on the other shore had begun appearing between the houses. I looked at what she was seeing and felt the dangerous pleasure of being able to give an answer large enough for it.

I rested my hand over hers. “Whatever you can point at.”

She pointed at the horse. “Who the fuck brought that?”

I promised to investigate.

Before I could, an engine rose over the music. A red speedboat came around the point and headed directly toward our dock. Its driver stood behind the wheel with his white shirt open to the waist. Two men sat behind him with rifles across their knees.

Beside me, Tank stopped eating. His plate went onto the nearest table as he turned toward the boat.

Tank spread his arms toward the party. “Everybody inside.”

Half the party kept talking. Then the boat hit the dock. Planks jumped against their fixings, and the people nearest the water turned toward the sound.

The man at the wheel cupped a hand beside his mouth. “Where the new owner at?”

I raised my glass. “Home.”

The driver pointed up at the house. “You bought this from my cousin.”

I looked from him to the house, giving the matter all the examination it deserved. “Your cousin looked old enough to sell it.”

The man gripped the wheel. “Brock had no right. That's my fucking house.”

Shawna stood beside my chair. “Your name on anything?”

The driver turned his head away from Shawna. “I’m talking to the man.”

I sat forward. “Then look at me while you regret that.”

His eyes came to mine. One of the riflemen shifted his weapon across his lap. Between songs, the music stopped and the silence spread over the patio. Women in the pool held the edge, jeweled hands gripping stone. One woman in the pool began moving hand over hand toward the steps. Another followed, keeping below the edge. I looked at the kitchen door and hated how much open patio they had to cross.

The visitor thumped a hand against his chest. “Roman Pike. Ask around.”

I pointed along the shore. “Go around. Dock's full.”

Roman put one foot on the planks. “I have a claim.”

I pointed at the splintered timber below him. “So does my insurance company. You just hit the fucking dock.”

He climbed out onto the damaged planks. The riflemen rose behind him and stepped onto the dock while he approached the patio. He came with his chin up and his shirt blowing open, as though he'd come over to inspect a purchase I hadn't been informed about. The gold chain against his chest bounced with every step. I could see the little marks his bare feet left on my dock.

Roman stopped at the end of the dock and lifted both empty hands. “Twenty thousand a month. Brock paid it. You pay it. We won't have no trouble.”

I looked past those empty hands at the two rifles still pointed our way. His idea of no trouble had brought its own witnesses.

I turned to Shawna. “He sold his house and left a subscription.”

She laughed into her hand. Roman watched her do it. His face tightened, and he drew the pistol from beneath his open shirt.

Tank moved first. Roman’s men lifted their rifles. Shots broke the quiet and the glass doors behind my chair exploded inward.

I pulled Shawna down with me. We struck the patio hard. The pain washed my sight white, but I kept my body over hers until the next shots went past. Glass hit the stones around us. Somewhere close, a woman was crying. I heard her say she'd only come for a drink, and I wanted to get every person on that patio inside before there was something worse to cry about.

Mercedes hauled two women out of the pool and drove them toward the kitchen door. Porsche caught Leon by his collar and dragged him behind his Bentley at the end of the patio drive.

Leon tried to look over the Bentley's hood. “My fucking car!”

Porsche put a hand on his head and shoved him down. “Your head first!”

I'd seen Rosa take my pistol off the table as Roman drew his. Now she came across the patio on her side, keeping low, with the pistol flat against the stone ahead of her.

Rosa pushed the grip against my fingertips. “Housewarming gift.”

I closed my hand around it. “You shouldn't have.”

Rosa flattened herself behind a planter. “I didn't. It’s yours.”

Roman ran back along the dock. His riflemen covered him while he got behind the wheel. Tank fired from the stone barbecue, striking planks around their feet and making them draw back toward the boat. A round broke a champagne bottle above him. He flinched as it poured over the shoulder of his suit.

Tank wiped champagne off his cheek with his shoulder. “Now I’m fucking mad!”

I looked toward the Bentley. It stood at the end of the short patio drive. Fifty feet of lawn separated it from the dock, broken by two chairs and a low stone border around a raised bed. Beyond that border, the damaged end of the dock sat almost level with the boat’s rear deck.

The boat’s engine screamed. Roman was turning away from the shore, bringing the hull broadside for a moment before it could leave.

Shawna followed my gaze from the car to the water, then caught my sleeve. “Again?”

I turned my pistol's muzzle toward the ground before getting up. “Different boat.”

Shawna tightened her hold. “Aiden.”

I looked toward the kitchen. “Get inside, baby.”

She caught my face and kissed me, hard and brief. Then Shawna looked me right in the eyes. “Then come back in my house.”

She moved toward the kitchen while I ran for the Bentley, bent over the wound. Porsche saw me coming and opened the driver's door. Leon held out both hands as if the coming damage were a physical object he might persuade us to hand back.

Leon put a palm against the open car door. “It’s new!”

I ducked under his arm. “So’s my house!”

I dropped into the seat and tucked the pistol between my thigh and the console. Porsche climbed in beside me. “I know this car.”

I grabbed the wheel. “Then introduce us fast.”

Porsche pointed out the selector, then pulled her seat belt across her chest. I fastened mine, put the car in drive, and pressed the accelerator. The rear tires tore out of the drive and onto the lawn, throwing clods behind us. The chairs went beneath the bumper. At the dock, Roman looked over his shoulder. His mouth fell open. He pulled hard at the boat's wheel while I came across the lawn in Leon's proposal.

The front wheels struck the raised border. The Bentley pitched upward, cleared the broken end of the dock, and came down across the boat’s rear deck.

Fiberglass burst beneath us. The hood folded. The speedboat lurched beneath the weight, its stern driven deep and its bow kicking upward. Roman struck the wheel, lost his hold, and went over the side. Spray covered the windshield. Porsche ducked behind the dash while the seat belt drove into my chest and every injured part of me demanded a separate explanation.

For a moment, we stopped moving.

The spray cleared. I was sitting in a car on a boat outside a house I had only just bought. Through the cracked windshield, I could see people clustered at the kitchen door. Several were screaming my name. One man held a steak over his head. I didn't know what kind of help he thought the steak would be, but I appreciated his willingness to commit it.

Porsche lifted her head. “You owe Leon a car.”

I pointed through the windshield at Roman's head coming out of the water. “Nigga owes me a dock.”

Roman coughed beside the boat. Both his hands were open as he tried to keep his head above water; I couldn't see the pistol. One rifleman had jumped into the shallows and stood with his hands up. Tank held his gun on the other at the end of the broken dock.

I leaned out of the Bentley and called Roman’s name. He pushed wet hair out of his eyes.

I braced my elbow on the window frame. “House still yours?”

Roman shook his head, coughing.

I leaned a little farther out. “Speak. I bought acoustics.”

Roman spat water and shouted up at me. “Your house!”

I tapped the Bentley's door. “Whose car?”

Roman stared at me. His mouth opened, but all that came out was another cough.

“Wrong answer.” I looked past him toward the patio. “Leon! Come get your speedboat!”

Leon stood with both hands on his head. Behind him, Mercedes climbed onto a chair she had found still usable and raised two bottles. Rosa held Shawna around the shoulders. Tank started laughing while keeping his gun on the prisoner, then told the man to stop fucking looking at him, as if the captive had caused the problem by witnessing joy.

Shawna came to the edge of the broken dock and held out her hands. I unfastened my belt, retrieved the pistol from beside my thigh, and secured it inside my waistband. Then I climbed out slowly. There was nothing impressive about that part. My legs shook when they took my weight, and the car shifted beneath me as the boat settled lower.

She caught both my hands and drew me across the last gap.

Shawna held on while my feet found solid dock. “You are the most ridiculous man I’ve ever met.”

I tried to stand straight for her. “Best-looking too.”

She brought my forehead against hers. “I married a dream tonight and I haven’t even married you.”

Over her shoulder, I saw Porsche climbing out onto the crushed rear deck. Tank took her forearm and helped her step onto the dock. She walked past us holding one broken fingernail up for inspection.

Behind us, Leon announced that he had never loved an automobile more and never wanted to see it again. The boat sank another inch beneath the Bentley, politely ending the option.

Knox's officers came down the side path while I sat on the patio, and I watched them take custody of Roman and his men. Roman left wet. I hoped the seats in the police car were cloth. My new dock was broken, the lawn looked excavated, and one of the patio doors hung in glittering pieces. The kitchen remained full of friends arguing about who had screamed first. I held on to that last fact while Shawna pressed a fresh towel against my side.

Knox called that night.

Knox got straight to the part that concerned her. “Two boats in five days?”

I shifted the phone to my other ear. “First one I cleared.”

“That distinction may save me a form,” Knox said. I heard paper move beside her phone. “Could you do that in uniform?”

Shawna lay curled against me on the sofa. The house was still awake around us. I looked at the top of her head and smiled.

I looked at my bandages beneath the open shirt. “Depends how nice you make the uniform.”

The club opened for me the following night.

I wore white because I wanted the room to know I wasn't expecting to bleed. This was a clothing decision, not a medical prediction. Shawna wore blue silk with her back bare and long earrings that touched her shoulders. Rosa had done her hair. I had bought the earrings. Dante’s watch remained on her wrist, loose beneath the new beauty we had arranged around her.

Blue lights ran the length of the sidewalk. Above the entrance, my name stood in letters taller than the apartment windows I had grown up behind. They had put it there without asking. I got out and looked up long enough for a driver behind us to lean on his horn.

Shawna took my arm. “They waiting on you.”

I kept looking up at my name. “Let them.”

Inside, the west side had dressed for its own imagined future. Velvet jackets crowded the tables. Champagne bottles sweated in silver tubs. Through the open door of a private room, I saw cash beside a mirror striped with cocaine, all of it lit more gently than the customers deserved. Women I knew from bad months wore shoes that cost more than those months. I recognized men who owed half the room money. They were buying bottles with both hands. Apparently their plan was to get the creditors too drunk to count.

A dancer stepped down from her platform and kissed my cheek. “You made it, Aiden.”

I reached into my jacket. “We made it.”

I put a stack of cash in her hand. She held it against her chest before climbing back up. Shawna saw the gift and moved closer to my side.

At a rear table, a man in a silver shirt stood when we approached. We had spent a reckless weekend together the previous year, broke and drunk, promising things off his balcony that neither of us could afford. I remembered telling him one day I would own the view. He looked at the room behind me as if checking whether I had finally made the down payment.

He kissed the corner of my mouth before I could turn. Shawna’s arm stiffened through mine.

The man in the silver shirt put a hand on my shoulder. “Look at you. Knew you had something.”

“He still has it,” Shawna told him.

The man smiled at her. “Keep it, girl. Cost me a weekend’s sleep.”

At our table, Shawna stopped with her hand on the back of a chair. “How many people in here you fucked?”

I looked around. Purely to improve accuracy.

Shawna caught my chin and turned it back toward her. “Don’t look around. Answer me.”

I pointed at my mouth, seeking a useful limit. “We counting kisses?”

Shawna hit my good shoulder with her purse. “I want you sitting your ass down.”

I sat. Surviving T had not made me interested in fighting Shawna in those earrings.

She settled sideways on my lap. Pain pulled me forward before I could hide it. Shawna immediately lifted her weight off me. Then she moved into the chair beside me, keeping her hand on my thigh. Whenever someone approached, she drew me a little closer by the collar before letting go.

I loved being claimed in public. That is the honest part. People who had wanted me for a weekend could look across the table and see that somebody expected me on Monday. I had spent so much of my life feeling leaveable that even her jealousy looked like a place to put my coat.

Then the music stopped.

Rosa stood onstage in the red velvet coat, gold shining beneath it. She took the microphone and said somebody owed her a song.

Shawna shook her head before her name was mentioned.

“I ain’t said your name yet, bitch,” Rosa told her.

People began clapping. Rosa held out her free hand toward our table. She left it there without adding another word. The clapping continued while Shawna stared at that hand.

Shawna turned toward me. “You set this up?”

“Wasn't me,” I told Shawna. “I'd have bought an orchestra. There'd be a nigga standing on your dress with a tuba.”

She laughed once, then looked back at Rosa's outstretched hand. Shawna placed her own hand against her stomach. I moved my chair so she had room to get past.

She pushed her chair back and rose. Her first steps looked harder than mine coming out of the hospital. At the stage, Rosa kissed her cheek and passed her the microphone.

Rosa leaned close enough to Shawna that the microphone caught her voice. “You know the one.”

Shawna closed her eyes. The piano began.

She sang about a porch light left burning. About a car passing the house. About getting dressed for someone who wasn't coming home.

Her first line trembled. She steadied the microphone with her other hand and kept going. By the second verse, men had stopped pouring drinks. A woman at the bar bent over her own hands. Shawna’s voice moved through the room without needing to be loud. It made every table feel close to where she stood.

The watch slid down her wrist when she lifted the microphone.

Behind me, a man at the next table leaned toward his companion. I heard him whisper that it was Dante's song.

I turned so fast the wound pulled. He wasn't even looking at me. His face was lifted toward the stage; he wiped one eye with his thumb and went still again. I couldn't find a sneer, a challenge, any little sign I could take as permission to be angry at him. The ugly feeling in my chest belonged to me.

My name glowed outside. Inside, the woman I had brought home sang for her husband.

I swallowed what was in the glass before me. Then Vega’s instructions arrived in my head and I set it down, furious that I needed a doctor to supervise my jealousy while a dead man got a whole fucking song.

On the last verse, Shawna opened her eyes and looked directly at me.

I didn't know which of us she saw. She held my eyes through the end of the verse, and I hated that I couldn't just be happy she was looking at me.

When the song ended, the club rose. People struck their tables and shouted. Rosa cried without hiding it. I stood slowly and clapped until my palms hurt.

Shawna came back smiling. She was still holding the microphone until Rosa hurried after her and took it from her hand. Shawna laughed at herself, then reached for me.

“Did you like it?” Shawna asked. Her face was bright; she'd barely seemed able to cross the room before the song, and now she stood close enough for me to feel her breathing.

I kissed her before I answered. Long enough for people to see. Long enough to turn a question about her singing into a demonstration of who stood beside her.

“Beautiful,” I said when I let her go.

She touched my face. “Why you looking at me like that?”

“Like what?” I asked.

Shawna's smile faded. She took her hand from my cheek. “Never mind, baby.”

Later, a man from the cash tables caught Rosa by her coat as she tried to leave him. I saw her stop, turn, and remove his hand. He put it back.

“I paid,” he said.

Rosa peeled his fingers off her coat again. “You paid for last time.”

The man caught a fresh handful of velvet and pulled Rosa a step back toward his chair.

I was out of the chair before Tank reached me. Shawna said my name behind me, but I was watching the man's fingers bunch Rosa’s velvet. He looked over his shoulder, saw me coming, and continued to hold on.

I hit him once.

The blow sent him to the carpet. Something beneath my bandages pulled hard enough to turn my stomach. Tank caught my elbow before I could throw a second punch.

“Take your hands off her,” I told him.

He wasn't holding anything by then. I pointed at him anyway. “You hear me?”

The man nodded from the floor.

Rosa straightened her coat. Her hands shook. Our eyes met, and she tucked both hands into her pockets.

“Get him out,” I told Tank.

“Sit down,” Tank told me.

I pointed at the man again. “His ass first.”

Tank beckoned to two of our men and passed the customer to them. Then he took my arm and walked me into the office while Shawna and Rosa followed.

The people we passed had gone quiet. A woman moved her chair to clear our path. A man started to raise his glass, saw the red spreading down my shirt, and set it on the table. I had come in imagining a whole night of people admiring me. Now Tank had to hold me upright because I'd thrown one punch. Somewhere behind us, Rosa's heels kept striking the floor, and I listened until I knew she was following.

In the office, Shawna unbuttoned my jacket and found red coming through the shirt.

Shawna pressed a folded towel over the blood. “You dumb motherfucker.”

I leaned back against the office chair. “I'm okay.”

Shawna looked up at me without taking pressure off the towel. “You don't know another fucking sentence?”

Rosa stood by the door with both hands hidden inside her coat. She started to say she hadn't needed me to do that.

“I know,” I told her. “I wanted.”

She pressed her lips together. Then she came over and kissed the top of my head again.

Rosa kept her mouth against my hair a moment. “Stupid nigga.”

She stayed while Shawna found a clean shirt. Tank stood in the doorway. When someone came to ask about the music, he told them to turn it back on, and the bass reached us faintly through the office wall.

At home, the lake was black beneath the windows. Shawna stood at the bathroom counter removing the earrings. She laid them side by side, then began opening the clasp of Dante’s watch.

I watched from the doorway. I was tired enough to feel my knees shaking. That should have warned me to sit down and stop talking. Instead I offered to buy her a watch that fitted.

She held the clasp open against her palm.

Shawna held up the watch. “I have one.”

I looked at the loose links gathered in her palm. “It hangs off you.”

Shawna lowered her hand. “I like it.”

I rubbed the doorframe with my thumb. “I can see that.”

She turned toward me. The room had enough marble to carry a whisper, and suddenly every small movement sounded accusatory.

Shawna set her free hand flat on the counter. “Say it, Aiden.”

I looked at the watch again. “That song was about Dante?”

Shawna answered before I could look away. “Yes.”

She gave me the truth without padding it. I wanted one little lie to lie down beside. Something that would let me postpone the work of sharing a room with a past I couldn't defeat.

I forced my eyes back to her face. “You look at me when you sing to him?”

Shawna stared at me. “I looked at you because you were there.”

“I'm always there,” I said. “His watch. My earrings. His chain on my fucking neck. I put you in a house and he already wanted the water.”

Shawna looked at the cross against my clean shirt. I wished I had stopped at the watch. Instead I spread my hands, inviting her to admire the case I'd made.

She reached toward the cross. “You want it off?”

I caught her wrist.

We both stopped.

I let go. Quickly, but the quickness didn't erase the fact that my hand had closed around her before I had decided what right I thought I possessed.

“Don't,” I said.

She rubbed the place my fingers had been.

“I'm sorry,” I said. I put my hands at my sides and left them there. She watched me for a moment before letting her hand fall.

“What do you want me to give you, Aiden?” Shawna asked.

I looked down at the floor I had bought and couldn't find a place on it that made me feel less lost.

I could barely make myself look up when I answered. “One thing that’s only mine.”

The tight line beside her mouth eased. She didn't apologize, and I didn't ask her to. She came close and laid her palm over my heartbeat, clear of the wound.

“This is you,” she said. “I know who this is.”

Then I asked why she had said his name in the hospital.

She closed her eyes. I wished I had kept the question inside. I also knew I had been carrying it around looking for somewhere to set it down, and the watch had only been a convenient table.

“I dreamed he was there,” she said. “I was trying to tell him you were hurt.”

I didn't know what to do with that. I had imagined his name taking me out of the bed. What she was telling me now put us both there: her dead husband in a dream, me bleeding in the world she had to wake up to. I'd spent days punishing myself with a story she hadn't told.

She put the watch on the counter and took my hand. Before leading me to bed, she picked it up again and carried it with us. She laid it on her bedside table and helped me lower myself onto the mattress. We had not solved the argument. The watch stayed within her reach. The cross stayed on my neck. She kissed me until I stopped asking questions that needed more of us than that exhausted night could give.

She switched off the bedside lamp and put her head against my good side.

I woke once before dawn. The lake beyond the curtains had begun to separate itself from the sky. I could hear Shawna breathing beside me, feel the weight of her hand against the sheet.

She was still there.

For that morning, that was enough.

## 5. Flowers for the Man I Killed

Big T's coffin was candy blue.

I had approved that color myself. The funeral director slid a little rectangle of it across his desk, and I held it against the light until it shone like the door of a car nobody could afford. Then I pointed at the gold handles in his catalog. By the time I finished spending money, the man had stopped using the word package. We were developing a fucking estate.

I paid for the flowers, the cars, the musicians and the meal afterward. I ordered a portrait six feet high. T smiled in it with one hand tucked into his jacket, looking pleased to be the most expensive thing in the room even after dying.

He had called me son. Then he had shot me. I couldn't get those two things to occupy the same place in my head, so I kept buying things that could occupy the chapel.

On the morning of the funeral, I stood before the mirror downstairs trying to close my cuff. My black suit covered the bandages. It didn't cover the fact that my fingers kept missing a button directly in front of them. The medicine left a little delay between what I wanted and what my body agreed to do.

Tank came down wearing black too. He stopped behind me, large and dark in the glass.

“You ain't gotta go,” Tank said.

I tried the button again. “I killed him.”

Tank took the cuff from my fingers. “That's why you ain't gotta go.”

He closed it for me without looking up. That was worse, somehow, than if he'd told me I looked sick.

I watched his face in the mirror. “He got nobody else?”

Tank smoothed the sleeve. “Plenty people.”

I turned toward him. “People that loved him.”

Tank went to the window. Beyond him, men were waiting beside the cars. I could see one of them adjusting his tie in a windshield, using both hands with a freedom that irritated the shit out of me.

I let my arm drop. “You loved him.”

Tank kept looking outside.

“You can say it,” I told him. “I'm standing right here.”

Tank rubbed his thumb along the window frame. “He gave me work when I came out.”

I looked down at the cross resting against my shirt. “Gave me a car.”

Tank finally turned. “Ain't the same.”

I nodded. “I know.”

I did know, though I had needed him to say it. A gift could be a kindness. It could also be a hook. Those didn't cancel each other just because it would have been easier to bury a man who had only ever been cruel.

We stood there in our funeral suits, the two men who had left T beside a motel pool. I could still remember the sound his body made against the fence. Now I was wondering whether the flowers I'd bought were enough. There was no dignified way through a morning like that. I was hoping for a way through at all.

Then Shawna came down the stairs dressed entirely in white.

Tank's head went up. “Oh, hell no.”

She stopped on the bottom step. Her coat hung open over a white dress. She carried white gloves in one hand, and the watch on her wrist caught the light when she rested her other hand on the banister. Dante's watch. I could see my own black sleeve reflected in its face.

Shawna looked at Tank. “What?”

Tank held both hands out toward her outfit. “It's a funeral.”

Shawna came down the last step. “I know whose.”

Tank looked at me, then back at her. “You can't wear that.”

Shawna waited for my answer.

I could have asked her to change. I could have explained that we already had enough problems without arriving as the bride and groom of the man I'd killed. But she stood in that white dress with her chin level and her husband's watch on, and I wasn't about to make her put on mourning for his murderer.

Also, she looked fucking beautiful.

I picked up my jacket. “Tank, get the car before she decides on a veil.”

The chapel stood above a circular drive, its front steps facing a little fountain and the cemetery entrance across the road. Black cars filled the spaces along both sides. Our men had left room for the hearse by the steps. A wheelchair waited there for me too. I looked at it as Tank opened my door, and then I looked away before he could make a reasonable suggestion.

Shawna helped me out. Once I was upright, a pallbearer came over and held out his hand. Rico, thirty-two. He had his wrist turned toward the light before I finished shaking it.

“Daughter sent these,” Rico told me. “Moved out of state, still telling me how to dress.”

He turned his wrist so the design caught the sun. I held his sleeve lightly and looked.

“Girl got taste,” I told Rico.

“Told her that,” he said, smiling with his whole face. “She said I better wear them where people could see.”

He stepped aside for us. I took Shawna's hand and climbed the steps slowly. Tank stayed close enough behind us that I could hear his shoes stop every time mine did.

Conversation thinned when we entered the chapel. Some people stood. Others remained so firmly seated I could see their backs press into the pews. A woman near the front began crying harder. I didn't know whether seeing me had increased her grief or given it somebody to blame.

I kept moving.

A week before, T had sent me to pick up a woman and a bag of money. Now he lay at the far end of this aisle, his men followed me, and the woman had her fingers between mine. I hadn't felt proud of that arrangement until a man behind a pew whispered that I shouldn't have come.

Pride rushed into me hot enough to scare me.

I stopped without turning. “Which nigga said it? Say it loud so I know who to look at.”

Nobody repeated himself. I waited one breath longer than I needed to, then made my legs carry me to the second row.

Shawna sat with her knees together. Her unused gloves lay in her lap. I held her bare hand while the preacher told us about a generous man.

That part was true. I knew men T had found rooms for when nobody would open a door. I had seen him pay a bill, fold the receipt, and tell the person thanking him to go home before he changed his mind. Some of the people crying around me had eaten because he'd answered his phone. I could hear them agreeing with the preacher, little yeses rising from different parts of the room.

Then the preacher began talking about T's love for his family.

Shawna's fingers slipped out of mine.

I turned. She stared at the portrait. One of the white gloves was balled in her fist, and a thread at its seam stretched until it broke. I put my palm on her knee. She didn't look at me, but she let it stay.

Tank stood up beside us.

He hadn't told me he was speaking. He walked to the front with a folded sheet of paper, opened it at the microphone, and looked down. I watched his chest rise inside the black jacket. He folded the page again and put it away.

“T knew my mother,” Tank said. His first words boomed through the little chapel. He moved back from the microphone and tried again. “Knew she worried about me.”

The preacher took a step aside. Tank put one hand on the lectern.

“Every time I came around, he made me call her,” Tank told us. “Could be three niggas waiting on money and one nigga waiting to get his ass beat. Didn't matter. T would hand me the phone and tell me to call my mother. Tell her I ate.”

A woman near the aisle laughed while wiping her nose. Tank glanced toward the sound, then looked at the blue coffin.

“She died while I was inside,” Tank said. “He paid for everything. Told me I didn't owe him for that.”

He rubbed his free hand down the front of his suit. The microphone picked up the cloth scraping beneath his palm.

Tank looked out over the pews. “I owed him for it anyway.”

Beside me, Shawna drew a long breath through her nose. I kept my hand still on her knee.

Tank's voice grew rougher. “He did some shit.”

The preacher shifted his weight. Tank looked at him, then returned to us.

“Some shit I can't stand here and fix,” Tank said. “I ain't gonna try. But he picked me up when nobody was answering. And I remember that.”

He left the lectern without taking the paper out again. When he reached our row, he looked at me. I nodded and moved my knee so he could pass.

It hurt to hear him. I kept my face turned toward Tank until he sat down. His hands lay open on his knees, and I left him room to keep them there.

Red's flowers stood beside the coffin, taller than every other arrangement. White roses. Blue ribbon. His name printed on a card large enough to qualify as attending in person.

He hadn't come. I looked at the door every time it opened anyway, and hated him for making me wait at a dead man's funeral.

When the preacher invited us forward, Shawna stayed seated. Other people filed past our row, touching one another's shoulders. I waited until there was room in the aisle, then leaned toward her.

“You don't have to,” I told Shawna.

She opened her fist and smoothed the torn glove against her thigh. “I know.”

Then she stood and took my hand.

Up close, T looked almost comfortable. Somebody had cleaned the blood from him and settled his head against white satin. The ring that had cut my mouth lay on his folded hand. I remembered wanting to impress him. I remembered how quickly I had believed him when he called me son.

I stood over a corpse embarrassed by how much I had wanted from it.

Shawna took a folded photograph from her coat. She opened it against her palm before placing it on T's chest, and I recognized the little chapel where she and Dante had married. In this print, his brother hadn't been cut out of the edge. T stood with a hand on Dante's shoulder. Dante smiled as if there were nothing in the world behind him he needed to watch.

Shawna pushed the photograph beneath T's folded fingers. “You can take your fucking picture with you.”

I thought she was finished. Then she bent closer to his face.

“He loved you,” Shawna whispered. “You knew he did.”

Her voice broke on loved. I looked at the gold handle nearest my knee. Behind us, the preacher cleared his throat, and I wanted to tell him to swallow whatever the fuck was bothering him. Let her say it. Nothing left on his schedule could matter more to T.

Shawna straightened and wiped beneath one eye with the side of her finger. I touched T's cuff. I still don't know why. The cloth was cold and smooth. I could imagine his arm moving inside it, reaching up to pull me into that proud embrace he had given me behind the club.

“All you had to do was let us go,” I told him.

I took my hand away before I could wait for an answer.

Outside, Rosa stood beside the cars in sunglasses, holding the front of her coat against the wind. I'd heard her tell two people already that she was there for Shawna. When I reached her, she made sure I had the information too.

“I ain't crying for that nigga,” Rosa told me.

I glanced back toward the chapel. “Nobody asked you to.”

Rosa lifted her chin. “I know. I just want the dead to be clear.”

Shawna leaned against her. Rosa opened the coat enough to pull her inside it, and the two women stood together while the pallbearers brought T down the steps.

Mercedes and Porsche waited by our Mercedes with a tray of sandwiches. They had dressed for church and brought provisions. In our circle, even an ambush had a chance of being catered.

A new white Bentley stood a few spaces away. Porsche showed me the card Leon had sent with it. He'd written that watching the last car die had changed his feelings about ownership. I admired a man capable of learning the wrong lesson so expensively.

Tank took two sandwiches. “That's love.”

Porsche held the tray steady for him. “That's a nigga with insurance.”

Rico adjusted his cuffs before taking the coffin's weight again. He and the other five pallbearers brought T to the hearse. The driver held the rear door open while they slid the coffin onto its rollers and secured it. Rico stood back, smoothing his sleeve over the gold link.

The first black SUV came slowly around the chapel. A second followed close behind. Sunlight lay on their dark windows. Through the first windshield, I could make out men leaning forward. One turned sideways, and a gun barrel appeared beside the lowered passenger window.

Tank stopped chewing. “Down.”

The sandwich tray burst in Porsche's hands.

I caught Shawna around the waist and took us down behind the Mercedes. My knees struck first. Then my side hit the door, and pain went through me so hard I couldn't get air for a moment. Rosa fell beside us, her coat spread across the pavement.

Glass showered from the chapel doors. I heard screams and more shots, close together, and looked past the rear wheel toward the hearse.

Rico was lying beside it. One arm lay straight out from his body. Sunlight flashed from the cuff link I'd admired. Another pallbearer crawled toward him and caught his shoulder. Rico didn't turn.

Tank fired over the Mercedes's hood. Porsche dragged Mercedes down behind the rear tire as a burst tore our mirror off. It struck the ground beside Rosa, who pulled her coat away from it as if the mirror had been deliberately rude.

“Everybody inside!” I shouted.

The doors were open, but the drive between the cars and the chapel had become a place nobody could stand. Halfway down the steps, an older woman remained upright with her husband's hand in hers. He was trying to pull her down. She stared at Rico and didn't move.

I pointed them out to Tank. “Get her!”

Tank looked down at me. “You stay here.”

I pressed my back against the car. “I'm sitting, ain't I?”

Tank ran bent low across the drive. A wreath behind him jumped as bullets stripped flowers from its frame. He reached the couple, caught the old man under one arm, and pushed the woman ahead of him into the chapel. The three of them disappeared past the broken doors.

The first SUV moved toward us. Its passenger leaned out farther, trying to get an angle behind the Mercedes. I drew my pistol, braced my wrist against the tire, and fired. The man jerked back inside. The SUV clipped the fountain, swung wide, and ran straight over my empty wheelchair.

I watched a wheel spring free and roll down the drive.

“He hit my fucking chair,” I told Shawna.

She fired twice at the second SUV before ducking back beside me. “You weren't using it.”

I looked at the folded wreckage. “Still mine.”

Across the drive, the hearse began rolling.

The pallbearers had got T inside before the shooting. I could see blue through the rear glass. The driver's door stood open, and the driver lay on the pavement behind it. I couldn't tell whether he'd been hit or had dropped to avoid the bullets. The engine was still running. With nobody at the wheel, the hearse crept down the slope toward the street.

The first SUV turned after it. Its passenger jumped out and ran toward the open driver's door.

Rosa caught my sleeve. “They're taking T.”

For a second I just looked at her. I'd bought the man a coffin. I'd got him into it. There should have been a point in this process where he stopped generating fucking errands.

Shawna caught my jacket as I started to rise. “Aiden.”

I watched the gunman reach the hearse. “They don't get him.”

Shawna pulled me back enough to make me face her. “Baby, he's dead.”

I kissed her once. “Then he ain't driving himself home. Stay with the car.”

I tucked my pistol into my waistband and went across the drive. I needed both hands for the door, the man, anything that might keep me on my feet. My ribs felt as if somebody had hooked a finger beneath them and was pulling. A voice shouted my name from the chapel. Another man called for everyone to look at me. I wished one of them would look at the fucking shooter.

He had a hand on the hearse's wheel when I reached him. I caught the back of his jacket and slammed him against the open door. His gun went off above us. We stumbled alongside the moving car, his shoulder driving me into the window frame.

He was bigger than me and had arrived with all his blood inside him. That seemed to be his entire argument.

He jammed an elbow against my throat. I couldn't get a full breath. I pulled his jacket close and drove my forehead into his nose. His grip broke. Blood spread over his mouth, and I shoved him backward off the door before climbing into the driver's seat.

The hearse hit the curb while I reached for the wheel. Both front tires thumped up and down. Behind me, T's coffin struck its restraints.

“You can shut up too,” I told him.

I got the door closed and drove across the road into the cemetery.

The main drive was wide enough for the hearse, though it didn't feel that way with gravestones flashing past both windows. I kept to the gravel between the rows, hands tight on the wheel. The first SUV followed through the gate. The second had still been beside the chapel when I left; I lost sight of it as I turned between the trees. In the mirror I saw a flash inside the pursuing car's windshield, and then the hearse's rear window broke apart.

Glass scattered over the coffin. One gold handle shone through the mess.

“They're scratching your box,” I told T. “You better appreciate what this cost.”

Ahead, the drive widened around a stone circle. I took it hard, felt the hearse lean, and waited for the coffin to move. It held. Gravel rattled beneath us. In the mirror, the SUV slid sideways before its driver got the nose pointed after me again.

I took the narrower drive toward the service gate. A groundskeeper stood beside a watering truck, holding a running hose. When the hearse came at him, he stepped back so quickly he left the hose hanging in the air for a moment before dropping it.

I lifted two fingers off the wheel as I passed.

He lifted two fingers back. Water ran over his shoes while he watched us go. The man maintained a professional greeting through the entire fucking thing.

The hearse cleared the service gate onto a paved road. The SUV came after me with a bent bumper and one headlight dark. Another engine rose ahead, and I had just enough time to think they had sent a third car before the white Bentley crossed the intersection.

Shawna was driving. Her coat was off. Her white gloves held the wheel. Porsche sat beside her with her purse braced against the dash, grinning so broadly I could see it through the windshield.

Only later did Shawna tell me how they'd got clear. Our men had pinned the second SUV behind the parked cars. She'd handed her coat to Rosa while Porsche fetched the Bentley's keys, then they had driven out through the other end of the lot. Tank stayed until two men took his place at the chapel doors. Then he followed them in the Mercedes.

At that moment I knew none of it. I saw the Bentley and the two women inside it, and I saw exactly where Shawna was aiming the fucking thing.

Shawna turned toward the pursuing SUV.

“Baby, don't—” I shouted through my open window.

The Bentley hit its front corner before I finished. The SUV spun toward a low stone wall. The Bentley turned the other way, tires pouring white smoke, Porsche's door flying open and slamming shut with the spin. Then the SUV struck the wall and its hood sprang up.

I stopped the hearse beside the Bentley. Broken glass covered Shawna's lap. She looked across at me through the space where her side window had been.

“Your wife drives too,” Shawna said.

We weren't married. I found no useful reason to raise the subject. I was busy falling in love with her for the fourth fucking time before lunch.

A man got out of the SUV holding a gun. I opened my door, drew mine, and fired from behind its edge. He dropped behind the engine. His weapon hit the gutter. Another man reached out to drag him back toward the vehicle, then froze when our Mercedes appeared at the other end of the road.

Tank got out with his pistol raised. The second man's hands went up so high I thought he might leave the rest of his body behind.

“Who sent you?” Tank called.

The man looked past him at the Mercedes.

Tank advanced a step. “That's the easy question.”

The man turned back. “T had friends!”

I got out of the hearse, keeping its open door between me and the wreck. “Where the fuck were you when they passed the collection plate?”

The man looked at me, then through the hearse's shattered rear window.

“You killed him,” he said.

I pointed toward the coffin with my free hand. “And I'm burying him. Come between those things again, you can ask him how he feels about it yourself.”

His eyes dropped to the pistol in my hand. I followed his gaze and saw the blood beginning to show through my shirt. Shawna opened the Bentley's door behind me. Glass fell onto the road around her white shoes.

The man looked from me to her. “You don't stop.”

His voice had lost its shout. I thought I heard something almost admiring in it, though I wasn't interested in interviewing him about the finer points.

I held the gun steady. “You should've asked somebody before you came.”

Police cruisers arrived behind Tank. Knox got out of the first. I hadn't called her. She told me afterward that enough other people had called to describe three separate wars, each starring me.

Her officers spread along the road and took over from Tank. Guns were kicked away. The men from the SUV went face down on the pavement. I lowered my pistol when an officer reached me and pointed it safely at the ground.

Knox stopped beside the hearse. “Please tell me that's empty.”

I nodded toward the back. “Family.”

She looked through the broken window, took off her glasses, and looked again.

“You brought the deceased into a pursuit?” Knox asked.

I wiped sweat from my upper lip. “He wasn't walking.”

For three seconds she stood absolutely still. Then Knox put her hands on her hips and laughed toward the sky. One of the men in handcuffs raised his head to watch her. An officer pressed him back down, leaving the chief free to enjoy the evidence.

Knox wiped an eye beneath her glasses. “Aiden, you're wasting your fucking talents in civilian clothes.”

I looked at the blood on my civilian shirt. “You keep saying that.”

Knox put her glasses back on. “Till somebody gives me what I want.”

I secured my pistol as Shawna reached me. I took her hand and eased the torn glove back from a shallow cut. There was more blood on the cloth than the little wound seemed able to account for.

“Most of that's yours,” Shawna told me before I could ask.

I kissed the unbroken skin beside it. “I asked you to stay with the car.”

Shawna looked back at the Bentley. “I did. I moved the car.”

Porsche came around the crushed front fender and gave Tank the keys.

“Tell Leon this one had a meaningful life too,” Porsche said.

Tank looked at the wreck, then down at the key in his palm. “He gonna start sending bicycles.”

We returned T to the chapel in the same hearse, a cruiser ahead of us and Shawna beside me. I drove slowly. With the shooting finished, I could feel every movement I'd made catching up to my body. Shawna kept one hand on my knee, and I tried to breathe without showing her how carefully I had to do it.

The second SUV stood where I'd last seen it. Its doors were open now. Men sat along the curb with their hands behind them while officers watched over them. Medical workers moved through the guests on the steps.

People came out when they heard the engines. A woman on the steps clapped above her head until her hat slid crooked. Somebody shouted my name. I stopped by the curb and waited a moment before opening the door. Then I lowered my feet to the pavement and pulled myself upright against the doorframe.

The older couple Tank had brought inside came toward us. The man leaned on his wife's arm now. He told me he was seventy and had never seen anything like it. Then his wife took my face in both hands.

“You brought everybody home,” she told me.

I looked beyond her shoulder. A white sheet lay beside the curb where Rico had fallen. The breeze lifted one edge and put it down again.

Not everybody.

Tank came close and told me Vernon had died inside after the men carried him in. Forty-one. Two people gone while I had been racing around with somebody who was already dead.

I held the woman's hands gently and lowered them from my face. I couldn't explain what her gratitude had just done to me, and I didn't want to make her regret surviving.

“Help me finish this,” I told Tank.

The funeral workers brought a replacement hearse alongside ours. There was glass all over T's coffin. One man brushed it away with a folded cloth while the rest of us took our places. Tank and I stood on one side. Four other men joined us at the handles.

We lifted together. My side pulled. I held on.

The musicians started the hymn again while we carried T between the cars. The first few notes wavered. Then the voices came in, and people stopped talking long enough for us to lay him down.

Nobody interrupted him that time.

Tank told me later they buried T that afternoon. I wasn't at the graveside. By then, Vega had me upstairs at the house with my ruined shirt open, and the look on her face had persuaded every armed man in the hallway to discover an urgent duty elsewhere.

Shawna sat on the edge of the bed in her dirty white dress. Vega stood over me, removing the dressing.

“You were told to rest,” Vega said.

I watched her hands. “I was sitting at a funeral.”

Vega looked up. “I'm interested in what happened after the sitting.”

I turned my head toward the window. That was the only part of me I could move without inviting further discussion.

Vega cleaned me up and wrote out her instructions. She put the paper on the nightstand and tapped it with her pen.

“If this gets worse, he comes back under my care,” Vega told Shawna. “Not after another event. Not when he finishes a telephone call.”

I opened my mouth. Vega held up one finger without looking at me. I closed it again.

Shawna leaned over to read the paper. “Say that last part again.”

I lay between them while they discussed my body like a damaged car neither one trusted me to drive.

When Vega left, Tank came in with the names. Rico and Vernon. The others who'd been hurt. He made me repeat them, and I did.

I reached toward the phone. “I'll pay for everything.”

Tank put it in my hand. “Their mothers need a human being first.”

So I called.

Rico's mother answered after several rings. I gave my name and sat straighter against the pillows.

“Rico was at the funeral with me,” I told her. “I'm sorry.”

“They told me,” his mother said.

I looked down at the blanket. Somebody laughed in the hall before a door closed over the sound.

“Was he by himself?” Rico's mother asked.

I closed my hand around a fold of the blanket. “One of the men carrying the coffin went right to him. I saw him reach him.”

She drew a breath. I waited while she cried. Shawna moved her hand beside mine without trying to open my fist.

“Showed me his cuff links,” I told Rico's mother when she grew quieter. “The ones his daughter sent. He was wearing them.”

“Called me about those,” she said.

I shut my eyes. “He looked good.”

She began telling me about him. I stayed on the line. When she went back to something she'd already said, I listened again. My hand had opened beside Shawna's before the call ended.

Vernon's mother made me give my name twice. She wanted to know why her son had gone to a funeral and why anybody had brought guns. I told her what I'd seen. There were things I couldn't answer. She kept asking them, and I kept the phone against my ear. I didn't tell her about the hearse or how many people had thanked me. None of that could have given her a son to answer for himself.

After the second call ended, I threw the phone against the wall.

It struck below a painting and fell to the carpet. Shawna watched it land. Tank picked it up, set it on the dresser, and left us alone.

“I want Red,” I told Shawna.

She turned toward me. “You don't know it was him.”

I pushed myself up against the pillows. “Who the fuck else?”

Shawna folded her gloves together. “People loved T.”

I looked toward the door Tank had closed. “I paid for his funeral.”

Shawna kept her voice level. “You also killed him.”

I wanted her angry beside me. Instead she had said something true, and I hated the truth enough to reach for the worst thing I could accuse her of.

“You defending Red now?” I asked.

Shawna set the gloves down. Her mouth became a straight line.

“Say that again,” she told me.

I didn't.

She opened the clasp of Dante's watch and laid it on the bed between us. The face pointed upward. I could hear its bracelet settle against itself.

Shawna rested a finger beside it. “You think I forgot what those men did?”

I looked at the watch.

“House and earrings,” Shawna said. “That's what you think it took? Now I don't remember where they left him?”

I shook my head. “No.”

Shawna picked the watch up. “Then don't talk to me like I'm sleeping through this.”

Her fingers shook while she tried to close the clasp. I reached toward her hand and stopped short of touching it. She held her wrist out. I fastened the watch, carefully, feeling how little force it took to help with something I couldn't bear to see.

“I'm scared,” I said.

Shawna sat still beside me.

I'd rather have taken another punch than repeat it. I looked down at the white dress bunched against her knees and saw her on the pavement again, glass falling around her head.

I kept my fingers beneath her wrist. “I'm scared they get you while I'm laying here.”

Shawna shifted closer on the mattress. “Then stop trying to stand up every time somebody says your name.”

I bent my forehead to hers. For a while we stayed like that, with the watch between our hands and the curtains moving in the air from the vent.

Outside the bedroom, Rosa ordered Tank to eat something. His answer came through the door, low and tired: he wasn't hungry.
