“I didn't ask for your fucking feelings about the sandwich,” Rosa called back.

Shawna let out one small laugh through her nose. I felt it against my cheek.

I held on to her.

At sunset, Tank returned carrying a black envelope on a silver tray. He told us a man from the Saint had left it at the gate. I recognized the tray from the kitchen and watched him bring it toward the bed with both hands beneath it.

“Why you serving me a threat?” I asked Tank.

He lowered the tray onto my knees. “Didn't want to touch it more than I had to.”

I took the envelope. Inside was a breakfast invitation printed in gold: tomorrow morning, penthouse at the Saint. My name and Shawna's sat together on the card as if somebody had been delighted to arrange our company.

A smaller card lay behind it.

I read the handwriting once. Then I passed it to Shawna.

She had been standing beside the dresser. She sat down so abruptly that the mattress moved beneath me. Her eyes stayed on the card while her thumb found Dante's watch.

Tank stepped closer. “What?”

Shawna didn't answer. Neither did I. I watched her thumb stop against the metal and thought about the photograph now buried with T, his hand on his brother's shoulder.

Red had written one sentence.

*Dante didn't die for three million.*

## 6. Forty Million Reasons to Die

I went to see Red the morning after the funeral with a bullet in my body and a diamond pin covering the hole in my coat.

I had better coats. Wearing that one felt like an answer, though I hadn't decided which question I wanted him to ask. Tank drove. I sat beside him with Red's little card open on my knee and read the same sentence I'd been reading all night.

*Dante didn't die for three million.*

Red ink. The motherfucker had coordinated the threat with his name. I'd lain beside Shawna wondering what she hadn't told me, then hated myself for letting his handwriting into our bed. By morning I had resolved to look like a man who hadn't thought about him at all.

Tank glanced at the card. “You sleep?”

I turned it facedown. “I can dream about firing you with my eyes open.”

Tank opened the console at a light and handed me the bottle Vega had left. “Take what she said.”

I put it in my pocket. “I heard the doctor.”

Tank returned his hand to the wheel. “Then listen to her.”

He wore black again. Yesterday he'd carried a coffin. Today he was driving me toward a man who had sent flowers to it. A cut crossed his knuckles. I didn't remember seeing it happen, and I realized there were whole pieces of the previous day I'd never asked him about.

At the Saint, the valet had barely reached our car before two other men stepped past him. I opened my door slowly and stood with the card in my hand. One of the men looked at the shape beneath my jacket and held his palm out.

I offered him the card. “You can have the note.”

He left his hand where it was.

I nodded toward the building. “Your boss sent for me. He know what I come with.”

The man looked over his shoulder. Red stood inside the glass entrance in a cream suit. He raised one finger. Both men moved aside.

Red came forward as I reached the door. “Aiden. Glad you came.”

I tucked the note into my jacket. “Would've been sad if I didn't?”

Red took my hand. “I had breakfast made.”

I let him keep the handshake for a moment. “Then feed me before the conversation gets hungry.”

He put an arm around my shoulders and guided me inside. I let that happen too. I wanted to know how close he would come to a man who had killed his partner. Close enough to squeeze my shoulder, as it turned out. Close enough for his cologne to follow me after he took his hand away.

We passed through a room with a white piano by the windows. There were fresh flowers on its polished lid and a photograph of Shawna singing on the wall above it. Red slowed beneath the picture. I could have kept walking, but he had arranged the little pause so neatly that I stopped and looked.

The Shawna in the photograph held a microphone in both hands. She wore a dress I hadn't seen. The man beside me owned the wall, the piano and the photograph. I felt the deliberate placement of all three as plainly as a finger against my chest.

Red looked at the piano. “You play?”

I glanced behind it. “Breakfast back there?”

Red smiled. “I wondered what you brought to a woman who already sings.”

Tank closed his eyes behind me. “Please.”

I looked back at him. “You got a request?”

Tank opened one eye. “Eggs. Before the niggas serving them retire.”

I pulled out the piano bench.

There was sheet music on the stand with Shawna's name at the top. I sat carefully and drew it closer. The introduction ran across crowded staves, both hands busy, inner notes tucked where a careless player would lose them. Red stood behind my right shoulder while I looked. I could feel him there without turning.

“Difficult arrangement,” Red said.

I set my fingers on the keys. “Haven't heard your kitchen yet. Everything difficult till somebody does it.”

The first chord filled the room. A waiter carrying coffee stopped by the door.

My hands remembered before the rest of me relaxed. A church piano with two bad keys. An old woman tapping my wrist until I quit trying to pound the sound into it. Staying after everybody else had gone because there would be supper if I played while she put the room away. I had learned what an open score could give me long before I could afford anything in this room.

I played the introduction as written. Then I took the melody into my right hand and let the left answer it with something that had been downstairs after church. The room's expensive little silence broke apart. I could hear the sound returning from the windows, clean and bright above the lower notes.

Tank opened both eyes. “Oh, this nigga.”

Red turned toward him. “Something wrong?”

Tank pointed at me, then the keys. “With him? All morning. With that? Listen.”

The coffee waiter shifted his weight in time. He stopped moving his shoulders when Red looked toward him, but one foot kept tapping. I put a little bounce beneath the melody. His heel caught it, his knee joined in, and he glanced down at his own leg as if it had sold him out.

Red moved beside the bench. “You just read that?”

I turned a page. “You left it open.”

Red looked up at the photograph. “Shawna never said—”

I kept playing. “You want music or witness statements?”

A man at the door laughed. Red turned. The man straightened so quickly that I nearly lost the next measure enjoying him.

I finished the piece and lifted my hands. For a moment the last chord hung in the room without anybody helping it. Then one waiter clapped. He looked at Red, looked back at me, and clapped again.

The others joined him.

Tank spread both hands toward the piano. “You ordered that. Asked him what he brought. He unpacked.”

Red waited for the applause to end. “She never mentioned you played.”

I closed the cover gently. “I keep giving her things to find out. Ought to try it.”

Tank caught my elbow as I rose. Pain had started pulling beneath the bandage while I was playing, and he got there just before it showed on my face.

“Breakfast,” Tank said.

I let him steady me. “Hungry?”

Tank guided me toward the lift. “I've lived two years since we parked.”

Upstairs, the penthouse looked scrubbed clean of the night Shawna and I had left it. No overturned furniture. No broken glass. I looked toward the place where she'd stood holding the bag, then followed Red through the terrace doors.

Six waiters surrounded a table set for three. Beyond the railing, the city spread out in the morning sun. Roses stood in narrow crystal vases. The only red flower had been placed beside my plate.

Tank picked it up before sitting.

“Don't eat that,” I told him.

Tank looked at the rose, then at me. “Wasn't going to.”

Red laughed. He had dimples. I hated learning that.

I lowered myself into the chair and shifted to take pressure off my side. Red's eyes followed the movement.

“T knew where to put a bullet,” Red said.

I settled both hands on the table. “Didn't know where to keep his own.”

The waiter filling my water glass stopped with the pitcher tilted. A drop struck the tablecloth. Red raised his orange juice.

“Our absent friend,” he said.

I drank water. Tank set the rose down and left his glass alone. When Red offered him the bacon, though, he took two pieces before looking down at his plate as if breakfast had betrayed him personally.

Red cut into his food. “Where's Shawna?”

I watched his knife. “With people who like her alive.”

Red raised his eyes to mine. “Always liked her alive.”

I leaned back enough to breathe. “Expensive way of showing it.”

Red laid down the fork. “I bought the dress she was wearing when you met her.”

I moved forward too fast. Pain ran through my side, and I held the edge of the table beneath the cloth until it passed.

“Want me to send you the motherfucking dress?” I asked him.

Red rested his hands beside his plate. “I want you to understand how little you understand.”

I let go of the table. “Then use the invitation for something. Explain.”

Red smiled again. “Valentino dies. I give Shawna a room. Protect her from people who might shorten her life over what she did to him. Then my partner arranges to take her and three million out of my house.”

He paused and looked toward Tank. Tank kept his eyes on him.

Red returned to me. “My partner dies. You get his business. His car. His necklace. The woman. Fortunate week.”

I touched the diamond pin on my coat. “Forgot the bullet.”

Red glanced beneath it. “I can see you haven't.”

Tank's chair scraped backward. I raised a hand toward him without turning. He stopped.

I looked straight at Red. “You think I planned that?”

Red picked up his glass. “I think you're young enough to tell the truth when it pays.”

I kept my hand raised until Tank sat back. “Here's some for free. Send another man after her, I'll come without an invitation.”

Red wiped his mouth. He was thirty-six; I'd seen that in a business profile. A waiter older than him approached with more juice. Red lifted a finger without looking back, and the waiter stopped where he was. Another man stood ready to clear the plate. Neither moved until Red laid down his napkin. I watched the little finger return to the table and wished I could make him ask for something instead of pointing at it.

“You can keep T's streets,” Red said. “Generous beginning.”

I looked down at my plate. “Can keep what's mine? Damn. Put a bow on it.”

Red folded his napkin. “The money comes back. Shawna comes home. We stop putting people in the ground.”

I met his eyes. “Which one you want back? T? Valentino? Dante? You got a favorite?”

The smile left his face at Dante's name.

I sat back. Pain made the movement slow enough to look deliberate. For once, the bullet contributed something.

I took his card out of my jacket and laid it beside my plate. “You wrote me a note. Finish it.”

Red looked toward the waiters. One opened the terrace door. The others followed him inside, and Red waited until the last door closed.

“Eight years ago,” Red said, “I bought something extraordinary from a man who didn't appreciate the opportunity.”

I kept my hand on the card. “What man?”

Red turned his glass slowly against the tablecloth. “Marcellus Vale. Jeweler. Violent when disappointed.”

Tank's fingers tightened around his water glass. I saw it because his wrist moved beside my plate. I looked at him, but he kept watching Red.

Red went on. “His masterpiece was called Blue Mercy. A large blue diamond. Around forty million dollars, depending on who's asking.”

I had been pretending all morning that nothing in that house could surprise me. Forty million kicked the door off that performance.

“Forty million for one rock?” I asked.

Red held up a finger. “One.”

I stared at it. “For forty million, that bitch better call when I'm lonely.”

Red lowered his hand. “Made quite a few men lonely.”

I stopped looking at his finger. “What does Dante have to do with it?”

Red pushed his plate away. “Met Vale in prison. Came home with unfortunate ideas. Decided the money in my accounts belonged to a woman he'd never met.”

I felt my jaw tighten. “What woman?”

Red looked directly at me. “Vale's dead wife.”

He let that sit between us. I thought of the watch on Shawna's wrist, the song she'd sung at my club, the picture she had put into T's coffin. Red took another drink.

“Men do peculiar things when they love dead women,” Red said. “Best avoid the habit.”

I stood, pushing the chair back with my knees. Tank rose beside me.

I leaned over the table. “Tell me what you know about Dante. Keep talking about my woman, this breakfast gets expensive.”

Red stayed in his chair. “Shawna knows the story.”

I shook my head. “No.”

Red's voice stayed soft. “Ask her.”

I felt Tank's hand near my elbow and moved it away. “I did.”

Red drew an envelope from beside his plate. “Ask again.”

He pushed it across the table. I opened it standing up. Two invitations lay inside, heavy blue paper with silver edges. Shawna's name came before mine.

“The diamond will be the centerpiece of my gala,” Red said. “Three nights from now. Thought she'd like to see what her husband died chasing.”

I looked from the printed names to his face. “Had these made before the funeral?”

Red spread a hand. “I print plenty of invitations.”

I put the cards back in their envelope. “Print one for yours.” I picked up the handwritten note too and put all of it inside my jacket.

Tank touched my elbow. I pulled away. I wanted Red to see me walking out under my own power, even if my own power was presently borrowing against tomorrow's ability to stand.

At the terrace door, Red spoke behind me.

“Tell her I still have the piano,” Red called.

I turned. He had picked up his fork again.

Red looked at me over it. “The white one. She'll know.”

I went back across the terrace. Tank said my name once. I kept walking until I reached Red's vase. I pulled one rose halfway out, caught its stem between my fingers, and lifted the heavy crystal with my other hand.

Water ran over my wrist. I swung the vase over the railing and let it go. The rose stayed in my fist with its stem snapped crooked.

For a moment, nothing happened. Then glass broke below us and a woman screamed.

Red got out of his chair and came to the railing. He looked down. I watched his cream jacket tighten across his shoulders as he gripped the stone edge.

“That's my fucking car,” Red said.

I passed his chair on the way to the door. “Tell it I still have the vase.”

I laid the wet, broken rose stem on his plate and left the flower beside his eggs.

Downstairs, his red Aston Martin had a hole in the roof.

The valet stood beside it holding a towel. He dabbed one patch of water on the door, looked at the hole in the roof, and stopped. Rose petals clung to the glass. A piece of crystal large enough to be furniture sat where the driver would have put his head.

“Mr. Red's going to be upset,” the valet told me.

I looked up toward the terrace. “Got a head start. Where's mine?”

Tank went to our Mercedes. Its windows had been replaced, the body repaired and polished between the attacks. I could see myself approaching in the door, a little bent and trying not to show it. I loved that car. Every time somebody fixed it, it looked willing to meet me again.

A man came out from the next hotel carrying a motorcycle helmet. He stopped when he saw the Aston Martin, then turned toward me.

“You Aiden?” the man asked.

I paused beside my door. “Depends who's hoping.”

He came closer, grinning. “You flew over a boat.”

I glanced at Tank. “Word getting around.”

The man introduced himself as Saint, then corrected it to Little Saint. Thirty-one, he told me, with a scar running down his chin and a silver eagle spread across the back of his leather jacket. He pointed toward a black motorcycle parked beyond the valet stand.

“I ride,” Little Saint said.

I looked at the bike. “Good for you.”

Little Saint shook his head, still smiling. “I mean I ride. Thought you ought to know.”

Tank opened my door. “Everybody got something he ought to know today.”

Little Saint looked past us. The smile left his face.

“Then know those ain't valets,” Little Saint said.

A gray sports car had stopped sideways across the exit. Behind it, a black SUV rolled toward the entrance with its windows lowering. The front passenger held a pistol against his chest. I could see no badge, no hotel uniform, nothing that told me which dead man or living man had purchased his morning.

Tank put a hand against my shoulder. “Inside.”

The first bullet struck the Mercedes's open door while his other hand was still on it. A second shattered the window above his fingers.

I caught the valet's collar and pulled him behind the car. Glass broke in the revolving entrance behind us. People dropped behind luggage carts and planters. Little Saint ran toward his motorcycle while Tank fired from beside my shoulder.

I folded myself into the passenger seat. Tank came around the hood, dropped behind the wheel, and put the car in reverse before his door had shut. The open door swung hard as we backed away from the gray car blocking the exit. He caught the door and hauled it closed. I pulled mine shut and braced my hand against the dash.

Tank stopped, turned the wheel toward the gap beside the gray car, and drove forward into its front corner. It shifted but didn't clear the exit. Its driver shouted something I couldn't hear through the glass. Tank backed up far enough to hit him again, then forced us through the wider gap before the man could close it.

Metal scraped down my door. Behind us, I caught a glimpse of Little Saint fastening his helmet strap as he kicked the motorcycle upright.

“Somebody really hates breakfast,” I told Tank.

Tank turned the wheel. “Head down.”

I slid lower in the seat. “Need it where I can see.”

Tank looked past the mirror. “See lower.”

The gray car followed with a loose fender beating against one wheel. The SUV came behind it. A shot struck the back of the Mercedes. Another took out the rear glass. I heard the pieces hit the seat behind my head and crouched until I could see the following cars through the mirror without sitting upright.

Tank swung around the first corner. A taxi braked across our path. He missed its front bumper by inches and put us into a narrow street lined with delivery trucks. The gray car made the turn. Behind it, the SUV clipped a streetlight. I saw the pole bend in the mirror and heard the metal strike pavement as the vehicle kept coming.

Little Saint appeared beside my window on the motorcycle. His visor was up. He pointed ahead, laughing loudly enough for me to hear him over the engines. I couldn't tell what he found funniest. I was mostly concerned with the tour bus blocking the next intersection.

Its driver stood outside arguing with a man in a yellow vest. Neither looked toward us until I hit the horn.

“Tank!” I shouted.

Tank's foot was already on the brake. “I see it!”

I caught the handle above my door. “Keep hearing that right before we hit something!”

The Mercedes slid. The gray car couldn't stop behind us. It struck our rear quarter and swung us sideways, turning my window toward its front bumper. For a moment I looked directly at its driver. His hands climbed over each other on the wheel as he tried to turn away.

I drew my pistol and fired through the broken side glass. His windshield starred white. He ducked, and the gray car ran into the bus's open luggage compartment with its whole nose.

The bus driver abandoned his argument and ran, still holding up one finger at the man in the yellow vest. Apparently they weren't finished. I could see one pink suitcase jumping inside the torn compartment while Tank found the gap beside the bus and drove us into it. Our door scraped along the bus panels. I held my arm in and kept the gun pointed down until we emerged onto the open road.

I secured the pistol in my waistband. Little Saint was beside us again. He raised a fist. I raised mine back.

Then the SUV hit his rear wheel.

The motorcycle snapped sideways. Little Saint came off it, rolled across the hood of a parked car, and fell into the street. His bike slid ahead in a shower of sparks. I saw his helmet strike the pavement before he got an arm beneath him.

Tank swore and braked. I opened my door while we were still slowing.

“Where the fuck are you going?” Tank shouted.

I was already outside. “Get him!”

The SUV turned toward Little Saint. I ran across the road and reached him as he pushed himself onto one elbow. His visor had cracked. Blood ran from beneath the helmet down his cheek. He looked up at me, then at the car coming toward us.

“Your leg?” I asked him.

Little Saint pulled one knee beneath him. “Works.”

I put his arm over my shoulders. “Work it with me.”

We stumbled toward the parked trucks. His first step nearly took us both down. I tightened my hold, felt the wound in my side pull, and kept moving. The SUV's engine grew louder behind us.

Our Mercedes shot across its path.

The crash sounded close enough to happen inside my head. I pushed Little Saint behind a delivery truck and turned. The SUV had stopped against Tank's car. Steam or smoke rose between the hoods. I didn't care which until I could see Tank move.

His door opened. He got out, crossed to the SUV's driver, and caught the man's door as it began to swing toward him. Tank slammed it shut again. The driver folded inward. Tank reached through the window, broken in the crash, and hauled him out over its lower edge.

I drew my pistol again and moved far enough past the truck to cover the passenger. He was still holding his gun, looking from me to Tank with his mouth open.

“Keep breathing,” I told the passenger. “That's all you getting today.”

He dropped the gun into the footwell and put both hands on the dashboard. I stayed where I could see them while Tank held the driver against the SUV.

Little Saint came out from behind the truck. His jacket was torn at the shoulder. He lifted his helmet off carefully and looked at me with blood drying along his jaw.

“You came back,” Little Saint said.

I kept one eye on the passenger. “Told me you ride. Ain't seen enough yet.”

Little Saint looked down for a moment. When he raised his face, the grin came slowly.

“Any road,” Little Saint told me. “Any fucking road, Aiden. You call, I'm there.”

A crowd had gathered by the corner. A woman held up her phone and called my name, asking me to look toward her. I turned enough to give her my good side. If Vegas intended to put every mistake I made on a screen, I could at least participate in the lighting.

Knox's officers arrived before the cars stopped smoking. One took charge of the passenger while another cuffed the driver Tank was holding. A second patrol stopped farther down the street beside the sports car jammed into the bus. I watched an officer pull its driver clear of the wreck. Then I lowered my gun and stepped back. A young officer looked from the SUV to me and shook his head.

“Chief said you were something,” the officer told me.

I leaned against the truck. “Chief been paying attention.”

Tank called for a car to take Little Saint. I waited until it arrived and watched him lower himself into the back seat, his damaged helmet in his lap. He raised two fingers before the door closed.

Our Mercedes still ran. Tank examined the door that wouldn't latch, took off his belt, and tied it shut from inside. I put my pistol away and climbed in on the other side. People watched us leave, one man jogging a few steps after us before running out of things to shout.

Before we reached the highway, I called Shawna.

“Baby, you want lunch?” I asked.

Shawna's voice came back sharp and clear. “Why there sirens behind you?”

I looked at Tank's belt holding his door. “They wanted breakfast. I'm thinking ahead.”

At home, Shawna stood barefoot on a little fitting platform beside the piano while a woman pinned a silver dress around her hips. Rosa sat beside a tray of sewing supplies, holding a strip of ribbon against the fabric. The afternoon light touched the dress every time Shawna moved, and for a moment I wanted everyone else out of the room so I could look at her without sharing the view.

Then I saw the watch.

Dante's watch. Dante's wife. A forty-million-dollar stone connected to Dante that she hadn't mentioned. I had driven home carrying Red's account of him, and suddenly every beautiful thing in my house seemed to have arrived through somebody else's life first.

Rosa looked up. “Why you got that face?”

I stopped in the doorway. “What face?”

Rosa lowered the ribbon and pointed at me. “The one gonna make this dress cost more.”

I took the invitations from my jacket. “Blue Mercy.”

Shawna stopped moving. The seamstress kept working until Rosa touched her shoulder.

“Let's get coffee,” Rosa told the woman.

The seamstress looked at the pins still in the dress. “I don't drink coffee.”

Rosa helped her to her feet. “Good fucking day to start.”

She took the tray so the woman wouldn't have to reach back for anything. They left through the side door. Shawna stepped off the platform, holding the skirt clear of her feet. A pin fell onto the floor between us. I heard its little click and hated how quiet the room had become.

“What did he tell you?” Shawna asked.

I laughed once. It sounded ugly even to me.

“Everybody asks that,” I told her. “What she tell you? What he tell you? What the dead man tell the other dead man? How about you tell me something?”

Shawna drew back a little. I saw it and knew I ought to stop long enough to change the way I was speaking. I didn't.

I held up the blue cards. “You knew about forty million?”

Shawna looked at them. “I knew about the stone.”

I let my hand fall. “Didn't think it mattered?”

Shawna took a breath. “I thought you being shot mattered. Thought getting you through yesterday mattered.”

I glanced at the silver cloth gathered in her hand. “Time to pick a dress, though.”

She let the skirt go. It fell around her ankles.

“I picked it because I wanted one night,” Shawna said, “where you looked at me and didn't see somebody else bleeding.”

I looked at the dress properly then. Pins caught the light along one side. She stood barefoot in the middle of the room while I held the price of a diamond between us as if that were the only thing worth asking about.

I had no answer ready.

Shawna came close and took the invitation from my hand. Her thumb moved across Red's printed name.

“He tell you about his piano?” Shawna asked.

I looked toward our own black piano by the water. “Told me. Tell everybody?”

Shawna shook her head. “I smashed the keys with a bottle the night I left. He bought another one. Now he can act like I'm coming back to finish the song.”

I remembered Red standing beside the white piano, waiting for me to look at the photograph. Remembered his voice telling me what he still had. I had let him put a whole relationship into one piece of furniture, and she had already broken the fucking thing.

I looked back at Shawna. “He said you knew what Dante died for.”

She held the invitation against her thigh. “I know what Dante told me.”

I rested a hand on the back of a chair. “Tell me.”

Shawna glanced at the chair. “Starts before me.”

I couldn't keep the bitterness out. “Everything in this house starts before me.”

Her eyes lowered to the cross visible through my open jacket.

“Want me to take that back?” Shawna asked.

My hand closed around it before I answered. “No.”

Shawna touched the chair beside me. “Then sit down, Aiden.”

I sat. The relief in my side made it impossible to pretend I hadn't needed to. Shawna picked up the fallen pin and laid it on the piano before pulling another chair close. She arranged the unfinished dress over her knees, keeping the pins away from me.

“Dante told me after we got married,” Shawna said. “We were in the room Red paid for. I thought we'd go downstairs and drink with everybody. He locked the door.”

My jaw tightened. I made myself loosen my hand around the cross.

“I'm listening,” I told her.

Shawna took my hand off the chain and held it between hers.

“He said there was something I ought to know,” she told me. “Before I decided what kind of man I'd married.”

She looked down at our hands, then back at me.

“Then he told me about the old man,” Shawna said.

## 7. The Man Who Cut the Heart Out of a Mountain

Before Shawna got much farther, my body interrupted her.

I shifted in the chair and couldn't keep the pain off my face. Shawna called Rosa back. Tank took one look at me and put a hand beneath my arm. Between the three of them, they got me upstairs before I could make sitting down difficult for anybody else.

Tank draped my coat over a chair and helped me onto the bed. Rosa took Shawna into the bathroom. I heard a pin strike the sink, then Shawna asking how many were still in the dress.

“Enough to keep you standing still,” Rosa answered through the door.

Rosa emerged carrying the silver dress, and Shawna came back in a robe. By then I was propped against the pillows with one leg straight and the other bent because apparently that was the arrangement under which my ribs would permit information.

Shawna put the blue invitations on the bedside table and sat beside me. Dante's watch rested loose against her hand.

She told me their wedding room had rose petals all over the bed. Dante had begun gathering them into the ice bucket as soon as he locked the door.

“I asked if they were bothering him,” Shawna said. “He said they had dirt on them.”

She smiled a little at the memory, then repeated his answer in her own quiet voice: Dante hadn't wanted her sleeping in dirt. She had laughed and kicked off her shoes. According to her, he watched the shoes land as though he hadn't quite believed she was going to stay.

That fucked with me more than the picture of the wedding bed. I had looked at things she set down in my house the same way. The watch beside a lamp. A bag left open on a chair. Little proofs that she might mean to be there in the morning.

I asked her to go on.

Dante had sat beside the ice bucket and told her about prison. Nine months of his life, a little more than three years before the afternoon when I heard the story. He had been twenty-eight. Shawna made a point of giving me the age, as if she wanted me to see a man then rather than only the one she had buried.

His first afternoon, he saw another prisoner spill soup down an older man's shirt.

The older man looked down at the stain and kept eating. The man who'd done it stayed beside his table. Dante told Shawna the silence bothered him before the violence did: a whole room carrying on as if everyone already knew which person was allowed to do this to which other person.

Shawna described the bully leaning over the older prisoner's bowl, telling him to look up. When the old man finally put down his spoon, he pointed to the bully's hand.

“You have my soup on your hand,” Shawna quoted him.

The man slapped him.

Shawna drew a breath. “Dante said he got up. Next thing he remembered, he had the man against the wall and blood on his shirt. He was telling somebody to bring the old man another bowl.”

I looked at her. “What happened between those things?”

Shawna gestured from one end of the bed to the other. “I told you what happened.”

I shook my head. “Told me somebody got hit. Skipped where the nigga found out.”

She stared at me for a moment. Then Shawna settled back against the pillows and pulled her robe clear of her knees.

“The man had two friends,” she told me.

I made myself comfortable enough to pay the proper attention. “There we go.”

One friend moved into the aisle when Dante crossed between the tables. Dante described him to Shawna as a big, shaved-headed man of forty, with a towel over his shoulder. The man planted himself in Dante's way and ordered him to sit down.

Dante kept coming. The man asked whether he was deaf.

“Dante told him no,” Shawna said. “Then asked if he was finished.”

The man swung. His fist caught Dante across the jaw. Dante still had his food tray in both hands, and he drove its edge into the man's face. The man folded over the end of a bench with both hands against his nose. Food flew across the aisle. A cup fell beneath somebody's shoe. Men stood around them, benches scraping the floor.

The soup-spiller grabbed the older man's collar and pulled him halfway off the seat. Now the table lay between Dante and the two of them. Shawna held one hand out to show me the distance, then lifted it across the bed.

“He went over it,” she said.

In my head, I saw his feet leave the floor. I knew that stupid certainty: once you decided you were getting to somebody, the furniture could work out its feelings afterward.

I didn't enjoy finding that much of myself in her husband. I listened anyway.

Dante hit the man holding the prisoner with both knees. They went down together. The older man fell against the bench, steadied himself, and reached beneath the table for his spoon.

“He got the spoon?” I asked Shawna.

She nodded. “He liked that spoon.”

I rested my hand over the cross on my chest. “Respect an attachment.”

The last man came behind Dante with a chair raised. According to Dante's account, the older prisoner put his foot against the man's ankle as he passed. The man's step broke. His chair struck the table instead of Dante's head, and Dante turned into him before he could lift it again.

Shawna showed me how Dante said he'd caught the chair. One hand against its back, the other closing where a leg met the seat. He tore it sideways. The man held on, so Dante drove it back into his ribs and shoved him against the table. Dante had broken a finger somewhere in the fight. He hadn't known which movement did it until the guards were holding him and it began to hurt.

The man who'd spilled the soup tried to rise. Dante hauled him up the rest of the way and put him against the wall. That was the part Shawna had given me first: blood on Dante's shirt, his hand at the man's collar, his voice carrying across the room asking for another fucking bowl.

The guards arrived. Dante let the bully drop. Three men were on or near the floor, and the old prisoner stood by his table with the spoon in his hand.

Shawna told me a guard ordered Dante toward the door.

“He said the old man hadn't eaten,” she said. “The guard told him to move again.”

The older prisoner touched Dante's shoulder and said he could wait. Dante looked at the empty bowl, picked it up, and put it into the guard's hand.

“So can they,” Shawna quoted Dante, nodding toward the place she'd used to indicate the men on the floor.

She smiled after saying it. I let her have the smile. Her dead man had earned that much.

I held up three fingers. “Three.”

Shawna looked at my hand. “Three.”

I lowered it. “I had twelve.”

She tapped my good shoulder with a pillow, gently enough that the bullet declined to comment.

“You asked for the story,” Shawna reminded me.

I caught the pillow before she could put it back. “Can listen and hold the record.”

She laughed. I put the pillow behind her, and when she leaned against it, her shoulder rested against mine. For a moment, hearing about Dante didn't feel like being pushed out of my own bed.

The fight had bought him punishment and a friendship.

The old man's name was Marcellus Vale.

When Dante returned, Marcellus sat on his bunk with a little paper box. Inside were a broken comb, some buttons, and a gold-colored wrapper folded into a heart. He offered Dante the heart for his trouble.

Shawna made the shape between her fingers. “Dante asked what he was supposed to do with it. Marcellus told him to give it to somebody.”

Dante had asked whether he meant a woman. Marcellus said that depended on whether Dante liked women. Dante assured him that he did.

Shawna lifted her chin slightly as she repeated the old man's answer. “Then perhaps one will like a heart.”

I looked at her hands. I could picture the little folded wrapper more easily than forty million dollars. Somebody confined to a room had still found a way to make a gift with what the room allowed him.

The folds were so fine that Dante asked whether Marcellus made jewelry. Marcellus said he used to. Dante admitted that he used to steal it.

“Then we may have met professionally,” Shawna quoted Marcellus.

Dante laughed. The old man shifted along the bunk so he could sit down.

They ate together for two months. Marcellus taught him cards. Dante offered advice about which men in the prison were dangerous and which ones mainly needed other people to look frightened. Shawna said Marcellus listened, nodded, and sometimes greeted those same men by name when they passed. I asked her how long he'd already been inside.

“Years,” she told me.

I shook my head at the ceiling. “Dante gave him the tour anyway?”

Shawna rested her head against the pillow. “You never told somebody something they already knew?”

I looked back at her. “Not in the last few seconds.”

She went on before I could improve that record.

At night, Dante saw Marcellus holding a photograph where the corridor light reached his bunk. The picture showed a woman on a little stage, singing with her eyes closed. A blue stone rested at the base of her throat.

Celeste. His wife.

When Dante said she was beautiful, Marcellus said he knew. When Dante asked whether she still visited, Marcellus turned the photograph over and answered no.

Dante left it alone until a letter arrived. Marcellus carried the unopened envelope through dinner. Dante offered to read it for him, but the old man said he already knew what it said.

Shawna glanced toward the invitations on my bedside table. “Dante asked why they sent it, then.”

She gave me Marcellus's answer slowly: some people thought they could finish a love affair by closing a bank account.

Celeste had died three months earlier. Dante learned that sitting on the opposite bunk, looking at an envelope the old man couldn't bring himself to open. It wasn't the letter from Celeste that I would read later. It was one more official piece of business arriving after the person who might have made it bearable was gone.

After a while, Dante asked him about the stone in the photograph.

What came next was Marcellus's account, told to Dante in that cell and repeated to Shawna in their wedding room. She kept his watch between her hands while she passed it on to me.

The man who sold Marcellus the rough stone brought it in an empty cigarette packet. Marcellus paid enough to empty his account. Then two men stopped him on the way out, one with a hand extended and the other blocking the doorway.

Shawna told me how Marcellus had buttoned his coat over the packet. The first man claimed a share. Marcellus told him that if he put his hand inside the coat, he'd need help using it afterward.

“He put it away,” Shawna said. “The other one hit him.”

The punch caught Marcellus in the jaw. He struck the doorframe, kept one hand flat over the packet, and drove his free elbow into the man's chest. Then he shoved him into his companion. Both hit the wall. Marcellus pulled his sleeve free and left with the stone and blood at the corner of his mouth.

He had given Dante all of that. The part he hadn't been eager to reach, Shawna told me, was the conversation waiting in his kitchen.

Celeste was frying chicken. According to the story Marcellus told Dante, she turned down the burner when she saw his face and asked how much he'd spent.

He put the cigarette packet on the kitchen table and asked her to look first.

Shawna held her hand flat as if setting it down herself. “She asked him again. How much, Marcellus?”

Then Celeste opened the packet. Marcellus described the rough blue surface to Dante as something that looked as if a piece of sky had been buried in dirt. I had only Red's price in my head until Shawna told me that. The little packet and the chicken pan made the number feel absurd in a different way.

Celeste looked at the stone, then at her husband.

“We are not eating that,” Shawna quoted her.

Marcellus held it toward the window. Light passed through and laid a blue shape across Celeste's white sleeve. She stopped talking. He remembered that she said only one little word when she saw it: oh.

He watched her instead of the stone. That was what he later told Dante. It mattered to me that he'd once known where to look without being reminded.

Cutting the diamond took months. Marcellus worked in a room behind the house. Celeste sang at a lounge that barely paid her and brought him food between sets and errands. He told Dante about finding meals cold beside his tools because he'd forgotten she had come in.

At the end of the first month, she brought his pillow to the workshop.

At the end of the second, she put a photograph of herself beside his lamp.

Shawna reached toward our bedside lamp as she gave me Celeste's explanation. “So you remember who the fuck lives out here.”

Marcellus reached for her. Celeste put his dinner in that hand and went back into the house.

I laughed before I meant to. Shawna looked back at me, and I put my hand down from the cross. I had been holding it again.

At the end of the third month, Marcellus put on his good suit and went to the lounge.

Shawna described him walking toward the stage with a small box in his hands. Celeste was halfway through a song. The pianist saw him coming and lost his place. Celeste turned, saw her husband, and spoke into the microphone.

“Don't propose again,” Shawna quoted Celeste. “I fell for that shit once.”

People laughed. Marcellus reached the steps and told her he'd made something for her. She came down to him. He opened the box.

On their wedding night, Shawna said, Dante had held her hand beneath a lamp, moving it in and out of the light while he tried to show her the blue Marcellus had described. The color deepened in the shade of his hand. It looked wet even when you knew it wasn't. Now I watched the light on her fingers while she told me. I wanted to touch them, and I waited until she had finished showing me.

Celeste put her fingers over her mouth. Marcellus lifted out the necklace and asked her to turn around. He fastened it behind her neck. Small pale stones surrounded the blue one, making it appear darker against her skin.

Shawna touched the base of her own throat. “She asked what he called it.”

Marcellus answered Blue Mercy. Celeste asked what needed mercy.

“The last three months,” Shawna quoted him.

She told me Celeste put her arms around him and kissed him in front of the room. The pianist found his place and started playing again. I pictured Marcellus still holding the empty box behind her back, unable to put it down because she had both his arms occupied.

Red had been in the lounge with Valentino. Twenty-eight then. Marcellus identified both men in the story he told Dante. I wondered whether they'd clapped along with the room. Knowing what followed made that possibility harder to stomach.

I wanted to interrupt Shawna. I wanted to ask whether Dante had kissed better, promised more, understood faster. Those were the questions rising in me while she told me about a man who had spent months forgetting his wife for a stone.

I kept my mouth shut.

For once, I wanted to hear what happened more than I wanted to hear myself.

Two weeks after the lounge, Red came to Marcellus's workshop with T and a bottle of wine. Shawna said Marcellus had claimed the wine cost more than his first car. Red asked to buy the diamond. Marcellus refused.

Red named more money. Marcellus told him the necklace belonged to his wife.

“Then ask your wife,” Shawna quoted Red.

She paused before giving me Marcellus's answer. “She isn't for sale either.”

T laughed. Red left the wine behind when they went.

Celeste read the label and carried the bottle to the sink. Marcellus caught her wrist and asked whether she knew what it was worth. She pulled free and poured it out.

Shawna looked at me while repeating Celeste's words. “That's why I don't want to find out what we owe him if we drink it.”

But a necklace worth a fortune didn't pay the power bill when nobody wanted to sell it. Valentino offered to arrange a private showing. There were clients who wanted work in a similar blue setting, men who wanted Marcellus to examine stones they owned, people willing to pay for his hands rather than his wife's necklace.

Marcellus agreed. He told Celeste there would be enough work to stop worrying.

“She asked whether he'd ever have enough work to stop,” Shawna told me.

Before the showing, he made a second necklace. This mattered, and I asked Shawna to slow down while she explained it. A lesser blue stone, convincing under warm light, set in an identical necklace. It could sit in the display while Celeste wore the true diamond.

Marcellus marked the removable mounting in a hidden place. The real diamond also carried a tiny irregularity inside it, something he hadn't made.

I looked at Shawna. “Find his mark, find the stone?”

She shook her head. “The mounting comes off. Could put either stone in it. Dante said you have to look inside the diamond.”

I nodded. “The little flaw.”

“That one,” Shawna said. “Marcellus knew exactly where it was.”

It didn't save either necklace. The men who came took both.

They took the loose jewels from the back room too, and Marcellus's book of customers. T held him against the workbench. Valentino removed the necklace from Celeste's throat carefully enough that, in the account Marcellus gave Dante, her hair stayed where she'd pinned it.

Red stood in the doorway while the others did the work.

“Perhaps now we can agree on a price,” Shawna quoted him.

Marcellus lunged toward Red. T hit him. The lamp fell from the bench, and Marcellus went down beside it.

Celeste dropped to her knees. She told Red to take the stone and leave. Red said she should have agreed in the beginning, then put an envelope on the bench. A fraction of what he'd offered. Enough paper money to hold up afterward and call payment.

I pushed the blankets aside and got out of bed. Shawna stopped speaking until I reached the window. I rested one hand against the frame and told her to keep going.

Marcellus had stayed on the floor after the men left. Celeste pressed a towel to his face and kept asking him to look at her.

“He told Dante he couldn't stop looking at her throat,” Shawna said behind me.

I stared at the lake. I could hear her shift on the mattress.

Marcellus had been alive on the floor, with Celeste alive beside him. The necklace was gone, and he had kept looking at the empty place on her throat while she tried to help him. I hated how easily I could imagine doing the same thing with different names in the room.

Marcellus went after it.

He wore the same good suit he'd worn to give the necklace to her. When Shawna told me that, I looked down at my own coat, thrown over a chair with the diamond pin still fastened above the hole.

I turned halfway toward the bed. “He told Dante what he wore?”

Shawna nodded. “Said Celeste tried to stop him.”

I leaned against the window frame. “With words?”

Shawna lifted one hand. “His car keys.”

I nodded toward her. “Smart woman.”

He took a taxi. A gun in his coat, Red's envelope of money on his knee. He told Dante he looked back and saw Celeste in the doorway holding the useless keys. She didn't shout after him. He remembered that silence long afterward.

Valentino's club had gold doors and a stairway lit from beneath. Marcellus recognized the singer onstage. She stopped singing when she saw him. He crossed to Valentino's booth, where two men sat beside him, and put the envelope on the table.

Shawna repeated Marcellus's words: he had brought back their money. He had come for what was his.

One of Valentino's men stood. Marcellus hit him with the pistol before he got upright. The man fell across the table, knocking a champagne bottle sideways. The other reached beneath his jacket. Marcellus shot him.

The bullet struck the man's shoulder. He lived; Shawna told me Marcellus had heard that repeated through the case afterward. Valentino dropped behind the booth and disappeared through a door near the bar while the room emptied around them.

Marcellus followed him into a corridor, then through another door into a room full of jewelry under bright lights. Watches and rings lay in open boxes. Some carried names Marcellus knew. His own tools sat on a table.

Shawna described him picking one up and finding his initials on it. He had engraved them when he first bought the tool. He turned it over in his hand while the gun remained in the other.

The diamond wasn't there.

Two men came through the door behind him. A third appeared at the far end of the room. Marcellus turned and asked where the stone was. Someone hit him behind the ear. He struck the table, and rings scattered across the tiles. Hands caught his wrist before he could bring the pistol around. Another blow folded him forward. He fell among the jewelry.

Shawna's voice had become slower. I watched her in the window's reflection. She was looking down at the watch while she described what Dante had told her, and I knew she was passing along something she had once heard in a wedding dress.

When the police arrived, Marcellus could hear Valentino in the doorway explaining how dangerous grief had made him. Marcellus tried to rise. The men holding him pressed him down, then carried him out through the club.

The taxi was still outside. According to Marcellus, its driver held up the meter when the police brought him through the gold doors. He pointed at the fare, then at his car, and followed them halfway to the cruiser before anybody answered him.

I let out a breath that wasn't quite a laugh. The meter had survived the whole fucking expedition.

Celeste came to the station wearing blue slippers. One heel was crushed beneath her foot. Marcellus told Dante he saw the slippers before he looked at her face.

He told her he hadn't found the necklace.

“I found you,” Shawna quoted Celeste.

I looked back at the lake.

Marcellus hadn't answered. He told Dante he kept returning to that moment in his mind, trying to arrive with something better to say. I could imagine the cold chair, her slippers, all the time in the world afterward to find a sentence he needed right then.

“Red has to die,” I told Shawna.

She came to the window and took my hand. “Keep listening.”

I held on to her fingers. “I am. That's what the listening's doing.”

Marcellus went to prison. Red opened the Saint with money raised against a diamond he claimed to have bought from a respected jeweler. Marcellus learned that from a magazine left in the visiting room. Shawna told me he'd remembered the photograph of the new hotel almost as sharply as he remembered his own workshop.

Celeste visited while she could. At first, she wore bright dresses and brought stories from the lounge. A pianist had arrived in two different shoes. A couple had argued through an entire song, then both requested it again. If Marcellus interrupted to ask who'd been seen with the necklace, she pushed the candy she'd brought closer to him and told him to eat.

Later, she came less often. She was sick. Marcellus admitted to Dante that he'd believed the missed visits were punishment until they argued about a doctor he hadn't known she was seeing and money he couldn't send. Hearing Shawna repeat that admission, I looked at the watch on her wrist and kept my hand open around hers.

On Celeste's final visit, she wore earrings Marcellus had made before either of them knew Red. She asked him to remember the first thing he'd given her.

He guessed the earrings, then a bracelet. Celeste told him no. He kept trying to name an object.

“A ride,” Shawna quoted her. “I was standing in the rain. You drove me home. You didn't ask for my number till we got there.”

Marcellus said he remembered.

Shawna's fingers tightened around mine as she gave me Celeste's reply. “Then remember that man too.”

Celeste put her hand against the visiting-room glass.

After she died, a letter came among her things. Marcellus carried it until the folds began to split. One night he read it to Dante. Dante wrote the words down after his release because, Shawna told me, he was afraid of losing them. He read them to her on their wedding night.

Shawna could give me parts from memory. Later that same day, Marcellus let me read the worn letter itself. I remember Celeste's words in full, including the ones Shawna couldn't finish saying.

*My love,*

*You think I never understood that stone. I did. I understood every hour you spent getting it right. I know the little words you hid beneath the blue mounting. I know you were waiting for me to find them, and I know you were disappointed when I liked the front so much I forgot to look behind it.*

*I found them the night you fell asleep with your shoes on. I was angry with you for missing supper. I took the necklace off and nearly dropped it, and there you were underneath, saying what you used to say at my door.*

*I came for you.*

*I wore those words against me every night afterward. You never asked whether I'd found them. You just watched me wear it and smiled.*

*I am so tired now, Marcellus. I can't keep driving out there. Don't let anybody tell you I stopped because I stopped loving you. I am stopping because I am sick, and I want the strength I have left for things that don't require me to pass through a locked door.*

*If you get your beautiful stone back, put it down before you touch somebody you love. I know your hands. They are warmer without all that metal in them.*

*And if you are still angry when you come home, be angry. You always were. Just open the curtains. Pay the piano man what I owe him. There is chicken in the freezer because I never learned how to cook for one.*

*I would take the ride again.*

*Celeste.*

At the window, Shawna fell quiet after telling me the last part she remembered. I stayed facing the water until I could trust my face. My hand lay in hers. I was grateful she didn't pull it away to make me look at her.

“What did Dante do?” I asked.

Shawna stood close to my shoulder. “Promised he'd get it back.”

I watched a little boat moving out on the lake. “For a man he met in prison?”

Shawna followed my gaze. “For him. For her. For whatever he thought that would make him.”

I turned toward her. “And you?”

Her voice was quiet. “I thought I married the kind of man who'd do that.”

I nodded. I didn't like Dante better. I just had a harder time hating him in a way that left me looking good.

Shawna led me back to the bed. I sat, and she remained beside me, holding the watch between us while it was still fastened to her wrist.

“Dante took the three million first,” she told me. “Wanted a lawyer for Marcellus. Wanted a way out for us when Red found out what else he was after. He thought T would choose him.”

She looked down at the metal. “His brother.”

I kept my voice low. “He didn't.”

Shawna shook her head. “No.”

She turned the watch over until the clasp faced upward.

“Last night Dante came home,” Shawna said, “he asked if it still looked too big on me. I told him everything he owned was too big. He said he'd put something in it that fit.”

I looked from the clasp to her face. “What something?”

Shawna's fingers paused against the catch. “Thought he meant himself.”

Then she opened it. Her hand was shaking, and I slid my palm beneath the watch so it wouldn't fall when it came free.

For the first time since I'd met her, I wanted to see what her dead husband had left inside.

## 8. Let Him Think You Love Him

Jealousy discovered the seam.

I would like to offer a finer explanation. An eye for craftsmanship. Unusual powers of observation. But I had been staring at Dante's watch every time it touched Shawna's face, her pillow, the hand she laid on my knee. She had missed him for eight months. Since taking the watch from Valentino's glove compartment the night she killed him, she'd carried a piece of him everywhere she went. I had studied the motherfucker as if it might eventually apologize.

When its clasp shifted beneath her thumbnail, I saw a narrow line move where no line should have moved.

I caught her hand before she closed it. “Wait. Something there.”

Shawna held the watch beneath the bedside lamp. “Where?”

I pointed at the inner edge. “That bit. It moved.”

She let me turn it once between my fingers. “You know how to open it?”

I examined the seam from another angle. “No.”

Shawna held her palm out. “Then give it back before you become the next thing I hit with it.”

I returned the watch. “Make a discovery, museum throws me out.”

She called Rosa and put the phone on speaker between us. Rosa answered from elsewhere in the house, sounding as if we'd interrupted a much better conversation. Shawna asked for a jeweler she trusted.

“Trusted to do what?” Rosa asked through the phone. “Those are different men.”

Shawna turned the watch over in her palm. “Open this without breaking it.”

Rosa's voice brightened. “Got that nigga. Owes me money.”

I leaned toward the phone. “Makes him trustworthy?”

Rosa answered immediately. “Makes him findable. You want reliable or you want found?”

I looked at Shawna. “Send the findable one.”

An hour later we sat downstairs with the jeweler at our table. He had laid a cloth beneath the watch and opened a small case of tools. I watched him bring the clasp close to a loupe held against his eye. When I reached toward it, Shawna slid the cloth farther away from me without turning her head.

The jeweler lowered his hands. “Who built this?”

Shawna kept her hand at the edge of the cloth. “Marcellus Vale. Dante told me he worked on it.”

The jeweler took the loupe from his eye. “You know where he is?”

Shawna looked at me, then back at him. “Prison.”

The jeweler shook his head. “Got out nine days ago. Came asking about his old bench.”

Shawna grabbed my forearm. I put my hand over hers and leaned toward the man.

“Call him,” I said.

The jeweler reached into his pocket. “I'm not—”

I pointed at the phone emerging in his hand. “Then call him whatever you are. Call the fucking man.”

The jeweler paused with the screen lit. “Not certain he answers this number. What I was going to say.”

Shawna turned her head slowly toward me.

I sat back. “Your nigga's excited.”

Shawna returned her attention to the phone. “Your nigga's letting him dial.”

The jeweler made the call. I heard only his side at first: his name, a reminder of the visit to his shop, a question about a watch. Then Shawna held her hand out. He gave her the phone, and she told the man at the other end who she was.

Marcellus's answer came loudly enough for me to hear across the table.

“Dante's wife?” he asked through the phone.

Shawna covered her mouth and nodded. I touched her wrist. She blinked at me, then spoke so he could hear her too.

“Yes,” Shawna told him.

I kept quiet while she gave him the address and explained why we needed him. My part was to hold the hand she wasn't using. For once, I managed to leave it at that.

He arrived before sunset.

I saw him from the front steps: a tall, narrow man in a brown coat too heavy for the weather, with close gray hair and a paper bag tucked under his arm. Marcellus was sixty-two, though I learned the number after I learned how stubborn he could be about a shirt.

Our gate had been left partly open for him. He was turning through it when a car stopped outside and three men got out. The tallest caught the back of his coat. Marcellus turned, the paper bag slipped, and a folded shirt fell onto the dusty edge of the drive.

I put a hand against the porch rail. My dock was still broken. My car had come home tied shut with Tank's belt. Now somebody had decided my front gate needed a fucking incident too.

“Tank!” I shouted.

He came out through the side door with bread in his mouth. When he saw the men, he took it out and set it on the rail.

Tank looked at me. “Jeweler's friends?”

I started down the steps. “Holding him like enemies.”

Shawna came out behind me. Her footsteps stopped at the top of the stairs.

“Marcellus,” Shawna said.

I looked back at her face. Then I turned toward the gate and kept going.

The tall man had pulled Marcellus against the outside of the open gate leaf. He wore a purple suit, a pale scar along one cheek, and rings on nearly every finger. He looked about thirty-five. One unadorned finger stood out from the rest as if humility had been assigned there by mistake.

He watched me approach from inside the drive.

“Go back in,” the man in purple told me.

I stopped a few feet short of him. “Came out for you. Don't make the trip disappointing.”

His eyes moved toward Tank. He shifted his grip higher on Marcellus's coat.

“Private business,” the man said.

I rested a hand on the gate's inner edge. “My private gate. Keep finding me in your problem.”

One of the other men pulled his jacket open to show a pistol. I looked down at it, then up at his face.

“Buy a second one?” I asked him. “For when that one leaves you?”

He reached for the gun. Tank hit him before it cleared the jacket, driving him backward into the car. The rear window broke behind his shoulders. His pistol struck the drive, and Tank kicked it beneath the car. The third man swung at me. I caught his shoulder, turned with him, and put his face against the stone gatepost. He dropped to one knee, both hands rising to his nose.

Pain pulled through my side. I kept one hand on the gate to stay upright.

The man in purple dragged Marcellus in front of him. His own pistol appeared against the old man's coat.

I stopped moving.

“Don't,” Shawna called from behind me.

I didn't know which of us she meant. The gun lay flat against Marcellus's chest. Marcellus looked down, past the weapon, at the shirt in the dust.

“Pick it up,” Marcellus told the man holding him.

The man blinked. “What?”

Marcellus nodded toward the ground. “My shirt.”

The man in purple stared at the side of his head. “You out your fucking mind?”

Marcellus kept looking at the shirt. “I've been out nine days. That is the only shirt I have bought.”

The man looked at me. Marcellus kept staring at the shirt. He had apparently finished discussing the gun.

I held my free hand out. “Let him get it.”

The man tightened his arm across Marcellus. “Think I'm stupid?”

I kept my hand open. “Giving you a chance to surprise me.”

He pulled Marcellus backward, away from the gate's edge. The old man's heel caught the fallen paper bag. He stumbled, and the pistol moved a few inches off his chest as the man in purple tried to keep hold of both him and the gun.

I put both palms against the gate and shoved it outward.

The bars struck the man's forearm. His gun went off into the drive, taking a white chip from the paving beside my foot. Marcellus dropped clear. I caught the gunman's wrist against the iron before he could bring it around again.

His rings dug into my hand. The bones beneath them moved like any other man's.

The pistol fell. I drove him backward against the gate a second time and let him slide down the bars. Tank had forced his first man to the ground beside the car. He pulled the man I'd put into the pillar away from me and planted him beside the other.

Shawna ran past my shoulder and knelt with Marcellus. She got her hands beneath his arms.

“You hurt?” Shawna asked him.

Marcellus looked at the torn front of his coat. “My coat.”

Shawna lifted his chin. “Fuck the coat. You hurt?”

He shook his head. Mercedes came through the gate, bent to pick up the shirt, and shook the dust from it. I hadn't seen her arrive at the house, but there she was, taking charge of the one thing the old man had demanded from an armed stranger.

Mercedes brushed the sleeves, then held it by the shoulders against the sunlight. “Little wash. Handsome again.”

Marcellus looked at her, then down at the shirt. His mouth moved as if he had begun an answer and lost it. He took the cleanest corner of the cloth between his fingers.

Mercedes folded the shirt over her arm while I turned back to the man against the bars.

“Who are you?” I asked him.

He pressed his empty hand against his stomach. “Felix.”

I waited. “Felix what?”

The man swallowed. “Barrow.”

I pointed toward Marcellus. “What do you want with him?”

Felix looked at his fallen gun. “Owes people.”

I moved my foot beside the weapon so he would have to look at me again. “Which people?”

Felix shifted against the iron. “Inside. He made things. Promises got made.”

Marcellus had got to his feet with Shawna's help. He looked at Felix and shook his head once.

“I promised him nothing,” Marcellus said.

I looked back down at Felix. “There. He ain't got your promise. You ain't got your gun. Everybody learned something.”

Felix looked past me at the house, then toward Tank and the two men lying beside the car.

“You're that Aiden,” Felix said.

I bent just enough for him to hear me comfortably. “Which one you hoping for?”

Felix's eyes came back to mine. “Boat. Hearse.”

I shook my head. “Behind already. Two boats.”

Mercedes laughed with the shirt folded over her arm. Tank laughed too, keeping his eyes on the men at his feet. Marcellus looked from them to me. The corner of his mouth moved.

Felix leaned his head back against the bars. “Should've asked where he was going.”

I straightened slowly. “Should've stayed home.”

Tank called Knox's men. I left him by the gate and helped Marcellus toward the house. Shawna held the old man's other hand. Mercedes carried his shirt and the retrieved paper bag, holding the torn bottom closed with her palm.

At the front door, Shawna hurried ahead to fetch water. I stopped beside Marcellus and offered my hand properly.

“Aiden,” I said.

Marcellus took it. “I know.”

I glanced toward the drive. “People talking?”

Marcellus looked at Shawna crossing the living room. “Dante's widow said your name six times on the telephone.”

I liked him right then. He hadn't even asked for a favor yet and had already brought useful news.

His eyes dropped to the cross at my chest. He touched it with one fingertip, following the letters.

“I did those,” Marcellus said. “Dante sent the cross with the watch. Said his wife thought he was magnificent. He wasn't arguing.”

I looked down at the inscription, then at him. “Come inside.”

Marcellus paused on the threshold. “Dante would've liked you.”

I held the door wider. “Everybody gets there. Some men take the long way.”

He smiled, small but plain enough for me to see. For the first time, I could imagine him in the kitchen Shawna had described, holding a stone toward a patch of sunlight while a woman swore at him over chicken.

“Apparently,” Marcellus said, “I came to the right house.”

Cruisers pulled up at the gate while we stood on the threshold. I looked back and saw Tank pointing out the fallen pistol to an officer. Two others brought Felix and his men to their feet and began cuffing them. I waited until all three were in police hands, then took Marcellus inside.

Shawna set the water down when we reached the living room. She crossed toward him and stopped short. Marcellus opened his arms. She put her face against his coat, and he laid one hand between her shoulders.

His eyes closed. Hers were hidden. I stood close enough to hear her crying and gave them the space between the chair and the table. Until then I'd been the man listening to a story about both of them. Now they were in my living room, and I couldn't solve what they were feeling by buying another fucking thing for it.

Through the open front door, I saw Tank take a bag of food from a delivery driver before coming back from the gate. He saw Marcellus and Shawna together and stopped, letting the bag hang by his leg.

“Who this?” Tank asked me quietly.

I looked over at him. “Marcellus.”

Tank glanced toward the front door. Marcellus opened his eyes and looked at Tank's face for a long moment.

“Your friend?” Marcellus asked me.

I nodded. “Yeah.”

Marcellus looked back at Tank. “I see.”

He didn't add anything. Tank put the food down and went outside. I watched him leave, then turned toward the table where the jeweler had kept the watch waiting on its cloth.

Marcellus sat down and asked for the loupe.

The jeweler gave him room. I stood beside Shawna and watched Marcellus turn the clasp beneath the light. His hands hardly seemed to hurry at all. After the trouble at the gate, their steadiness held my attention almost as tightly as the gun had.

Marcellus held the clasp toward the light. “Dante asked where a man could keep a letter. Back when we were inside.”

He selected a narrow tool from the jeweler's case and turned it against his thumbnail to inspect the point.

“After he got out, an old client arranged a legal repair commission,” Marcellus told us. “Watch came in with the cross. I made the space and sent them back.”

I touched the letters at my chest. His hands had been on this too, in that cell, while Dante was still making plans that required him to remain alive.

Marcellus pressed a tool into the seam. A thin cover lifted. Folded paper lay beneath it.

He set the paper on the cloth, then laid both hands flat on the table.

Shawna picked it up. I moved beside her shoulder as she unfolded it. One edge had been torn from a longer note. The ink was red, the handwriting crowded and slanted exactly like the card I'd carried to breakfast.

I read the order with her.

*T — He has the withdrawal and Vale's story. If he gets the stone, he can name every one of us. Finish Dante tonight. No more brother shit. Valentino takes the watch. Keep her alive. I'll deal with her. R.*

Beneath it, in darker ink, Dante had added four words.

*Shawna. If I'm wrong.*

She went back to the beginning. I watched her eyes reach the bottom and begin again. The third time, her mouth opened but she couldn't get the sentence out.

“No,” Shawna said.

I reached toward her hand. She pulled away and stood so fast that her chair fell behind her.

“He knew?” Shawna asked, looking at Marcellus.

The old man kept his eyes on the paper. “He suspected.”

Shawna shook her head. “He fucking knew.”

I took the note before it could tear in her hands. Everything in me became very still.

Red had spoken about protection at breakfast. About the room he'd given Shawna. About buying her a dress. On this paper, he'd ordered her husband's death and reserved the widow for whatever he intended next. The same hand that offered me breakfast had decided who would get Dante's watch.

I looked at the signature.

“Nigga,” I said, so quietly that Marcellus turned toward me.

I still don't know who Dante got the note from. I didn't know why he hadn't run the moment he read it. He had written if beneath an instruction to kill him, as though a brother might explain the words until they meant something else.

I knew the kind of hope that could do that to a man. T had put his hand on my neck and called me son. I had wanted to believe him right up to the moment he ordered me back inside to fetch Shawna.

She stood by the living-room window with both hands on her head.

“He went to talk to his brother,” Shawna said. “Told me there was a mistake he needed to straighten out. I was mad he wouldn't stay for dinner.”

Marcellus closed his eyes.

Shawna turned toward us. “Told him I'd throw his plate away.”

Marcellus closed the empty compartment in the watch and set it beside the cloth. I laid the note on the table next to him and went toward Shawna.

“Shawna,” I said.

She held one hand out. “Don't say it.”

I stopped a step away. “Wasn't going to—”

Shawna's voice rose. “Don't say there was nothing I could do. Could've locked the door. Could've gone with him. Could've—”

I answered before she finished. “Died too.”

She slapped me across the mouth.

My head turned. The place that hadn't finished healing split again, and blood touched my tongue. I stayed where I was with my jaw clenched. When I looked back, her hand was still in the air.

“Aiden,” Shawna said.

Behind her, Mercedes had stopped beside the table. Marcellus's hands lay flat against the cloth. I could feel myself wanting to turn pain into something the room would have to deal with for me.

I took a breath through my nose. Then I caught Shawna's wrist, lowered her hand, and let it go.

“I'm going outside,” I told her.

I walked out through the patio doors.

Tank stood near the lake throwing pieces of bread to the birds. He turned when he heard me and looked at my mouth.

“What happened?” Tank asked.

I stopped beside him. “Don't.”

He put the rest of the roll on the low wall. I stood looking across the water until my breathing slowed enough that I didn't want to break something simply because it was available.

Tank kept his eyes on the lake. “She hit you?”

I turned toward him. “Said don't.”

Tank raised a hand. “All right.”

A bird walked between us, hopped onto the wall and took the entire roll. Tank watched it struggle to get the bread turned sideways in its beak before waddling away.

“That was mine,” Tank said.

I lowered myself onto the wall. “Go tell him.”

Tank stayed where he was. I almost laughed. It pulled at my mouth, so I stopped halfway through and wiped the blood with my thumb.

“Red ordered it,” I told Tank. “Dante. His own handwriting.”

Tank stared at the water.

I watched the side of his face. “You know?”

Tank shook his head. “No.”

I waited before asking the next thing. “Hear anything?”

Tank moved his jaw once. “Heard Dante took money.”

I looked down at the blood on my thumb. “All?”

Tank faced me. “All anybody said around me. Didn't know him like that. T had me moving things. Driving. Wasn't in every room.”

I wanted to believe him. I wanted somebody I loved to finish a story before another man arrived carrying the piece they'd left out.

I looked back at the house. “Taking Red's diamond.”

Tank followed my gaze. “Then?”

I touched the edge of my split lip. “Look at how poor he got.”

Tank folded the empty bread bag. “Forty million ain't everything he has.”

I kept watching the patio door. “Then I'll stay busy.”

Shawna came out carrying a wet cloth and the watch, closed again but loose in her other hand. Tank picked up the bread bag and walked toward the side of the house. She stopped in front of me.

“Shouldn't have done that,” Shawna said.

I looked at the cloth. “No shit.”

She held it a little higher. “Can I see?”

I let her touch my chin. She cleaned the blood from my mouth while I looked past her shoulder. I was still too angry to look directly at her without preparing something cruel to say.

“I love him,” Shawna told me.

I closed my eyes for a moment. “I know.”

She lifted the cloth away. “Love you too.”

I opened my eyes and looked at her. “That last night with Dante. Did you get to tell him you loved him before he left?”

Shawna shook her head. “Mad about dinner.”

Her answer took the next bitter sentence out of my mouth. She laid the cloth on the wall and touched my face with her empty hand.

“I love you,” Shawna said again.

I took that hand and pressed it against my chest, clear of the wound. I wanted her to feel me there, alive, without having to strike anything to find me.

“Don't hit me again,” I told her.

Shawna met my eyes. “I won't.”

I looked at the watch still in her other hand. “Don't leave that off to shut me up either. Put it on. When you tell me something, I want the whole thing.”

She lowered her eyes to the watch. Then she fastened it around her wrist.

We went back inside. Marcellus returned the loupe and tools to the jeweler, who packed his case and said goodbye. While the food was opened, Shawna went upstairs and returned with the blue invitations. Then we sat down together. Marcellus took a clear sleeve from inside his coat and laid it beside my plate. The folds in the paper inside had worn pale across the writing.

“Celeste's,” Marcellus told me. “What Dante heard.”

I put down my fork. Shawna rested her hand beside mine while I read the whole letter, including the lines she hadn't remembered upstairs. At one fold I held the sleeve closer to the lamp. Marcellus reached across, flattened the crease beneath two fingers, and withdrew his hand so I could finish.

When I returned it, he slid it inside his coat carefully, smoothing the pocket afterward with his palm.

I waited until he finished the movement. “Want your stone?”

Marcellus looked at me. “Yes.”

I kept my voice level. “Want Red dead?”

He looked at Shawna's face before answering. “Yes.”

I leaned back. “Think we can work together.”

Marcellus didn't smile. Shawna set the invitations on the table, and he picked one up.

“He's displaying it in three nights,” Shawna said.

Marcellus studied the printed necklace. “He's displaying something.”

I put my fork down. “Two of them.”

Marcellus nodded. “Yes.”

I tapped the edge of the card. “You tell which one's which?”

Marcellus looked up at me. “Of course.”

I spread my hand toward him. “Then you're going.”

He set the invitation down. “He knows my face.”

I looked at his coat. “Knows the man he put away eight years ago. Different suit. Different name.”

Rosa had joined us while we ate. She leaned forward to examine Marcellus with the same attention she'd given Shawna's dress.

“Know a woman can make you look like you have money,” Rosa told him.

Marcellus looked around my dining room, then at me. “Apparently people are easily confused.”

Tank covered his mouth with a napkin. I let the old man have it. He'd had a hard day and found a reliable use for mine.

Marcellus borrowed a pencil from Rosa and turned the invitation over. On its plain back he drew the blue outer setting, then a smaller outline inside it.

“This comes off,” Marcellus said, tapping the outer shape. “Stone can be worn another way.”

He put a tiny mark inside the second outline. “The true diamond has a flaw here. I remember exactly where.”

Shawna leaned close to the picture on the other invitation. “This necklace could be either?”

Marcellus nodded. “The mounting doesn't make the stone genuine.”

I looked at the card, then at the hands that had opened the watch. “Get you close enough to look, then.”

Red had made me stand beneath Shawna's photograph. He'd made sure I saw his white piano. At breakfast, he'd offered me my own streets in exchange for returning her to him. I believed there was something he wanted badly enough to bring us closer than he ought to. The trick would be persuading him it was already coming willingly.

Shawna sat straighter in her chair. “I can get him to invite us closer.”

I looked at her. “No.”

Shawna put a hand beside my plate. “Aiden.”

I shook my head. “No.”

She left her hand there. “Haven't heard what I'm saying.”

I looked at the way she'd squared her shoulders. “Heard how you sat up.”

Shawna took my hand. “I tell him I made a mistake. You scare me. I'm bringing you to the gala so he can handle it quietly.”

Tank leaned forward. “He believes that?”

Shawna looked at him. “He wants to.”

I tightened my fingers around hers. “What else?”

She returned to me. “Tell him I know where you're keeping the money.”

I looked toward the note on the table. “Don't have his money.”

Shawna squeezed my hand. “He doesn't know that.”

Tank set his glass down beside his plate. I heard the careful click and looked over for a moment. He was looking at the invitation. I went back to Shawna.

She continued. “I'm tired of running. I'll come back if he clears the debt and leaves you alive.”

I let out a short breath. “Why he leaving me alive?”

Shawna held my gaze. “Because I'll say you can give him the west side.”

I took my hand from hers.
