Nobody spoke for a moment. Shawna leaned toward me across the table.

“Words he'll listen to,” she said. “You know that.”

I did. Knowing didn't stop the words from hurting. I had sat beneath his photograph of her that morning, and now we were discussing how convincingly she could promise to stand beside him again.

Marcellus folded the invitation closed. “If he thinks she can give you to him, he'll want you close enough to take.”

I looked at the old man. “He can try.”

Rosa put both palms on the table. “Near the jewel. He looks. We leave with what's his. Nobody gets mad halfway through and decides it's time to kill somebody.”

She looked at Shawna first. Shawna didn't answer. Then Rosa pointed at me.

“You either,” Rosa said.

I looked around the table. “Why everybody looking at me?”

Rosa pointed at my face. “Because you have it.”

Tank nodded. I looked at him until he lowered his eyes to his plate, still nodding.

Shawna made the call from our bedroom that night. I sat beside her while she brought Red's number up on the screen. She raised the volume enough for me to hear and held the phone between us.

Red answered on the second ring.

“There she is,” Red said through the receiver.

Shawna closed her eyes. I put a hand on her knee.

“Can you talk?” Shawna asked him.

Red's answer came softly. “For you?”

I wanted to take the phone and drive it through the wall. I kept my hand where it was.

“Think I made a mistake,” Shawna told him.

The line went quiet. Beyond the bedroom glass, the lake had gone black. Our reflection showed her holding the phone and me beside her, looking far less like a man helping with a plan than a man waiting to hear how the plan would hurt.

“He hurt you?” Red asked.

Shawna looked at the cut on my mouth. “Don't know what he'll do next.”

Red answered quickly. “Come home.”

Shawna lowered her eyes. “Can't just leave.”

Red drew a breath I could hear through the receiver. “You can.”

Shawna kept the phone steady. “You want the money. His streets. I can bring him to you, Red. Have to promise me something.”

Red didn't pause. “Anything.”

I stared at the wall. His written order to kill Dante had been shorter than the promises he was making to Dante's widow.

“Let him live,” Shawna said.

Red sighed into the phone. “Too good to these boys.”

My hand left her knee. Shawna caught it before I could stand and pressed it flat against the sheet.

“I know where he's keeping the money,” Shawna continued. “Can get him to bring what you need. At the gala I stay close to you. Everybody sees I'm back. After that, you talk to him.”

Red's voice came lower. “And you stay?”

Shawna looked straight at me. “Yes.”

He let out a long breath. I heard satisfaction in it, or what sounded like satisfaction to a man forced to sit there and listen. My fingers curled beneath hers. She kept holding them.

“Wear the blue dress,” Red said. “The one I liked.”

Shawna didn't look away from me. “I will.”

Red said her name again before she could end the call.

“What?” Shawna asked him.

Red answered slowly. “Knew you'd remember who loved you.”

She ended the call without replying. The room was silent except for the air coming through the vent.

I knew it was a lie. I had sat at the table and helped make it. I still felt every word as if she had tried it on against my skin.

Shawna put the phone facedown, took my face between her hands, and waited until my eyes met hers.

“I'm here,” Shawna told me.

I pulled her close. Too hard. My side burned, and I flinched against her. Shawna moved against my uninjured side, taking my hand with her, and held it at her shoulder until my breathing settled.

Downstairs, I could hear chairs moving and Tank's low voice on a call. I couldn't make out the words. Before we came up, Marcellus had spread the invitations beside his letter and Rosa had begun making arrangements for clothes. The house had filled with people preparing to take something back.

I held Shawna and pictured Red standing where I had stood that afternoon: looking at an empty hand, trying to understand who had left him with it.

Three nights.

I could wait three nights.

I told myself that until morning.

## 9. Our Valedictorian Has a Gun

I learned I was graduating from college while deciding which gun went with breakfast.

The kitchen table had become crowded enough that I kept the pistols on the empty chair beside me. Shawna poured coffee. Rosa argued with Mercedes about toast. Tank came in holding a cream envelope with a gold crest and my name written across it by somebody who had put more commitment into the A than I'd put into several relationships.

“School,” Tank said, setting it beside my plate.

I looked up. “Whose?”

Tank pulled out the chair across from me, leaving the armed one alone. “Yours.”

I put down the pistol I'd been considering. “I got a school?”

Shawna stopped pouring. Rosa turned around. Mercedes lowered her toast and looked at the envelope as though it might explain something troubling about her own education.

Tank read the crest. “Las Vegas College.”

I nodded. “Oh. Them.”

Shawna set the pot down. “You went to college?”

I opened the envelope. “Went to the building.”

Shawna leaned toward me. “When?”

I remembered the dining tables before I remembered the year. “Twenty-two. Free lunch. Excellent chicken.”

Tank took the second page from beneath my hand and stared at it.

“Says you're the valedictorian,” Tank told the room.

I reached for my coffee. “They've been paying attention.”

Rosa came around the table with her hand out. “Give me that.”

I moved my cup out of her way. “Don't wrinkle my achievement.”

Rosa held the letter close to her face. “I'm looking for the payment demand. Mercedes, see if he's on a milk carton too. Been somewhere two years and forgot.”

Mercedes leaned against Rosa's shoulder to read. “Highest marks in the college's history.”

Tank looked from them to me. “Marks in what?”

I nodded toward Mercedes. “History. She just said.”

Shawna turned my chin toward her. “Baby. What did you do to these people?”

I thought about the chicken.

Two years earlier, a friend told me the college had food at its adult scholarship day. You had to be at least twenty-two for the program. I was exactly twenty-two and hungry enough to consider a future if it came with a plate.

The woman at registration told me lunch was for applicants. I became an applicant before she finished the word.

She gave me a booklet and pointed me toward a table. There was math, reading, music that looked wrong before I even heard it in my head, and a question about two promises that couldn't both be kept. I ate and wrote. When the chicken was gone, I corrected the music and took two rolls. The back page was blank, so I used it to explain what else they'd got wrong.

I told everyone at breakfast that part. Rosa lowered the letter. Tank looked at my plate for so long that I pushed it an inch toward him.

“You ate a degree,” Tank said.

I took my plate back. “I thought while I chewed. You acting like it's forbidden technology.”

Mercedes reached around Rosa and turned the page. “They want you there at eleven.” Rosa passed the letter back to Tank, who checked the time for himself.

Shawna looked at the kitchen clock. Nine twenty. She put her coffee down without drinking it.

“We need clothes,” Shawna said.

I looked toward the blue gala invitations on the sideboard. “Got plans.”

Porsche came through the doorway with sunglasses on and a garment bag over one arm. She took one look at the college letter in Tank's hands and stopped beside my chair.

“Your plans got a hat,” Porsche told me. “You're going.”

I pointed at the invitations. “Red's gala—”

Porsche looked at the clock. “Two nights away. This is a hundred minutes. Even you can count that far.”

Tank lifted the college letter. “Actually, he's got the highest marks—”

“Don't help him,” Rosa told Tank.

Mercedes put her toast back on the plate. “Please don't.”

Shawna ignored both of them and kissed me. Then she stayed close enough for me to hear her without the rest of the room.

“I want to see my man graduate,” Shawna said.

I could have graduated from the moon if she'd said it like that. I stood, secured the pistol I'd chosen, and put the other one away before anyone started treating the breakfast table like a faculty meeting.

“Get dressed,” I told them. “My niggas going to college.”

Tank looked down at his unfinished breakfast.

I pointed at the plate. “Bring it. Apparently that's the exam.”

We arrived in a convoy because education deserved respect.

Tank drove our Mercedes. Its door latched again. I had heard him arranging that repair while the rest of us argued about clothes, and I was beginning to suspect the men fixing the car slept beside their tools with one eye open.

Porsche drove Leon's newest Bentley, dark green this time. She told us he'd said white brought back too many memories. Mercedes sat beside her with a camera. Rosa wore a yellow suit that made the campus flowers look as if they had lost their funding. Marcellus wore his clean shirt and a tie Rosa had selected. I watched him straighten the tie in a car window before we left. Then he looked toward me and asked whether it sat properly. I told him it did.

Shawna wore white again. Nobody had needed to die for this outfit.

I wore black trousers and an open-necked shirt, with the cross against my chest. My jacket covered the bandages and the pistol, which I kept clear of the wound. I'd read the invitation twice while dressing. Neither page asked me to arrive with fewer problems than usual.

Blue gowns filled the campus entrance. Grown men and women stood beside the fountain holding flowers and programs, adjusting caps, calling to people across the paving. I lowered my window while Tank eased the Mercedes past a little group near the water.

One man pointed at our car.

“That's him,” the man said to his friend.

His friend looked up from a program. “Who?”

The first man leaned close to the window. “Mercedes nigga.”

His friend waved the program at the parking lot. “College, Marcus. Seventeen Mercedes over there.”

Marcus shook his pointing hand. “Flying Mercedes.”

His friend looked properly then. His mouth opened. He checked the program and began hitting Marcus's shoulder with it.

“Our valedictorian?” the friend asked.

Marcus put both hands out. “What I'm saying!”

The friend stared through my window. “That nigga?”

Marcus leaned down until he could see me smiling back at them. “That nigga.”

By the time I got out, the people around the fountain had turned toward us.

A woman hurried down the steps carrying a blue robe over both arms. She had silver glasses, dark skin, precisely pinned hair, and no spare hand to move the graduates out of her way. She threaded between them with the hem gathered against her chest and arrived a little out of breath.

“Aiden,” she said. “Professor Yvonne Halcyon. We met at your admission.”

I recognized her when she smiled. “You had the chicken.”

Halcyon's smile widened. “You remember.”

I took the robe from her. “Don't forget a good plate.”

Halcyon looked past me at the car. “We've been looking for you.”

I followed her glance. “Been around.”

She faced me again. “We've seen.”

Principal Percival Mercer came out behind her wearing enough gold over his blue academic robe to make me wonder whether the diploma included territory. His gray beard was beautifully trimmed, his voice deep, and by the time he reached my outstretched hand, he was crying.

“At last,” Mercer said.

Tank leaned toward my ear. “What the fuck was in that booklet?”

I watched Mercer take my hand between both of his. “Apparently me.”

The principal shook it without letting go. “Your work changed this college.”

I looked toward Halcyon. “Which part?”

Mercer opened a folder. “The final page.”

I looked at the copy inside. My handwriting, crowded around a grease mark. “Blank one?”

Mercer nodded, still wet around the eyes. “You corrected the examination.”

I recognized a line I'd crossed out. “There was a mistake.”

Mercer held the folder higher. “Six. We'd found two in twenty years.”

That sounded like something I might have done while waiting for dessert. I asked if that was the original paper. He told me they'd framed it. This was a copy for handling. Somewhere in a college building, a grease spot from my chicken had achieved archival protection.

Halcyon stepped beside him. “The scholarship board accepted your independent work in place of the standard course sequence. Four departments circulated your essays. Your music analysis corrected the conservatory examination. Professor Lorne has taught your final solution since last spring.”

I glanced toward the men behind her. “Hope Lorne's got a nice voice.”

A man lifted one hand. “Do my best.”

He straightened his collar after saying it. I smiled at him before he could start apologizing to my own work on my behalf.

Shawna caught my sleeve. Her eyes were bright.

“Didn't tell me any of this,” Shawna said.

I looked from her to the carefully protected grease mark. “Didn't know they kept it.”

The graduates nearby laughed. One took off his cap and rubbed his hair.

“Came every night for three years,” the graduate said.

I turned toward him. “You here, ain't you?”

He looked down at the cap in his hand, then back at me. “Yeah.”

I nodded toward the building. “Come on, then. Graduating.”

He put his cap back on and joined us as Halcyon led the way. She stopped long enough to settle the robe over my jacket and put a cap on my head. I held still while she adjusted the tassel. It was the first part of the morning that felt like somebody had expected me to be a student.

On the way to the auditorium, Mercer insisted we go through the science hall. He wanted to show me a wall. Lorne went ahead while the principal stopped us outside.

Gold letters above the doors read AIDEN HALL.

I stood beneath them. Tank took a picture. Shawna laughed behind her hand, and I tilted my head until I could see my whole name at once.

“Got a hall,” I told her.

Mercer moved beside me. “Several.”

Rosa put a hand on his arm. “Don't tell him. He'll start naming doors.”

Inside, three professors stood around a table covered with papers. I heard one insist a number couldn't be right just before he saw us. The others stopped talking at different moments, leaving one last offended syllable to hang in the room by itself.

Lorne picked up a page. “Perhaps you could settle something.”

Halcyon made a little sound through her nose. “He has a ceremony.”

Lorne held one finger up. “One minute.”

I approached the table. “Everybody needs one minute till I get there. Show me.”

He turned the sheet around. A drawing of the auditorium roof. Lines, numbers, a span circled several times in red. I looked at it, then through the window at the building across the courtyard.

Lorne touched the circled part. “The model gives two possible answers.”

I looked out of the window again. “Old roof.”

Lorne bent over the drawing. “The dimensions—”

I pointed across the courtyard. “Moved the stage. Changed that end. Eight panels in the window now. You got seven.”

All three professors turned toward the real building. I took the pencil lying beside the page, moved the line to match what we could see, and followed the load across to where it belonged.

“There,” I told Lorne. “Now those numbers stop fighting.”

His mouth moved without a sound. The professor beside him found another sheet, compared the two, and sat down so suddenly his wheeled chair rolled back into the wall.

“He's right,” the seated professor said.

I put the pencil down. “Say it like you enjoyed finding out.”

The professor looked up, smiling this time. “He's right!”

The room laughed. Mercer began crying again. I looked at Shawna while Halcyon searched her pockets for something to give him.

“Your man got a roof,” I told Shawna.

She hooked two fingers through my robe. “Better get under it before they name another building.”

As we left, Lorne touched my arm gently.

“What do you do for a living?” Lorne asked.

Tank answered over my shoulder. “Improves transportation.”

The auditorium was packed. A choir stood on risers behind the stage, between blue curtains, and banners hung below the balcony. The program said the youngest graduate was twenty-two and the oldest sixty-eight. Grown people held flowers, smoothed one another's gowns and tried to find their relatives in the upper rows. Somewhere in that crowd sat the man who'd come every night for three years. I kept looking for his cap.

Our people took the front row. Shawna sat in the middle. Tank chose the aisle, examining his chair before lowering himself into it. The chair survived the inspection. I hoped it enjoyed the rest of its morning.

At the side of the stage, Knox waited with several officers. Something low and wide stood near her beneath a dark blue cloth.

I stopped at the steps. “Following me to school?”

Knox held up a program. “Invited.”

I came onto the stage. “Graduating?”

Knox looked toward the cloth. “Recruiting the valedictorian.”

I knew what was underneath it from the shape of the nose. A Lamborghini could wear a bedsheet and I'd recognize it in a church parking lot. I took another step toward the covered car before she put a hand against my robe.

“Speech first,” Knox told me.

I looked at her. “Cruel.”

Knox took her hand away. “Motivation.”

She went to stand beside Mercer. I waited just inside the curtain, where I could see the stage and the audience through the opening. Across the auditorium, a central aisle ran between the seats to the broad doors above the college's front steps. A side exit stood to my left. Behind the choir were the loading doors they'd used to bring in whatever beautiful thing I wasn't allowed to touch yet.

The ceremony began with names and applause. Graduates crossed the stage, some walking quickly, some stopping to look out at the person who had come to see them. I watched a woman hold up her diploma toward the balcony. Somebody up there shouted her name twice before breaking into sobs.

Shawna kept turning toward me. Each time our eyes met, I felt a little bigger inside the robe. I had a bullet in my body, two nights before the gala, and an entire college preparing to give me a microphone voluntarily. I meant to enjoy at least one of those facts.

Then the lights flickered.

I looked up. A man in a gray suit stood in the upper aisle. He was watching the exits instead of the stage. Below him, another man waited by the door under the balcony. Both jackets hung around shapes I recognized more readily than my own curriculum.

I caught Knox's eye and looked toward the upper aisle. She followed my gaze.

The man below the balcony drew his pistol. At the same moment, orange flame appeared at the lower edge of a curtain behind the choir.

It ran upward faster than I expected. Blue cloth darkened, curled, and became a strip of fire beside the white robes. A woman in the choir screamed. The gunman below the balcony fired into the ceiling, and people all over the auditorium tried to rise at once.

I stepped out from the curtain and grabbed the microphone.

“Off the stairs!” I shouted. “Heads down! Clear the aisle!”

My voice came through the speakers loud enough to turn faces toward me. Knox fired from beside the stage. The lower gunman fell backward behind a row of seats. Her officers moved toward him while Tank got our front row down. Shawna pulled Rosa away from the open aisle.

Above them, the other man raised his weapon over the balcony rail.

I drew my pistol from beneath the robe and fired. He struck the rail and dropped behind it. His gun fell into the empty strip beside the lower seats. An officer ran toward the balcony stairs while I turned toward the choir.

People were trying to descend from the risers through the smoke. A woman with a cane had stopped halfway down. I put the microphone back on its stand, secured my pistol, and ran to her. My cap fell when I bent beneath her arm. I left it.

“This way,” I told the woman with the cane.

A man behind her took her other side. We brought her down to the stage, keeping her feet clear of the next step, and got her to the wing away from the burning cloth. I looked back long enough to see other singers following the route we'd used.

Mercer stood near the lectern with my diploma pressed against his chest.

I caught his sleeve. “Principal. Move.”

Mercer held the folder up. “Your degree—”

I pulled him toward the clearer side of the stage. “Need somebody alive to sign it.”

He came with me. Shawna had got onto the stage and took his arm. My cap was in her other hand.

A burning crossbeam from the hanging decorations fell across the side exit before we reached it. Sparks rose from the broken timber, and Shawna pulled Mercer back from the falling edge. Behind us, fire had spread across the passage to the loading doors. People near the back of the auditorium were escaping through the main doors, but overturned seats and benches jammed the central aisle between them and the front rows. We could see daylight. We couldn't get our people to it.

I looked at the covered car.

Knox was already pulling the cloth away.

Dark blue. A gold shield on the door. Black wheels. A light bar above the windshield. It was the most beautiful fucking police Lamborghini I'd ever seen waiting to become evidence.

Knox threw me the keys.

“Don't scratch it,” she called.

I caught them against my chest. “Don't limit me.”

Tank reached the stage as I opened the driver's door. The woman with the cane stood beside the wall between two singers; he checked that they had her before turning toward me.

“Driving in the school?” Tank asked.

I lifted the robe clear of my feet. “Improving the exit.”

Tank looked toward the aisle. “Went to the wrong fucking college.”

Tank went down the shallow center steps and pulled the nearest overturned seats out of the way. Knox took the microphone and sent her officers ahead, telling everybody to get clear of the central aisle. I waited with the engine running while a graduate crawled beneath a row and an officer lifted a fallen chair off a woman's dress. Then Tank looked back and raised his hand. He moved aside, keeping the last two graduates behind him. Only furniture remained between my bumper and the doors.

Through the windshield, I could see Shawna at the stage's edge with Mercer's arm in her grip. She looked at the car, the fire, then me. Her hand shook against the principal's sleeve. She smiled anyway.

I held that smile for one breath, then drove.

The Lamborghini crossed the stage and dropped down the shallow steps. The impact drove pain through my side. I kept both hands on the wheel. Empty seats struck the bumper and went skidding aside. A broken bench turned beneath us with a crack that I felt through the floor.

Smoke thickened against the windshield. I stayed pointed along the aisle Tank had cleared. A burning strip of curtain fell over the hood, washing the glass orange. For a moment I couldn't see the doors. I kept the wheel straight until the cloth slid off and the opening appeared beyond it.

I pressed the accelerator.

We went through the far doors together, me and the car. Both doors tore from their hinges. Daylight filled the windshield. The Lamborghini dropped down the broad front steps and struck the courtyard paving with its siren screaming.

I braked beyond the foot of the stairs, out of the opening I'd made.

In the mirror, people appeared through the smoke: white choir robes, graduates supporting one another, Tank carrying the woman with the cane. Rosa and Mercedes dragged a loose bench away from the opening. Porsche came out with an extinguisher in each hand, set one at her feet and sent a white cloud into the burning frame with the other.

“Keep moving!” Porsche shouted. “Niggas, the air is outside!”

She sounded like she'd personally purchased the oxygen for the event. People obeyed her all the way down the steps.

I turned off the engine, took the keys, and climbed out. My legs shook when I stood, but I went back up the steps as fast as I could make them work.

Knox met me at the broken opening with Mercer beside her. I dropped the keys into her hand. Shawna came behind them, one hand over her mouth, the other still holding my cap.

“Baby,” I said, reaching for her.

She came into my arms, hit my good shoulder with the cap, then settled it on my head.

“Almost forgot something,” Shawna told me.

I straightened it. “Making an entrance.”

She brushed soot from my cheek. “Made an exit.”

I put a hand against her back. “Great man does both.”

She kissed me while people in the courtyard shouted. I kept my arms around her until she was breathing more easily.

Fire crews came through the gates. Knox's officers brought both attackers outside alive and cuffed. One leaned between two officers; the other walked with his jacket hanging open while a medic held a dressing to his shoulder. They stopped close enough for me to hear them arguing.

“We burned the building,” the lower-floor gunman said, staring at the Lamborghini.

The man who'd been in the balcony looked up at the broken doors. “Still came through it.”

His partner pointed his chin at the shield on the car. “Police car.”

The balcony gunman turned toward him. “I saw.”

They had come to ruin my graduation and ended up reviewing the best part together. I adjusted the tassel until it hung properly, then looked for Mercer.

“Principal,” I called. “Got a microphone out here?”

They put the lectern on a low platform beside the fountain. Halcyon brought a portable speaker. Mercer stayed close to my diploma while the graduates gathered in front of us, some with soot on their robes, some holding shoes they'd lost on the stairs. People kept finding one another and staying together once they did.

Lorne stood near the edge of the crowd, looking from the damaged auditorium to the roof still above it. I hoped he was getting some satisfaction out of having the right drawing at last.

I climbed the two steps to the lectern. Tank stood behind Shawna, arms folded. Marcellus rested a hand on Rosa's shoulder. Mercedes raised her camera.

I leaned toward the microphone. “Aight. Where we at?”

Laughter spread across the courtyard, loud and uneven. I waited until it let me back in.

“Ain't gonna lie,” I told the graduates. “Wasn't here much.”

A man near the fountain called out that I hadn't been there at all.

I pointed toward the smoke. “Look what happened when I came. You're welcome.”

They cheered. A man in the third row took off his cap and waved it. I looked past him at people still catching their breath, still holding on to the person beside them. They had come for their names. I could say something about that.

I put both hands on the lectern. “Some of you worked all night and came anyway. Some had people telling you this wasn't your kind of room. Some got somebody missing today who should've been here making too much fucking noise.”

Shawna looked down at her watch, then back at me.

“But you're here,” I told them. “When the doors caught fire, you started carrying each other. Saw it. That belongs to you. Keep it. Don't let anybody make you little enough to forget.”

I took the cross out from beneath my robe and held it up.

“Three letters,” I said. “D. B. M.”

Tank's eyebrows went up. Shawna began smiling before I gave the words to the crowd.

“Damn, boy,” I said. “Magnificent.”

The courtyard erupted. I let the noise rise around the fountain, then lifted my hand for another moment.

“That's you too,” I told them. “Men, women, every grown motherfucker here with a cap and a dream. Look in your mirror tomorrow and say it properly. Don't mumble greatness in your own house.”

Mercedes lowered her camera long enough to shout. “Say it again!”

I leaned into the microphone. “Damn!”

The crowd answered me. “Boy!”

I spread my arms. “Magnificent!”

They shouted the last word back so loudly that pigeons lifted off the roof of Aiden Hall together. I watched them go while Mercer put the diploma into my hand. He was still crying. I had driven through a burning auditorium and finished the speech before that man finished his first breakdown.

Education was going beautifully.

Knox came onto the platform before somebody could schedule the next thing for me to survive. Two officers followed with a blue velvet box. Another carried a dark uniform jacket with gold braid at the shoulders.

People went quiet as they noticed the box. I looked at Knox.

“You're making this difficult to postpone,” Knox told me.

I held up the diploma. “Busy graduating.”

Knox turned toward the crowd. “You've saved my officers and protected this city's people. You've also demonstrated a relationship with automobiles I've never seen in a man who still has all his limbs.”

Porsche began clapping. A few people joined her before Knox opened the box.

Inside lay a gold captain's badge.

“Captain Aiden,” Knox announced. “Las Vegas Police Department. Actual command. Actual badge. Actual trouble for anybody who thinks otherwise.”

An officer beside the platform turned toward his partner. His partner nodded, and they both looked back at me. I looked down at the gold. Nobody appeared to be bringing out the joke badge afterward.

Tank bent forward with his hands on his knees.

“Nigga,” Tank said.

Rosa caught his arm. “You all right?”

Tank looked up at her. “He's a cop.”

Rosa nodded toward the badge. “Heard.”

Tank turned toward me. “My nigga's the police.”

Rosa pulled at his sleeve. “Stand up before he arrests your posture.”

Knox held the badge in one hand and faced me.

“Right hand,” she said.

I gave Shawna the diploma at the edge of the platform, then raised my hand. Knox spoke the oath in short phrases, and I repeated each one. Keep faith with my people. Stand when somebody needed standing for. Put my whole name behind the authority I used.

I heard my own voice through the speaker. The chief, the officers, the graduates, Shawna: all of them stood in front of me while I said it. I had accepted a lot of things before understanding what they might ask of me. This time I listened to every word on its way out of my mouth.

Knox opened my robe and pinned the badge inside my jacket.

“Captain,” she said.

I looked down at the gold. My mother would have needed to sit down twice.

Shawna came up the steps with my diploma in one hand. She put it carefully on the lectern, took my face between her palms, and kissed me slowly. The officers began applauding before the students did.

“My man,” Shawna whispered.

I kept my forehead close to hers. “Your captain.”

She held my face where she wanted it. “Still my man.”

I could have stayed there letting her settle the rank. Knox waited until Shawna stepped back, then put the Lamborghini's keys in my hand again. An officer passed the new uniform jacket to Tank, who held it carefully over his arm, looking at the braid as though it might start ordering him around.

“Car's yours,” Knox said.

I looked past her at the smoke-darkened hood. “Noticed.”

She moved close enough that I could hear her beneath the crowd.

“Red's people thought they owned every uniform that mattered,” Knox told me. “Let him see yours.”

I looked at her properly then. She smiled and closed my fingers over the keys.

“I know who I recruited,” Knox said. “Captain.”

Shawna collected the diploma from the lectern before we left. She sat beside me in the Lamborghini with it resting on her lap and the badge inside my jacket. I had taken off the graduation robe and folded it behind the seat. The hood smelled of smoke. One door wore a long scratch. The siren still worked, because apparently the day had decided to leave me something perfect.

Tank followed in the original Mercedes. The rest of the convoy came behind him, horns sounding as we crossed the campus. Graduates turned to watch. At the fountain, Marcus lifted his cap.

“That's our nigga!” Marcus shouted.

His friend raised the program he'd hit him with earlier. “Our captain!”

I sounded the siren once for each of them.

Shawna put a hand on my leg as the college disappeared behind us.

“What you doing tomorrow?” Shawna asked.

I looked at her, then returned my eyes to the road and let the car stretch out beneath us.

“Baby,” I told her, “they better not schedule anything they can't afford.”

## 10. Forty Million and One Woman

The morning after I graduated, four men robbed a casino while I was buying coffee in uniform.

That was poor timing. Parking their van across my Lamborghini's bumper was personal.

I stood outside the coffee shop holding two cups. Shawna waited in the passenger seat with sunglasses on and my captain's jacket over her dress. She'd borrowed it because, she told me, the gold braid felt good against her wrists. I'd moved the badge to my shirt before handing it over. The department hadn't owned me long enough to begin inventorying what she'd taken.

Across the street, an alarm sounded. Four men came out of the casino carrying bags. One kept a pistol pointed back toward the doors. Another had a split lip. The biggest carried a bag in each hand and kept looking toward their van, which occupied the space my car needed if I intended to go anywhere with the coffee.

They crossed the road between stopped cars, the gunman still pointing back at the casino. The man who turned out to be the driver hurried ahead and opened the rear doors. I looked at him, then at the cups, then at Shawna.

“Hold these,” I told her.

She took both through the open window. “Getting involved?”

I stepped away from the car. “Involved me when they parked.”

The driver stood by the open back of the van, waving his men toward it. He saw me coming and laughed.

“Keep walking, pretty boy,” the driver said.

I turned so he could see the badge on my uniform shirt. His eyes went to it. The laugh stopped coming out of his mouth and stayed there as an unhelpful shape.

“Police?” the driver asked.

I looked past him at my blocked bumper. “Captain.”

He looked at my face again. “Don't look like police.”

I pointed at the van. “Police improving. Move it.”

The armed man had a short beard and a pistol coming up toward my chest. I caught the open rear door and drove its edge into his forearm. The shot struck the street. I shoved the door harder, pinning his wrist against the side panel until his fingers opened and the pistol fell beneath the van. He dropped to one knee, clutching his arm.

The driver ran for the front. The big man tossed his bags inside, grabbed the bearded gunman beneath his arms, and hauled him through the back of the van. The man with the split lip climbed in after them. All four were aboard when the engine rose and the van lurched forward, its rear doors still open.

A money bag rolled out and burst against the curb. The split-lip man reached for another one sliding after it. I caught the rear door handle as the van moved past me.

“Aiden!” Shawna shouted from the car.

I looked back once. “Coffee!”

I wasn't going to let these men ruin eight dollars of breakfast and a police jacket before I'd properly worn either one out.

The van pulled me around the corner. My shoes struck the curb, found purchase for one step, then slipped. I kept hold of the handle with both hands, got a foot onto the rear step, and hauled my knee up beside it.

The man with the split lip kicked toward my face. I ducked under his shoe and caught the doorframe.

“Let go!” the split-lip man shouted.

I pulled myself high enough to meet his eyes. “You let go.”

He kicked again. “It's our van!”

I caught his ankle. “Should've moved it.”

I came through the opening shoulder first, driving him backward into the money bags. The bearded gunman sat against the side panel holding the arm I'd struck. The biggest man crouched among the bags, trying to keep them from rolling out around us.

The driver looked back at the noise. The van climbed halfway onto the sidewalk. A newspaper stand burst against the windshield, and pages flew past the open rear doors. I saw my own graduation photograph turn over in the air before the paper went beneath a wheel.

Yesterday's miracle was having trouble with this morning's circulation.

The split-lip man reached toward his waistband. I put my foot on his wrist as his pistol came clear, grinding his hand against the floor until he let it fall. With one hand braced against a shelf, I drew my own gun.

“Don't,” I told him.

He looked up. His eyes moved over my face properly for the first time.

“You're Aiden,” the split-lip man said.

I kept my weight on his wrist. “Easier day knowing that at the coffee shop.”

The big man raised both hands when I looked toward him. The bearded man kept cradling the arm I'd crushed. I kicked the second pistol beneath the front seat before lifting my foot off its owner's wrist. He tucked that hand against his chest and left it there as the van turned.

Concrete closed over the daylight. The driver had taken us into a parking structure. Tires squealed on the first ramp. Through the open doors I could see a central well between two sets of ramps, cars parked around the outer edge, arrows on the floor going in opposite directions.

Then I heard my Lamborghini.

Shawna came through the entrance behind us with the siren on. She sat forward over the wheel, sunglasses still in place. I held the van's shelf with one hand and watched the blue nose appear at the foot of the ramp as though the garage had ordered something beautiful to correct its morning.

We reached the second floor. The driver took the outer lane toward the next ramp, glancing back through the open van at every turn.

“Still behind us!” the driver shouted.

I watched Shawna take the turn below. “She's thorough.”

The driver faced forward again. “Who asked you?”

I tightened my hold as the van tipped into another turn. “Brought me.”

Shawna cut across the second floor toward the inner ramp. Through the open well, I saw the Lamborghini climbing against the arrows while our van followed the longer loop around the parked cars. She disappeared behind a pillar. When we reached the third floor, the blue car came off the neighboring ramp and swung across our lane.

The driver hit the brakes.

The van slid. I caught the passenger seat with my free hand and kept the pistol pointed away from the three men tumbling into the money bags. They landed in a heap. It sounded financially painful.

Through the windshield, I saw Shawna get out of the Lamborghini with her silver pistol. She stood beside the door, keeping the car between herself and the van's driver.

“Everybody out,” Shawna called.

The driver stared at her. “Police too?”

I looked through the windshield at my woman. “Worse. Disappointed.”

He raised both hands.

We got all four men onto the concrete. I watched the driver while Shawna covered the others. She told me she'd called for officers on the way into the garage. Ten minutes later, Knox's people came up the ramp to find the money in the van and my Lamborghini across the lane. I pointed an officer toward the pistol under the front seat and told him where the first one had fallen, back at the coffee shop.

Once the officers had all four men in cuffs, I put my pistol away. A sergeant with a gray mustache shook my hand. I asked him to leave enough of it functioning for the coffee.

“Been after this crew for months, Captain,” the sergeant told me.

I looked at the van. “Parked wrong.”

He studied its position, then my car. “Should've tried that.”

Shawna put her silver pistol away and brought both coffees over. Mine was still warm. I looked inside the Lamborghini: dry cup holders, my jacket spread over the passenger seat. She'd set both drinks down before changing seats, she'd told me, then remembered the jacket and lifted it clear. Not a drop. She'd chased a van full of robbers and preserved breakfast while doing it. Every useful thought I'd ever had about marriage stood up to applaud.

The sergeant saluted her.

“Ma'am,” he said.

Shawna held her coffee away from the gesture. “Can call me Shawna.”

The sergeant nodded. “Yes, ma'am.”

Respect had declined her suggestion.

The casino owner came to the station before lunch. I was wearing my jacket again by then, though Shawna had told me she would need it back when the air-conditioning offended her. The owner brought two gold watches and an envelope. He kept trying to put everything into my hands at once.

Two of his employees sat nearby waiting to give statements. One held an ice pack against the side of his face. The other had a bandage over scraped knuckles. The robbers had knocked them down on the way out. I gave a watch to each of them and kept the envelope long enough to buy lunch for the whole shift.

Officers carried plates through the station and took turns getting pictures beside the Lamborghini. Knox watched from her office doorway. When I came past, she looked toward the car.

“First morning?” Knox asked.

I held up my cup. “I need bigger cup holders.”

Knox laughed. “Anything else?”

I leaned against the doorframe. “Raise.”

Knox glanced at the clock. “Four hours.”

I gestured toward the lunch arriving at the next desk. “Look what the four hours did for you.”

By evening, people all over the station called me by name. By the following afternoon, several had offered to wait outside the Saint in case my evening needed company. I told them to stay close to Knox. I wanted Red looking at the badge, wondering how much of the city stood behind the man wearing it.

That afternoon was the day of the gala. I went to buy a white suit.

The tailor began with black. When that failed, he brought out dark blue. Shawna sat behind me in a little velvet chair, drinking champagne and telling him I looked handsome every time he asked. She had not looked up from her phone once. I could have been wearing a fucking curtain.

I put my hand against the white cloth I'd chosen. “This. Want the whole room seeing who came.”

The tailor's eyes went to the bandage beneath my shirt. “With your injury—”

I looked at him in the mirror. “Suit going to heal me?”

He turned back toward the cloth. The conversation became more productive immediately.

Tank stood by the door in a black suit we'd bought forty minutes earlier. He reached toward a bowl of peppermints. A seam opened across his shoulder with a dry little ripping sound. The tailor paused, then continued smoothing my sleeve as though the noise had happened in another business.

I looked past him. “Get Tank another one.”

The tailor glanced at the torn shoulder. “Larger?”

I held my arms out while he worked. “Whatever size a cathedral is.”

Tank put the peppermint back.

I watched him in the mirror. “Eat. Rich enough for the candy and whatever seam comes with it.”

Shawna laughed then. She looked up at me, phone forgotten against her skirt. For one moment I wasn't trying to read Dante or Red or a missing fortune in her face. My woman was laughing because I stood full of pins and meant to dress Tank like a building.

I would have bought an actual church to keep her there.

The waiting had been harder than the public disasters. Graduation, the badge, the van: those gave me something to do with my hands. Sitting beside Shawna while she sounded like a woman considering Red again gave me nowhere useful to put them.

She'd called him on both evenings since graduation, following the first call we'd planned at the dining table the night before. Three conversations in three nights. On graduation night, she ended the call with baby. I knew why. I'd agreed to the plan. Every intelligent sentence I'd spoken in support of it left my body when I heard that word.

Afterward, I followed her from the bed to the window.

“You don't have to say it like that,” I told her.

Shawna turned with the phone still in her hand. “Like what?”

I pointed at her face. “With your mouth.”

She stared at me for a few seconds. “Aiden.”

I folded my arms. “He's got a name. Use it.”

She put the phone down and kissed me. I let the argument end there. The next evening, after the last call before the gala, she saw me getting ready to begin and put her hands on my waist.

“Come here,” Shawna told me. “You can do that without starting the fight first.”

I denied the accusation while moving closer.

Now, in the tailor's shop, her phone lit again. I watched her answer while the man knelt beside my shoes.

“Him?” I asked Shawna.

She kept typing. “Rosa.”

I tilted my head toward the screen. “Let me see.”

She held it up. Six gowns lay across a bed in the photograph. Rosa wanted to know whether I was paying for all of it.

I nodded. “Tell her yes.”

Shawna looked back at the picture. “Don't know how much.”

I faced the mirror again. “Capital fucking letters.”

Shawna sent the message, put her phone away, and came around the tailor to straighten my collar. She smoothed one edge, then the other. I watched her hands in the glass.

“You're allowed to be scared tonight,” Shawna told me.

I looked over her head at my reflection. “I'm not.”

She pressed the collar flat. “Okay.”

I looked down at her. “You are?”

Shawna met my eyes. “Yes.”

When she started to step back, I took her hand gently. Dante's watch slid against my fingers. I let it stay there.

“Then I'm scared too,” I told her.

She kissed the corner of my mouth that wasn't cut.

“Good,” Shawna said. “Maybe you'll listen.”

I listened to everything. The difficulty came afterward, when I sometimes decided the world needed correcting for what I'd heard.

At home, Rosa had turned our bedroom into a place where my belongings existed only until a woman needed the space. Shoes occupied the chairs. Perfume hung in the air. A champagne bottle stood in the bathroom sink beside my toothbrush. My pistol had moved from the dresser to a folded hand towel, making room for a heated hairbrush.

I stopped in the doorway. Rosa looked up from a pair of earrings and asked why I was there.

“Sleep here,” I told her.

Rosa pointed toward the hall. “Not while we're busy.”

She wore red with diamonds at her throat. Shawna sat farther inside with her hair being pinned up. I could see one eye in the mirror and the beginning of a smile when Rosa tried to send me away.

I put a hand against the doorframe. “Need Shawna.”

Rosa addressed her reflection. “Your nigga needs patience.”

I lifted my free hand. “Bought some. Arrives tomorrow.”

Rosa picked up a shoe. I retrieved my pistol, secured it beneath the white jacket, and clipped the captain's badge inside its lining. Then I went downstairs before she could explain the shipping options.

Marcellus sat by the windows in a gray suit. His shoes had been polished until they looked wet. We meant to pass him off as a wealthy jeweler under another name. The suit helped. The set of his mouth suggested the tailor had been unable to remove the part of him that wanted Red dragged behind a car.

Celeste's letter lay in its sleeve on the table. He had one palm across it and wasn't reading. I stopped beside the opposite chair.

“Ready?” I asked Marcellus.

He looked out toward the water. “For years.”

I touched the chair back. “Not what I asked.”

He turned toward me. A small smile returned.

“Yes, Aiden,” Marcellus said.

I lowered myself into the chair. My side complained before I finished sitting. That bullet had settled into my life like it intended to pay rent. Marcellus watched me pause until the worst of the pain passed.

“Does she know how much it hurts?” Marcellus asked.

I looked toward the stairs. “Knows everything.”

Marcellus kept his eyes on me. “Does she?”

I turned back. “Enough.”

He folded the letter inside its sleeve and put it into his inner pocket.

“The stone cannot love you back,” Marcellus said.

I rested my hand against my bandage. “Got somebody for that.”

Marcellus smoothed the pocket. “Remember which one you came for.”

Tank entered before I could answer. He held two phones. When he saw us, he turned one facedown against his palm.

“Cars ready,” Tank said.

I looked at the hidden screen. “Who's calling?”

Tank stopped beside the doorway. “Nobody.”

I pointed at his hands. “You need two phones for nobody?”

Tank slid them into his pockets. “In case somebody does.”

On another night I might have laughed. I watched his hands come out empty and thought about asking again.

Then I heard Shawna on the stairs.

I turned.

She wore dark blue, close against her body, with a train following down the steps. Diamonds touched her ears. Dante's watch lay on her wrist. Her hair was up, leaving the little scar above her eyebrow plain to see. I knew that small break in her skin as well as I knew my own hands. I wanted to kiss it before we went anywhere.

Shawna looked down at the dress as she reached the last step.

“It's Rosa's,” Shawna told me. “She never wore it.”

I got to my feet. “Said I'd buy anything.”

She smoothed the skirt. “Wanted this one.”

Rosa lifted the train off the step behind her.

“Want it back,” Rosa said. “Both of you hear?”

Shawna looked over her shoulder. “Yes.”

Her voice was soft. I heard it without giving it the attention I wish I'd given it then.

Rosa came down carrying a small envelope. She showed it to me before putting it into her purse.

“Watch paper,” Rosa said. “Original. Nobody gets it off me.”

Red's order was inside. Shawna had made a copy for the night and put that in her own purse. I watched Rosa close hers over the original and thought about how little paper it took to carry what had happened to Dante. If something went wrong with us, the truth still needed somewhere to be.

I looked back at Shawna because I didn't want to stay with that thought.

“Turn around,” I told her.

She made a slow turn, lifting the train clear of her heel.

I put my hand over my heart. “Again.”

Shawna smiled at me. “Aiden.”

I watched the blue cloth settle. “Trying to remember who I need to thank.”

She put the little silver pistol into her purse beside the copy of the note, then came close and rested both hands against my chest.

“Tear this dress,” Shawna told me, “I'm killing you before Red gets to.”

I touched her waist. “Buy the factory.”

Shawna brought her face closer. “Kill you in the factory.”

I kissed her. Tank cleared his throat behind us. I kissed her again, because that man did not determine how long I was allowed to be happy.

Outside, I counted the cars with one finger.

“I wanted six,” I told Tank.

He pointed to the Lamborghini, the Mercedes, Rosa's car and the green Bentley. “Four drivers.”

“Put somebody in the other two,” I said.

Tank kept his hand raised. “Which somebody?”

I looked toward our people. Shawna held her purse out to me.

“You can drive this,” she said.

I took it. “Four cars. Everybody got a fucking opinion.”

Apparently Tank had chosen the evening of a robbery to begin requiring arithmetic. I carried Shawna's purse to the Lamborghini, opened her door, and returned it when she sat down. Tank took the original black Mercedes. Rosa drove Marcellus, and Porsche brought Mercedes in the green Bentley.

Four cars reached the canopy together. Gold pillars. Flowers taller than the men guarding them. A fountain glittering with coins beneath lights that probably cost more to run than the wishes dropped into the water. I slowed beneath the entrance and looked at the building Red had raised against Marcellus's diamond.

White tie, the invitation said. I stepped out in my white suit with the cross at my chest and the badge inside my jacket. Shawna took my arm.

Cameras flashed.

That surprised me. I'd been preparing for guns.

“Keep walking,” Shawna said close to my ear.

I did, as if I'd bought the pavement and was pleased with the delivery.

A photographer asked us to stop beside a wall of white roses. Shawna put one hand on my shoulder. I settled mine at her waist. Before the flashes ended, a man with a microphone came alongside us and asked whether I was taking over T's interests.

I looked toward the lens. “Anybody owes them, owes me.”

The reporter moved the microphone closer. “And your relationship with Mr. Redmond?”

Redmond. He was using the whole government name for the evening. I respected the effort of dressing that much information for a party.

“Ask at the end,” I told the reporter.

Rosa stepped between him and us before I could improve the answer. Tank came close enough for his jacket to brush mine. He touched my arm.

“Anything happens,” Tank said quietly, “stay with me.”

I looked up at him. “Was the idea.”

He didn't answer. He took his hand away and followed us inside.

A staircase curved around a statue of a naked woman holding a bowl of real fire. I paused long enough to appreciate that. If you intended to waste money, you might as well make the building nervous.

I recognized people all over the room. Women wearing jewels I had seen being offered as security for drugs. Men shaking hands with people I'd heard them threaten across tables. A preacher from television stood beside a man I knew dealt in stolen cars. The preacher wore the larger watch.

Through the open door of a private lounge, I saw champagne buckets and cocaine laid out on a mirror. A man sitting between two women lifted his glass when he noticed me. I had spent a reckless, filthy weekend with all three before I knew Shawna. He looked entirely too pleased to find me dressed for public life.

“Disappear when you fall in love?” the man called to me.

I kept Shawna's hand in mine. “Got promoted.”

The women laughed. Shawna looked from one face to another, then up at me.

“All three?” Shawna asked.

I considered the possibility of improving the truth, then left it alone. “Long weekend.”

Shawna shook her head. “Nasty motherfucker.”

I smiled at her. “Love me.”

Her grip tightened. “Don't get confident.”

I got confident. Enough that when Red appeared on the far staircase in a dark green jacket, I smiled at him as though he'd arrived at a party I was considering letting him enjoy.

His eyes stayed on Shawna.

“There she is,” Red said when he reached us.

He held out both hands. Shawna gave him one. He lifted it and kissed her skin above Dante's watch.

I pictured his head going through the nearest arrangement. His green jacket catching fire in the statue's bowl. The preacher beside the stolen-car man explaining to God why everybody ought to let me finish.

I smiled instead.

“Good party,” I told Red. “Shame about your future.”

He looked at me at last.

“Captain,” Red said. The word came slowly, with his jaw tightening around it. “You clean up well.”

I met his eyes. “You still talking.”

Shawna's fingers tightened against my arm. Red laughed softly and turned toward a photographer.

“All three of us,” Red called. “Get a photograph.”

He guided Shawna into the middle before I could answer and placed a hand against her upper arm. A broad, dark ring sat on that hand, a blue stone set almost level with the surface. For a moment the green sleeve pulled back enough to show it clearly.

Marcellus had been approaching a few feet behind us with Rosa. When Red put his hand on Shawna, the old man stopped. I couldn't tell what he was looking at from where I stood.

The flash went off.

Red asked for another. I looked straight into the lens. If that picture ever reached a museum, I wanted visitors to have no trouble identifying which man had come with a plan to ruin the evening.

An attendant returned with small glossy copies while Red greeted Rosa. She took two and put them into her purse. Her smile stayed in place while the papers went inside. I watched her fingers close around the clasp before she let go.

Red turned back to Shawna and offered his arm.

“Promised this woman a dance,” Red said.

Shawna looked at me.

I remembered the plan. I remembered helping make it. She needed him close, distracted, willing to believe he'd already taken her back from me.

I also remembered having hands.

“One dance,” I told Red.

He smiled at Shawna. “See what she wants.”

She took his arm. I let her hand leave mine.

Then he led her onto the floor, and I followed with my eyes as if somebody had carried my heart into traffic and asked me to admire how carefully he was driving.

## 11. Blue Mercy

Shawna knew the song. She told me that later. What I saw that night was the change in her face when it began.

The band played its first few notes. Her head lifted slightly, then her eyes lowered. Red leaned close to her ear and said something I couldn't hear. She looked down at their joined hands.

I wanted to tear the music out of the speakers.

They moved beneath the chandelier, his green sleeve beside her blue dress. He held her at a distance nobody running a ballroom could have objected to. I objected to every inch of it.

Tank brought me a drink.

“This one's for drinking,” Tank told me. “Don't throw it.”

I took the glass. “Bring it where I got hands, what you expect?”

Tank kept his own glass close. “You might surprise me once. I keep hoping.”

I swallowed half. Across the room, Marcellus spoke with an older woman whose necklace looked heavy enough to place her under arrest gradually. He nodded at something she said and touched the inner pocket of his gray jacket. I remembered Celeste's letter inside it. I knew what he'd said about wanting Red dead.

At least I wasn't the only man in the room finding the evening difficult to admire.

Red turned Shawna. Her skirt swept around his legs. She smiled at something he said.

“Nigga,” I told Tank.

Tank followed my gaze. “I see.”

I kept looking. “Nigga.”

Tank nodded once. “I know.”

I turned toward him. “No, you don't.”

He drank his entire glass.

The man who interrupted wore six championship rings and a gold mouthguard hanging from a chain. I heard him say my name before I turned and saw his chest occupying the space a conversation should have needed.

“Captain Aiden,” he said.

I looked up. “Depends who's bleeding.”

He held his ringed hand near his waist. “Rex Crown.”

I knew the face from television. Forty-five, heavyweight champion twice, ex-champion three times. He had made a respectable living losing something and arranging to be paid while he tried to get it back.

Tank inspected the rings. “Bring the whole display?”

Rex kept his eyes on me. “Heard you think you can fight.”

I lifted my glass toward him. “Heard you get paid. Congratulations. Have a drink.”

Rex stepped closer. “I'm asking you.”

His friends spread behind him in a loose half-circle. Mercedes and Porsche had joined the people watching. Mercedes was already smiling. If I had been Rex, I would have spent a moment wondering why she looked so pleased that I'd come over.

One of his friends pointed at me. “Champ asked you something.”

Mercedes looked at the pointing hand. “My nigga answered. Want him to sing it? He plays piano too.”

Tank nodded. “Ruined a breakfast.”

Rex moved his head from side to side, loosening his neck. “Five thousand says I put you down before your woman finishes dancing.”

I looked past him. Red had turned Shawna again. His hand rested at her back. I watched it long enough for Tank to say my name quietly.

I set the drink down on a table beside us.

“Five?” I asked Rex. “You're famous. Don't give me the introductory offer.”

Rex lifted his chin. “Twenty.”

I turned toward Mercedes. “What did a front-row seat cost when this nigga used to win?”

Mercedes looked at Rex's rings. “More than he can lose, apparently.”

Rex's voice rose. “A hundred thousand.”

A friend caught his sleeve. Rex shook the hand off. I watched the man try once more before surrendering the suit to its owner's decisions.

Porsche put her drink on the table beside mine. “Anybody hear that besides me?”

Rex looked around the circle. “Everybody heard.”

Porsche nodded toward Mercedes. “Find a pen.”

I began taking off my jacket.

“Hold this,” I told Tank.

He looked at the bandage showing beneath my shirt. “Your side.”

I gave him the sleeve. “Hold the good side.”

Tank let the coat hang between us. “Your jacket wasn't shot. You were.”

I pulled my arm free carefully. “Then it's had an easier week. Let it help.”

The lining turned outward, exposing the badge. Rex looked at it and paused.

“You're police?” Rex asked.

Tank folded the jacket over his arm. “You called him Captain when you walked up. What you think he captains? Breakfast?”

The men behind Rex cheered him on. He looked back at them, then faced me again and rolled his shoulders.

“Said a hundred,” Rex reminded me.

Mercedes held out a pen she'd found. “Heard you. Save some breath for after counting.”

I smiled at him. He had bought the room's attention. I was willing to let him use it.

People made space beside the tall windows. A waiter rolled the champagne table back, leaving a chair against the wall. Rex removed his rings one by one and put them in his jacket before handing it to a friend. The band kept playing. Across the floor, Red turned toward us and smiled. Shawna looked over his shoulder and shook her head once.

I held up one finger.

She closed her eyes. When she opened them, I was still there, providing exactly the problem she'd warned me about.

Rex took the mouthguard off its chain and put it between his teeth.

“Jewelry he can chew,” Tank murmured beside me.

Mercedes bent forward laughing.

Rex swung.

It was a proper punch. His shoulder moved behind it, his weight came forward, and the fist traveled straight toward my face without any of the warning a drunk man's arm gives you. I stepped close enough for it to pass my cheek. Air touched the split in my lip.

Then I hit him beneath the ribs.

The blow made less noise than I expected. Rex made more.

He folded forward with a broken breath. I caught the back of his collar before his head reached the champagne table. His hands came toward my arm, then fell away while he tried to straighten. I felt his weight pull against my side and held on just long enough to turn him toward a chair.

The crowd had gone quiet.

“Nigga,” Porsche whispered.

Mercedes answered beside her. “Nigga.”

Tank looked down at Rex. “That's about the whole sentence.”

I lowered the champion into the chair. He braced his hands on his knees. One friend checked his watch, looked toward the band, then lowered his wrist as if neither one had explained anything.

I leaned close enough for Rex to hear. “Before the dance, you said. Still got two verses. Enjoy them.”

The circle erupted. People came over from the far tables. A man pushed his phone toward me. A woman touched my shoulder and said she'd watched every one of Rex's title fights and had never seen him sit so politely.

Rex drew a full breath at last.

“Again,” Rex said.

I watched him struggle to lift his head. “Breathe first.”

Rex looked up at me. “Again.”

I put a hand on the back of the empty chair beside him. “Rematch or hospital bracelet?”

His jaw worked around the mouthguard. He took it out, held it in his palm, and stared at it before looking at me again.

“What the fuck are you?” Rex asked.

I took my jacket from Tank. “Her man.”

I looked toward Shawna as I said it. Several heads turned with mine. She and Red had stopped at the edge of the floor. Her hand remained in his, but she was looking straight at me and smiling. Red turned to see what she was watching. His own smile disappeared.

I put the jacket back on. The movement hurt. I did it slowly enough that only Tank, standing beside me, reached to help the sleeve find my hand.

Rex took out a checkbook. Mercedes gave him the pen. His writing hand trembled, but he filled the line, signed his name, and held it up.

“I don't welch,” Rex said.

I took the check and passed it straight to Mercedes.

“You and Porsche,” I told her. “Got our car out that first night.”

Mercedes looked at the amount. “That's a hundred thousand dollars.”

I reached halfway toward it. “Want it smaller?”

She pulled it against her chest. “Don't you fucking dare.”

Mercedes kissed one cheek. Porsche kissed the other. Rex watched from his chair, breathing carefully while people laughed around him.

Then a thin man behind him drew a gun.

Silver jacket. Tight mouth. I had seen him watching while Rex wrote the check. Now he pointed the pistol toward Mercedes's hand.

“Give it back,” the man in silver said.

Mercedes stepped behind my shoulder. I looked at the gun, then at Rex.

“Yours?” I asked Rex.

Rex pushed himself out of the chair. “Put it away, Silas.”

Silas kept the muzzle raised. “He made you look—”

Rex stepped toward him. “He beat me. Put it away.”

Silas didn't lower it. I caught his wrist while Tank caught his shoulder. The gun went off above us. Crystal burst from a small chandelier and fell across the table. People ducked. Tank drove Silas downward while I kept his wrist turned away from the crowd.

His knees struck the carpet. Then his shoulder. The gun slid from his hand. Porsche kicked it beneath the edge of an empty table and put her shoe on it.

Mercedes crouched, put the check safely inside her purse, and hit Silas with the purse before standing again.

“You made me bend down,” Mercedes told him.

Rex stood over him with the mouthguard still in his hand.

“Pull a gun because I lost?” Rex asked.

Silas looked up from the floor. “I was helping.”

Rex pointed at the broken chandelier. “You was ruining my fucking reputation twice.”

Two of Knox's officers came in from the lobby. I recognized them from the cars outside. One saw the badge inside my open jacket and looked from it to the man Tank was holding down.

“Captain?” the officer asked.

I released Silas's wrist as the officer took it. “His evening's over.”

Porsche lifted her foot when the second officer reached for the pistol. Silas was cuffed and led away while the band found its place after one frightened pause. Glass still glittered across an empty table beside Rex's chair.

Rex offered me his hand.

“Ever need somebody in your corner,” Rex said.

I shook it. “Stay out of mine first.”

He laughed once, stopped because it hurt, then bowed his head. I watched him go toward the bar holding his body carefully, the way I'd been holding mine for days.

When I turned back, Shawna was dancing with Red again.

I'd beaten a heavyweight, given my friends a hundred thousand dollars and sent one fool away in handcuffs. The band was still working through the same song. None of it had changed the thing I'd been watching before Rex arrived.

Shawna told me later what Red had been saying on that floor. I asked for every word. Some I had to hear twice because the first time I was too busy getting angry to listen properly. The conversation I'm giving you now is hers. That night, I could see their faces and hands from beside the windows, but the music swallowed the words.

She said he began when they passed close to the piano. He bent toward her ear again, his arm drawing her around the next couple.

“You used to sing this upstairs,” Red said.

“I sang a lot of things,” Shawna answered.

His hand moved farther around her waist. “Before Dante.”

Shawna looked up at him. “I had a life before all of you.”

Red brought her through a turn. Her watch touched his cuff, and the blue stone on his hand passed beneath the light.

“You could have that life now,” Red told her.

“Could I?” Shawna asked.

He smiled. “Whatever you want.”

She looked down at the ring. Red rotated his hand inward and took another step, drawing her after him.

“I want him left alone,” Shawna said.

Red's mouth stopped moving with the song. “Already thinking about that nigga?”

“His name is Aiden,” she told him.

Red raised his chin. “I know what the nigga's name is.”

A couple passed close. He smiled at the man; Shawna made herself smile at the woman. She remembered the effort of doing that. I remembered seeing the result from the side of the floor and giving it a very different meaning.

“You said you were tired of running,” Red told her when the couple had passed.

Shawna kept moving. “I am.”

“You said you wanted to come home,” Red said.

She raised her face. “I said I wanted somewhere I could sleep.”

Red lowered his voice. “My bed was never the problem.”

Shawna drew her shoulder back. He kept hold of her. I had seen that small movement without knowing what had caused it; afterward she told me how hard she'd had to work to keep her voice level.

“Don't do that tonight, Shawna,” Red said.

“Then don't talk to me like that,” she answered.

For a moment they stopped, with the other couples turning around them. Red held their joined hands between their bodies.

“Do you know what it felt like?” he asked. “Standing there while he put that ring on you? Paying for a room where you were going to spend the night with him?”

Shawna looked him in the face. “Dante was your friend.”

“Nigga knew,” Red said.

“Knew what?” she asked.

Red lifted their hands a little higher. “That his nigga saw you first.”

When Shawna told me what she said next, she waited until I stopped moving around the room and looked at her.

“You all keep saying that like I was a fucking parking space,” she told Red.

I didn't need her to repeat that one.

She said his fingers tightened, then loosened. Another guest raised a glass toward them, and Red smiled back before he began dancing again.

“I could've given you everything,” he told Shawna.

She waited until they'd passed the next couple. “Then give me the diamond.”

Red slowed. Shawna kept moving, bringing his arm with her until he followed.

“That's what tonight is for,” she said. “You wanted me to know how serious you were. Show me.”

Red looked across the room. “And Aiden?”

“You let him walk out,” Shawna said.

Red kept looking at me. “Will he?”

She placed her palm against his cheek. “He will if I tell him to.”

Back at the gala, watching without any of those words, I saw her palm on his face and his head leaning toward it. I hadn't heard her ask for the stone or demand that he leave me alive. I'd seen her touch him and supplied the rest myself.

I retrieved the glass from the table, finished the drink Tank had brought me, and put it down again.

Then I went onto the floor.

Tank said my name. Rosa stepped toward me. I went around her before she could put a hand on my arm. I told myself I'd only take Shawna aside for a moment, check whether she was all right, make sure Red remembered I was still standing in the room.

“Dance is over,” I told Red when I reached them.

He kept moving, drawing Shawna through another step. “You hear the band? The band's still playing.”

I put my hand on his shoulder.

He stopped then.

Shawna looked at my hand, then at my face. She shook her head very slightly. I saw the warning and decided there must be some loving reason for me to ignore it.

Red's voice stayed low. “Take that off me.”

I held his shoulder. “Yours comes off her first.”

Three men in dark jackets moved from the edge of the room. Tank came after me. The singer missed a word. The band carried the tune beneath the little gap, and I felt every face around us beginning to turn.

Shawna stepped between me and Red.

“Go sit down,” Shawna told me.

I stared at her. “What?”

She kept her eyes on mine. “You're embarrassing me.”

We'd agreed she might need to hurt my feelings in front of him. Earlier that afternoon, at the bedroom mirror, we'd even discussed a slap if words weren't enough. She had refused twice. I had told her I could take one if it made the act convincing and got her close enough to finish the plan. I remembered her standing with her hand against the mirror's edge, asking if I was sure.

I'd said yes.

Now I stood in front of her, surprised to discover that the feelings I'd volunteered had arrived armed.

“Mean that?” I asked Shawna.

She gave me the one slap we'd agreed on.

The sound cracked across the music. My face turned with it. When I looked back, her hand shook beside her dress.

“I told you to listen,” Shawna said.

Her eyes were bright. I believed there was something real in the anger; I had just walked through her warning and put my hand on the man she needed to keep calm. Red stood beside her with his mouth softening into a smile.

I stepped back.

Shawna held my eyes for one more second, then turned away from me.

“Let's finish our dance,” she told Red.

He took her hand, and they moved off together. I stood in the space they'd left with people watching from every side. I had never had less need of my gun or more desire to touch it.

Tank caught my sleeve.

“Come on,” Tank said.

I looked at his hand. “Don't.”

Tank stepped between me and the dance floor. “Come on.”

I let him lead me to the side of the room. I could see the carpet beneath my feet; I couldn't seem to choose where on it I ought to stand. Rosa came up beside us.

“She just kept you alive,” Rosa told me.

I looked down. “I know.”

Rosa waited until I raised my head. “Then act like you know.”

My cheek burned. My side burned. The people I loved had found different places on me to leave the cost of being near them. I knew that wasn't the whole truth. It was the part I could feel while I stood beside Tank and watched Red kiss Shawna's hand again.

This time I stayed where I was.

At nine, Red stopped the music.

He went onto a low stage with Shawna beside him. Attendants opened a curtain behind them. A glass case stood under a white light, and inside it lay the blue necklace he was presenting as Blue Mercy.

Three days of stories had brought me to that little patch of blue. Forty million dollars. A marriage. A prison. Dante dying in the desert. I had expected the thing to stand up and introduce itself.

It was smaller than I expected.

That made it worse. A small bright object in a velvet cradle, with all those lives wrapped around it. I looked at Marcellus. His mouth had opened slightly. One hand rested against the front of his coat. His eyes stayed on the case.

Red lifted the microphone.

“Some things wait a long time to come home,” Red told the room.

He looked at Shawna. People clapped at the pause he'd left for them. I gripped my empty glass, realized I had picked it up again without thinking, and put it down before Tank could do it for me.

“Tonight isn't an auction,” Red continued. “Nobody here can afford what this means to me.”

A man near the stage laughed loudly. Red kept his eyes on Shawna.

“Tonight is a promise,” Red said.

He raised her hand. Dante's watch slid up her forearm. Tank said something under his breath beside me, but I couldn't make out the words and didn't ask.

Marcellus took the small jeweler's case from Rosa and moved toward the stage. Red's men let him approach. An attendant introduced him under the name we had arranged, describing him as a jeweler. Marcellus inclined his head and waited while Red looked him over.

“You approve?” Red asked him.

Marcellus kept his eyes on the glass. “I would like to see it closer.”

Red lowered the microphone from his mouth, though I could still hear him from where we stood. “Later.”

Marcellus set his case beside his leg. “A piece this old deserves—”

Red cut him off. “Later.”

The ring on Red's hand flashed blue as he lowered that hand. Marcellus looked at it, then returned his eyes to the case.

Shawna turned toward the crowd and found me. She held my gaze. I nodded once, trying to give her something useful after the trouble I'd made on the dance floor.

It felt like swallowing a knife.

After the presentation, an attendant came to find me.

“Mr. Redmond would like you upstairs,” the attendant said.

I looked toward the stage. “Can come get me.”

Tank touched my arm. The attendant waited.

Shawna stood beside Rosa near the steps. Rosa had a hand against her back. They both looked over when the attendant repeated the invitation, and Shawna nodded at me.

Fine. Red wanted privacy. I was tired of watching him perform ownership under lights somebody had chosen to flatter it.

Tank came with me. Marcellus picked up his case and joined us. The attendant began to object to the old man, so I stopped at the foot of the stairs.

“If he's giving away forty million,” I told the attendant, “he can let a jeweler look.”

The man looked toward Marcellus's case, then led us up without another argument.

Red's office stood above the ballroom. I could hear the party through the floor. The door opened onto dark wood, tall windows, and a desk wide enough to lay a king out on without troubling his shoes.

Red waited behind it. A second door at the far side of the room was just closing. Shawna moved away from that doorway toward him, carrying a small blue box. They had brought her up ahead of us.

“I thought you'd prefer privacy,” Red said.

I stopped short of the desk. “For what?”

Red rested both hands on the polished wood. “The end.”

Shawna took a step toward me. Red put one hand on her shoulder, and she stopped.

“Tell him,” Red said to her.

Her face was pale. Her fingers tightened beneath the little box.

“Take it, Aiden,” Shawna told me.

I held my hand toward her instead. “Come here.”

She extended the box. “Take it.”

I took it because her hands shook and, for that moment, stopping the shake mattered more to me than asking the right question.

Inside lay the blue jewel in the mounting I'd seen beneath the ballroom's light.

Red watched me open it.

“Forty million,” Red said. “Enough to start over anywhere.”

I looked up from the velvet. “You think I'm selling her?”

Red smiled. “I think she just sold you.”

Marcellus stepped toward the box. Red raised one finger, and two men appeared at the office door behind us. I heard their shoes before I looked back and saw the dark jackets. Marcellus stopped with his hand beside his case.

Shawna looked at the men, at Red, then back at me. Something about the plan had changed. We had meant to get close enough to take his diamond, and here he was placing it into my hands without asking another question. I couldn't make the ease of it fit the man I'd met at breakfast.

Red opened a desk drawer.

“Since your friends enjoy history,” Red said.

He took out a flat leather folder and laid it in front of me. A white label on its cover read DANTE.

Shawna made a small sound. I looked at her, then set the blue box against my left palm so I could reach for the folder with my right.

Red let me take it.

That was when I knew we were in serious fucking trouble.

## 12. The Money at the Bottom

The folder smelled of leather and old cigarettes. I laid it open on the visitors' side of Red's desk, keeping the blue box in my left hand while I turned the pages with my right. Dates on the first page. Amounts and names on the second, arranged in clean little columns.

Shawna leaned close enough to read beside me. For a second her shoulder touched mine. I remembered that touch afterward much more clearly than I remembered any of the numbers.

On the third page, beside Valentino's name, somebody had written *settled* in red ink.

Underneath was a copy of the order from Dante's watch.

Red had kept his own.

I looked across the desk. “Nigga, you sick motherfucker.”

Red's hands rested flat on the wood. “Careful where you point that mouth.”

Shawna took the loose pages from the folder. She found the order, laid the others back on the leather and turned the single sheet over. Its blank back faced me for a moment. She turned it again and held it closer.

I had watched her read the original at our table. Knowing the words hadn't prepared me for finding a clean copy here, in the same drawer a man might keep stamps. I couldn't imagine what it did to her. Her hand began shaking hard enough that the edge of the paper tapped against the leather folder.

“You kept this?” Shawna asked Red.

He looked toward Tank. “T believed in records. Nigga kept everything.”

Shawna moved the paper into his line of sight. “You're sitting behind the desk.”

Red turned back to her. “Shawna.”

She raised her voice. “You're sitting behind the fucking desk.”
