She threw the page at his chest. It caught his jacket and slid down onto the blotter. He looked at it without moving his hands, then raised his eyes to me.

“I was going to make this generous,” Red said.

I reached across his desk, took the order off the blotter and put it back with the other pages. “Try shutting your mouth.”

Tank moved up beside my shoulder. Both men at the outer door shifted forward. I heard a shoe scrape behind me and looked back long enough to see one of them watching Tank's hands.

When I faced Red again, he was smiling.

“Ask your man what generosity looks like,” Red told me.

Tank stopped moving.

I turned toward him. “What the fuck is he talking about?”

Tank said my name.

I waited. He looked past me toward the desk. That was all he did, and I felt something cold open beneath my ribs before I knew what belonged there.

“Nigga,” I said to Tank. “What?”

Red picked up a remote. A screen on the wall beside his desk lit up with a wide, grainy view of an empty swimming pool.

I knew the pale motel wall behind it. I knew the balcony rail and the blue tiles at the deep end. My body recognized the place before I saw the money: the ache in my side sharpened, and I caught myself taking the short breaths I'd taken against that fence.

Then Tank came into the picture.

Our Mercedes was already gone. T lay near the fence. The recording had no sound I could hear, just the quiet room around me and Tank on the screen, stepping down into the pool with a bag.

He bent over a bundle of money.

He picked it up.

I watched him put it inside the bag, turn, and reach for another.

The terrible thing about a recording is that it doesn't give a fuck when you've understood. It keeps going. The man I'd asked to help me was still bending and gathering and stepping carefully around the place where I'd nearly died.

“Turn it off,” I told Red.

On the screen, Tank climbed out with a full bag. He came back with another. One loose bundle lay beside T's shoe. Tank leaned over and took that too.

“You heard enough breathing in this room,” I said to Red. “Turn it the fuck off.”

Red pressed the remote. The screen went dark. I could hear his thumbnail clicking against the remote and Shawna trying to finish a breath. The little box creaked in my hand.

“Three million,” Red said. “All of it. Your friend is thorough.”

I turned toward Tank. He looked back at me. His arms hung loose beside his jacket.

“You took her money?” I asked him.

Tank swallowed. “I took it out the pool. I'm standing here saying that.”

I stepped closer. “Don't play with me.”

“I'm not,” Tank said.

I could smell the peppermint he'd been eating downstairs. I hated that ordinary smell. Those hands had brought me clothes, held doors, steadied me when I couldn't stand. On the screen they'd moved just as carefully around the blood beside the pool. I couldn't make the two things fit, and he was standing close enough to hit.

“You watched her hold me together,” I told Tank. “You saw her drive off with me bleeding. Then you went back for the fucking money?”

His jaw moved before the word came out.

“Yes,” Tank said.

I hit him with my right hand.

His head turned. Pain went through my side so hard my knees bent with it. I caught my balance and hit him again. He rocked back half a step, then came upright with blood on his lip.

I still held the blue box in my left hand. It seemed impossible that I'd remembered how to keep something so useless from falling.

“Look at me and hit me back,” I told Tank.

Tank looked at me. “No.”

I raised my fist again. “Hit me the fuck back.”

Shawna caught my wrist with both hands. I felt her weight against my arm before I looked down and saw her there.

Tank wiped his mouth. “I'm not hitting you.”

Red leaned against the far edge of his desk. “Touching.”

I turned toward him. Shawna's grip tightened.

“You say one more word,” I told Red.

He glanced at the box. “And what? You'll die rich?”

I looked down at the jewel. It lay where it had been, blue and bright in a velvet bed, having contributed absolutely fucking nothing to the evening except reasons for everybody to behave worse.

I wanted to throw it through his face.

Marcellus stepped close enough that his shoulder brushed mine. He bent toward the open box under the lamp. His eyes narrowed, then lifted quickly toward Red.

“Aiden,” Marcellus said.

I looked at him.

“Close it,” he told me.

I kept the lid open. “What?”

Marcellus put two fingers against its edge. “Close the box.”

I let the lid meet his fingers, and he withdrew them. He looked into my face. Whatever he'd seen, I took that look to mean he wanted to discuss it somewhere else.

Behind me, Tank spoke again.

“It's at your house,” Tank said. “Back of my closet. I moved it when we moved in.”

Shawna let go of my wrist and turned toward him.

“All of it?” she asked.

Tank nodded. “Every fucking dollar.”

Shawna stood very still. “Why didn't you tell us?”

Tank looked at the floor. I stepped toward him, and he raised his head before I reached him.

“I thought you was going to leave,” Tank said.

I stopped. “What?”

He rubbed his wet mouth with the back of his hand. “You got her. T was dead. You had the car. Then you started talking about water and houses, taking her anywhere. I thought you was gone.”

“I was fucking dying,” I told him.

Tank's eyes shone. “I know.”

I leaned toward him. “Do you?”

I'd seen Tank angry, drunk, half asleep in a doorway, laughing with food in his mouth. I hadn't seen him stand with tears gathering while he waited for me to decide whether to hit him again.

It made me angrier. I needed him solid. I needed something I could strike until the room stopped changing shape.

“I thought if you left, I'd have something,” Tank told me. “That's it. That's the whole thing.”

Red made a little impatient sound behind us.

“Tell him the rest,” Red said.

Tank looked past me. “You called me.”

Red nodded. “You answered.”

Tank took a breath through his nose. “You sent me that video.”

Red's smile came back. “And you answered again.”

Shawna moved beside me. “You told him where we were?”

Tank turned to her immediately. “No.”

She said his name and waited.

“No, Shawna,” Tank told her. “I didn't tell him about the house by the lake. Didn't give him your plans. He knew you was coming here because you fucking told him.”

Red straightened. “I offered him his place back.”

I kept my eyes on Tank. “What place?”

Red answered from behind me. “Yours.”

I heard the band through the floor. It had begun another song. Somebody downstairs laughed, and the ordinary sound reached the office like proof of a world none of us could get back into.

I thought about Tank sitting outside my bedroom. The spare phone. The way he'd watched me call men who used to answer to T, men who now waited for me to finish speaking before they asked what I wanted done.

“You want it?” I asked Tank.

He said nothing.

I stood closer. “I'm right here. You want it?”

His eyes dropped toward my waist. I followed them and saw a red mark spreading through the side of my white suit. I hadn't felt the wetness. Now that I could see it, the cloth seemed to cling to the wound.

Tank shook his head.

“I wanted you to ask me to stay,” Tank said. “Me. Not whoever drove the car.”

I'd been ready to hear he'd sold us. I'd been preparing my answer with my fists. I wasn't ready for him to stand there bleeding and tell me he wanted to stay in my house.

I didn't have an answer for that.

Red opened a drawer and tossed a car key onto the desk. It skidded across the blotter and stopped beside the open folder.

“Still time for an intelligent decision,” Red told Tank. “Bring me my money. Walk out with a future.”

Tank looked at the key.

He reached past me and picked it up.

My hands went cold.

Then he turned and threw it at Red's window.

The key struck with a sharp crack. A white star spread through the glass, but the pane held. The key bounced back and landed on the carpet beside the desk.

Tank watched it settle, then looked at Red.

“Fuck your car too,” Tank said.

The nearest guard grabbed Tank's shoulder. Tank spun and drove him into the wall. A framed photograph jumped sideways on its hook. The second guard reached inside his jacket, and Shawna snatched the lamp off the desk with both hands.

She brought it down across his forearm.

The bulb burst. For an instant the shade lit from within; then the office lost its warm pool of light and the windows reflected only the ceiling lamps. The guard shouted and bent his arm against his chest.

Red came around the desk while Shawna still held the broken lamp. He caught her from behind and pulled her against him. The lamp fell between their feet. I moved toward them.

The guard Tank had thrown into the wall twisted one arm free. I saw the pistol only as its heavy butt struck my wounded side.

The room left my lungs.

I folded against the desk. Loose pages slid under my right palm. I saw Shawna's shoes scrape the floor while Red hauled her toward the inner door. One heel came off. She kicked backward with the bare foot and twisted hard enough to knock a photograph from a shelf.

“Let her go,” I said.

There wasn't enough breath behind it to reach the doorway.

I pushed against the desk. “Let her fucking go.”

Tank tore the guard away from me. Marcellus caught my sleeve and helped me straighten. I pressed the loose papers together, closed the folder around them and gripped it in my right hand. The box stayed clenched in my left. I wasn't making sensible decisions about evidence. I was holding things because I couldn't stand the thought of losing anything else.

A gun went off.

Wood jumped from the desk beside my hand. I jerked back and saw Red at the inner doorway with his pistol pressed against Shawna. Smoke hung briefly in the light between us.

Downstairs, a scream cut through the band. More voices followed. The music stopped.

Tank stopped with one guard held against the wall. The man Shawna had hit cradled his injured arm and stayed where he was.

“There,” Red said. “You can learn.”

Shawna's hair had come loose. One earring was missing. Red's forearm lay across the upper part of her chest; his other hand held the gun against her body. She looked at me over his arm. Her eyes were wet, but she kept her mouth shut tight.

I didn't know what she was trying to tell me. I knew only that I could see her breathing and that he had a hand on the thing that could stop it.

“Take me,” I told Red.

His eyebrows rose. “Nigga, I don't want you.”

I moved one foot forward. “Let her walk. I'll stay.”

Red drew her tighter. “I heard you.”

Shawna shook her head once, hard.

“Aiden,” she said.

I couldn't look away from the gun.

“Take it and go,” Shawna told me.

I looked into her face. “I'm not leaving you.”

Her voice broke on the next word. “Go.”

The inner door opened wider. A third man waited beyond it. Shawna looked over her shoulder, then back past me toward Tank.

“Get him out,” she told Tank.

Red pulled her through the doorway.

I went after them.

Tank let the guard drop and caught me around the chest before I reached the desk's far corner. I struck him with my elbow, then with the fist holding the folder. Its leather edge scraped his face. I told him I'd kill him. I meant it with everything I had left, and he kept dragging me toward the outer door.

Behind us, a man shouted. A shot struck the wall near the inner doorway. I couldn't see whose gun had fired. Tank turned his body between me and the sound and pulled harder.

Marcellus reached the hall first. Rosa appeared there with her red gown gathered in one hand so she could climb the last steps. She looked at us, then into the room behind us.

“Where is she?” Rosa asked.

Marcellus caught her wrist. “He has her.”

Rosa tried to pull free and go past him.

“Not this way,” Marcellus told her.

Tank hauled me into the hall. I could still hear Shawna beyond the inner door. Then another door shut somewhere farther inside, and I couldn't hear her at all.

By the time we reached the ballroom stairs, people were pressing toward the exit below us. Chairs lay in their path. A waiter tried to move one and nearly disappeared beneath the crowd pushing around him.

One of Red's men pulled the ballroom doors shut and put his back against them.

I saw him do it. A room full of people trying to get away from gunfire, and that motherfucker decided the most urgent thing in the building was the fact that nobody had given them permission.

“Open them!” Rosa shouted from the stairs.

He raised his gun toward her.

Tank pulled me down behind the banister. The shot struck the wall over our heads and showered pale dust onto Rosa's hair. Marcellus drew her against the stairwell wall.

I could still hear Shawna telling me to go. I'd brought the box and the papers. I'd left the only person in that office I wanted, and now a fool downstairs was aiming at Rosa as if the evening still had a seating plan.

I shoved the blue box into the deep inner pocket of my jacket. The folder went inside my waistband, flat against my stomach. It hurt to bend far enough to put it there. Everything hurt. I was beginning to resent the variety.

“Put me down,” I told Tank.

His grip tightened. “Aiden.”

I caught his wrist. “I ain't going back upstairs. I'm getting these people out. Put me on my fucking feet.”

Tank lowered me until my shoes touched the step. I held the rail while the room finished tilting. Then I took my pistol from its holster and looked over the banister.

“Police!” I shouted toward the doors.

The guard laughed.

I opened my jacket with my free hand. The badge caught the ballroom light.

“Captain, nigga,” I told him. “Try your joke with the right title.”

His eyes went from the badge to the pistol. For half a second I thought intelligence might have reached him in time.

Then he fired again.

I went over the low banister beside the last few steps. My shoes hit the carpet, my knee followed, and I dropped behind a table while the shot struck wood above me. Tank came down the remaining steps with his own gun drawn. He fired toward the doorway, forcing the guard behind a planter beside it.

People crawled away from the line between them. A woman in diamonds caught my sleeve.

“Are we going to die?” she asked me.

I looked past her shoulder to make sure her husband was down. “Not with that nigga shooting. Behind the table. Both of you.”

She crawled. Her husband followed so closely that he lost a shoe and didn't even pause to negotiate with it.

A second guard came through a side corridor near the bar. I saw his arm before the rest of him. He was bringing his gun around the corner toward me when Rex Crown hit him from behind.

They crashed into a champagne tower.

Glass fell in a bright waterfall around their shoulders. Rex drove the guard across the table and into the wall, holding on with both enormous arms. The champagne ran down his back. If the man wanted a rematch with anybody, he had chosen a much better opponent.

“Captain!” Rex shouted.

The first guard rose behind his planter. I fired toward him, well clear of Rex on my other side, and he dropped out of sight again. Tank reached the door and hauled it open. Guests began running through to the lobby.

Rex pulled the second guard to the floor. The guard twisted beneath him and struck him across the face with the pistol. Rex's head snapped sideways. His grip opened, and the man rolled free onto one knee.

I crossed the gap between our table and the bar before he could stand.

He raised the gun toward Rex. I shoved my own pistol into its holster as I came up beside him, caught his gun wrist with my right hand and drove it away from Rex's head. With my left, I pulled the blue box from my jacket.

Then I hit him beside the temple with it.

Forty million dollars made a solid fucking sound. I would learn soon enough that it wasn't worth forty million. At that moment it was worth exactly one living boxer.

The guard fell onto his side. I set my foot on his gun hand until his fingers opened, then kicked the pistol deep beneath the bar. I put the box back inside my jacket and offered Rex my free hand.

Blood ran from his eyebrow.

“Twice,” Rex said, looking up at me.

I braced myself while he got his feet beneath him. “Keeping score now?”

Rex put a hand on the bar. “You beat me. Then you saved me.”

I glanced toward the open doors. “Keep up. Night ain't over.”

He laughed with blood on his teeth, put his shoulder beneath my good arm, and helped me toward the exit. Tank held the ballroom door. Rosa pushed Marcellus into the passage ahead of her. I could see Porsche beyond the lobby, waving people toward the street.

Then metal ground against stone above the staircase.

I looked up.

The bronze woman holding the bowl of fire had tilted forward on her base. Something had struck it; I hadn't seen what. Burning liquid spilled down her arms and fell onto the flowers below. Flame climbed through the dry arrangement in a sudden sheet.

Mercedes stood beneath her, trying to pull a fallen man to his feet.

“Mercedes, move!” I shouted.

She looked up. The statue began to fall.

I pulled free of Rex and ran the few steps back toward her. The bronze woman's arm struck the banister. Wood snapped. Mercedes had one hand beneath the man's shoulder, his other arm still dragging behind him.

I drove into both of them from the side, catching Mercedes around the waist and shoving the man ahead of us through the open passage. His shoes slid. We fell together beyond the doorway as the statue struck the lower stairs behind us.

The bowl flew off. Burning liquid splashed across the carpet we'd just left. Part of the broken banister crashed down after it.

Heat rolled over my back. Pain spread through my side so completely that, for a moment, I couldn't tell where the bullet was anymore. It felt like the whole fucking thing had gone into business across my body.

Mercedes rolled toward me and caught my face in both hands.

“Nigga,” Mercedes said. “Nigga, you got me?”

I tried to draw breath. “Got you.”

She gripped my cheeks. “You sure?”

I looked down at her. “Baby, count your fucking legs.”

She did. Two legs. One shoe. A dress she was going to mention for the rest of my natural life.

Then she kissed my forehead hard enough that I thought the bullet might come out from gratitude.

“You beautiful, stupid, magnificent motherfucker,” Mercedes told me.

The man we'd pushed clear sat against the wall, coughing and looking at the burning doorway. I saw him move both arms. Mercedes reached for him again while I tried to stand.

I couldn't do it the first time.

Rex and Tank reached me together. Each took an arm, and between them they got me up. The pistol still pressed against my waist; the blue box weighed down my jacket. Mercedes stayed beside the other man, helping him stand against the wall while we cleared the passage.

People had found their way through the passage. Porsche held the outer lobby door open with one leg and shouted directions at a frightened crowd that, for once in its expensive fucking life, followed instructions.

The woman in diamonds stopped beside us. Her husband stood behind her with one bare foot. She took my hand and pressed her wedding ring into it.

“For saving us,” she told me.

I pushed it back into her palm. “Keep your husband nervous. I got a woman waiting.”

She laughed through her tears. Her husband looked at the ring returning to her hand and nodded at me with an intensity I understood perfectly.

Then the cold air touched my face.

My own woman wasn't waiting outside.

The people around us kept moving, calling for friends, touching one another to make sure they had come out whole. Somebody called me Captain. Somebody else tried to shake the hand Tank was using to keep me upright.

I turned toward the building.

Tank caught my shoulder. “Not that way.”

I looked at the lit windows above the ballroom. “She's in there.”

Tank stepped in front of me. His mouth had swollen where I'd hit it. “She told me to get you out. You're alive enough to go get her somewhere else. That's what we got left.”

I didn't forgive him beside that car. I didn't decide the money didn't matter. I let him take my weight because I couldn't stand without help, and I needed to stay alive long enough to find her.

Rex helped him lower me into our Mercedes. Tank went around to drive. The police Lamborghini stayed at the Saint with Knox's officers; one of them told Tank they'd bring it back when the way was clear.

Rosa came to my open window before getting into another car with Marcellus. She pushed one of the gala photographs into my hand.

“She gave me this,” Rosa said.

Shawna stood between me and Red in the picture. His ring caught the light beside her arm. My hand rested at her waist. I looked at my own face, at how sure I'd been that standing close was the same as keeping her.

Rosa touched the edge of the picture.

“Told me to keep one for when you two got married,” she said.

I couldn't look up at her.

Tank started the car.

We hadn't gone far when Marcellus called. I balanced the photograph against my thigh, took out the phone and put him on speaker. Then I set it in the console between us.

“The stone,” Marcellus said. “Aiden, listen carefully. The one you have is the duplicate.”

Tank glanced toward me before looking back at the road.

I took out the blue box and opened it beneath the dashboard light. My fingertips left a red mark on the lid.

“You sure?” I asked Marcellus.

His voice sharpened through the speaker. “Nigga, I made it.”

I shut the box.

We'd gone in with Shawna and come out with a fucking lie in velvet.

“Red know?” Tank asked toward the phone.

Marcellus paused. “He expected you to take it.”

Streetlights moved across the windshield. I counted them until I realized what I was doing. Every time I stopped looking at something in front of me, I saw Red's arm across Shawna's chest.

Rosa's voice came through next.

“We still have the original order,” Rosa said. “And you have his papers.”

I pulled the folder from my waistband and laid it across my lap. Its corners were bent. A page had torn where my fist had held it. I saw Valentino's name again, then a list of payments tied to the jewel transaction, figures arranged so neatly that they almost concealed the bodies underneath.

“I don't want his fucking papers,” I told Rosa.

Her voice softened. “I know.”

I put my hand over the page. “I want her.”

Rosa answered me by name. “I know, Aiden.”

Another call lit the screen. Unknown number.

I picked the phone up, ended the first call and answered before the next ring could finish.

“Shawna?” I said.

Red breathed into my ear.

“Wondered how long you'd take to notice the gift,” Red said.

I pressed the phone so hard against my head that its edge hurt. “Let me hear her.”

Red kept speaking. “Your friend has my three million. You have papers that belong in my desk. Somewhere, somebody has a very old note.”

I held the box against my leg. “Let me hear her.”

Movement came through the line. A door, footsteps, the brush of something against the microphone.

Then Shawna spoke.

“Baby,” she said.

I closed my eyes. “I'm here.”

Her breath caught. “I know.”

I leaned forward against the phone. “I'm coming.”

Shawna answered softly. “I know.”

Red came back on the line before I could say anything else.

“The unfinished casino in the desert,” Red told me. “Midnight. Bring the money, the papers and the original note.”

I looked at the dashboard clock. Less than three hours.

“If you hurt her,” I began.

Red cut across me. “Come before I get bored.”

The call ended.

For a while neither Tank nor I spoke. He drove with both hands on the wheel. I laid the phone on the folder and held the photograph in one hand, the closed blue box in the other. The suit we'd chosen that afternoon had blood on the sleeve and the side. My cheek still held the heat of Shawna's slap.

Every place she'd touched me that night seemed like the only part of my body still alive.

“Take me to the money,” I told Tank.

He nodded at the road. “Yeah.”

I turned toward him. “All of it.”

Tank swallowed. “Yeah.”

I put the photograph inside my jacket, over the cross. I'd gone into the Saint believing I could steal a fortune and a future in one night. Now he had my future locked in a room somewhere, and all the money in Vegas couldn't make the next red light change.

“Tank,” I said.

He glanced at me. “What?”

I kept my hand against the picture. “After we get her.”

Tank faced the road again. “Nigga, we are getting her.”

I looked out at the city.

“After we get her,” I told Tank, “there isn't going to be enough desert left to hide what we do to him.”

## 13. Everybody Bring Your Heart

At nine forty, I put my fist through the front door.

I owned the fucking door. Paid for the glass, the frame, the handsome handle I had apparently decided was beneath me. None of that stopped the glass from cutting my hand. Being rich had failed to make even my own house respect my authority.

Tank caught my wrist before I could hit it again.

“Aiden.”

I swung at him with the other hand. Dante's folder fell from under my arm onto the path. Tank's head went sideways, but his grip stayed closed.

“Let go,” I told him.

“No.”

“Had the money this whole time.”

“Yeah,” Tank said.

I hit him again. Something in my side pulled so hard my knees tried to leave. I wanted to drag him into the broken doorway. He turned and held me against the wall instead, putting all that solid weight between me and the glass.

Tank could've ended an argument by falling asleep on the other person. That night I wasn't even making him work at it.

“You let me walk in there thinking I knew what was mine,” I said. “Let him laugh at me.”

Tank looked past my shoulder into the house.

“Heard him,” he said. “Was standing right there.”

I shoved once more. He let me go when my hands stopped trying to reach anything.

The front room still looked like money had come home drunk. Champagne in silver buckets. A red heel beneath the piano. Four men spread between the couches, suddenly remembering appointments elsewhere.

On a side table, somebody had put cocaine on a photograph of me and Shawna. Her face was beneath it. All the fucking surfaces in that house, and a man had picked the only one I couldn't tolerate.

I swept the table clear.

“Everybody out.”

The four men got up. One had a woman's purse over his shoulder. He looked down, turned toward the hallway to find her, then looked back at my face and decided he could notify the owner from somewhere outdoors.

Tank picked up Dante's folder and brought it inside. He pushed the ruined door as far shut as it would go.

“Want me gone too?” he asked.

His jacket was torn. One cuff link was missing. He had blood on his hands from people I'd watched him carry, as well as the blood I'd put on his mouth. I didn't know how to fit all of him into one answer.

“Get the money,” I said.

He went upstairs.

I sat on the piano bench because it was close and my legs had begun shaking. Shawna had sat there that afternoon, missed a note, and blamed the piano. I'd offered another piano. She'd asked whether I planned to replace the instrument every time she made a mistake.

“Yes,” I'd told her.

Meant it too. We would've had a fucking orchestra by Christmas.

Her perfume was still near the bench. I put my head down and saw her dancing with Red. Her hand against his cheek. We'd agreed she had to make him believe. I'd said I could stand there and take it. Then I'd arrived in my king costume and discovered I couldn't take shit.

A heavy thump came from upstairs.

“Don't drop her money,” I shouted.

“Got it,” Tank called back.

“You keep saying that.”

He came down carrying two bags. No silver case. He'd left the broken case at the motel and brought the cash here, through the same door I'd believed belonged to the three of us.

He opened the bags on the dining table. Three million dollars lay between the candles Shawna had bought because the house smelled, according to her, like men lying to each other.

My girl had selected the correct fragrance.

“All of it,” Tank said.

“Counted it?”

“More than once.”

“Feel good doing that?”

He shook his head.

I took a bundle and threw it at his chest. The band broke. Money slid over his shoes.

“Your fucking tip.”

He watched it settle.

“Look at me,” I said. “What did you think was going to happen?”

Tank looked up. “Thought you were going to die.”

The refrigerator hummed from the kitchen. I remember that noise. Three million dollars open on a table, two men bleeding, and the refrigerator carrying on with its little job.

Tank rubbed his hands over his head.

“That morning,” he said. “Before she drove. You kept saying water, house, room for singing. She kept telling you to look at her. Half the time you weren't answering what she asked.”

I could remember her voice. Couldn't put every word back. I didn't want him doing it for me.

“So you went shopping,” I said.

“Went back,” Tank answered. “T was there. Money was there. Ten years I stood behind him. Every room. Every car. Somebody said his name wrong, I was the man who got up.”

“Loved him.”

“Yeah.”

I stared at him. “Say that again.”

“Loved him,” Tank said. “Want another lie?”

His mouth was bleeding down his chin. He let it.

“You knew him one night. Knew him ten years myself. Know what he was. Give you something with one hand, keep the other round your neck. Know all that. Still got to that pool and wanted him to stand up.”

I looked at the bills on the floor.

“When he didn't?”

Tank swallowed. “Took what was left.”

That didn't make it right. Didn't make the money his. But it was the first answer that let me see him standing there, alone in a place where his whole life had just stopped giving orders.

“Red called after?” I asked.

Tank nodded. “Said he knew. Money bought him whatever he asked.”

“Believed him?”

“For a while.”

“Until when?”

Tank looked at my chest. “Put his hand on her, and you stopped breathing.”

“Don't tell me what I looked like.”

“Like you did in the car.”

I picked up a candle and hurled it into the empty fireplace. Glass broke against brick. The flame went out.

“Not dying in another fucking car,” I told him.

Tank stayed beside the table.

“Then let me drive,” he said. “Let me get one thing right.”

***

Blue lights moved across the windows.

For one stupid second, I thought somebody had brought Shawna home. I was on my feet before the cars stopped.

Tank looked through the front window.

“Not ours,” he said.

Captain Wade Frost came through the broken door like knocking was a tax he had successfully avoided. Silver hair. Pink face. Two younger officers behind him. I recognized the woman, Bell, from the station when I'd brought in the robbers the day before.

They looked at the bags. Frost looked at my badge.

“Take it off,” he said.

“Selling shirts now?” I asked.

“The badge.”

Tank stepped between him and the table.

“Got an appointment?” Tank asked.

“Move.”

“House,” Tank told him, pointing at the floor. “Ask like you walked into one.”

Frost put his hand on his gun. Bell glanced at the other officer. Neither came closer.

I stepped around Tank.

“What you want?”

“Redmond filed a complaint,” Frost said. “Theft. Assault. Armed men at his property. A woman held against her will.”

My face went cold.

“Got that last part right.”

“We're recovering the money. Your authority is suspended pending investigation.”

“Who sent you?” I asked.

“You don't question me.”

“Doing it beautifully. Who?”

Frost held out a folded paper. I let his arm get tired.

“Knox sign it?” I asked.

“Chief has larger concerns.”

“Bought me a Lamborghini two days ago. Thinking about me plenty.”

Bell looked at the paper again. She'd eaten the lunch I'd bought the station. I could see her trying to figure out how that man had become the assignment standing in front of her.

“Bell,” Frost said. “Collect the bags.”

She stayed still.

“Order, Officer.”

I put my phone on the table and called Knox. Frost reached for it. Tank caught his wrist.

“Let the telephone ring,” Tank told him.

I picked it up when Knox answered.

“Tell me you have her,” she said.

Nigga, that nearly broke me. Not the money, not the fire, not a question about the badge she'd pinned on my chest. She wanted to know whether I had my girl.

“Not yet,” I managed. “Send Frost?”

Silence. Then Knox told me to put her on speaker.

I did and set the phone back on the table.

“Wade,” she said.

Frost stopped fighting Tank's grip.

“Chief, we're securing—”

“At his house?” Knox asked.

“Evidence of—”

“Wade. At his house?”

Frost looked down at the phone. “Yes.”

“Captain Aiden retains his command,” Knox said. “You have no order from me. Bring whatever Redmond gave you to my office. Bring your badge with it.”

Bell breathed out. Frost turned a shade redder than his friend had managed at the gala.

“Choosing this thug?” Frost asked.

“Say Captain while you cry,” I told him.

Knox didn't laugh.

“Choosing the officer who carried people out while Redmond locked the doors,” she said. “Get out of his house.”

Frost jerked toward the phone. Tank released his wrist just as I stepped forward. The captain stumbled against the table, knocking a candle over. One bag slid toward the edge.

He reached for the money with one hand and drew his pistol with the other.

I hit him before he brought it level.

He went backward through a chair. Money flew. His pistol struck the floor and skidded beneath the piano, which was an extremely expensive place to learn you weren't the police in charge.

Bell had her gun on him before he started getting up.

“Stay down, sir,” she said.

“His side too?” Frost demanded.

“Following my chief.”

“People keep explaining,” I told him. “Help us a little.”

He tried rising anyway. Tank put one hand on his shoulder and returned him to the carpet like a piece of furniture somebody had set in the wrong place.

I picked up the paper. Red's handwriting sat across the bottom: recover the money, disarm me, use Shawna's name to make me cooperate.

I gave it to Bell.

“Her rescue money,” she said after reading it. “That's what he sent you for.”

Frost didn't answer.

Bell cuffed him. The other officer retrieved the pistol from under the piano, then took the paper as evidence.

“Where do you need us after this, Captain?” he asked me.

Tank had knelt to gather the scattered bills. Shawna's future lay beside a chair leg and a police captain's shoe.

“With Knox,” I told the officer. “Keep anybody else from coming here in a uniform Red borrowed.”

He nodded. “Yes, Captain.”

They took Frost through the doorway he hadn't bothered knocking on. The lights crossed the ceiling and went away.

Knox was still on speaker.

“Aiden.”

“Here.”

“Officers are searching the Saint,” she said. “Haven't found her yet. Your car's safe. Can bring it.”

“Keep roads open. My people are going to the unfinished casino. Desert. Midnight. That's where he's taking her.”

“I'll hold the outside,” Knox said.

I looked at my badge beneath the blood.

“Won't forget this, Chief.”

Her voice softened a little. “Bring your wife home, Captain. Give me something worth remembering.”

She ended the call before I corrected a word I wanted so badly to make true.

Tank put the last bundle in its bag and stood the candle up.

“Really are the police,” he said.

“Discussed it at graduation.”

“Heard the words.”

“Seeing the picture?” I asked.

He nodded. Then my brother smiled at me, just a little, through his swollen mouth. I let him.

***

Rosa came in with Marcellus on her arm. A man's coat covered her gala dress. One earring was gone. She stopped at the blood on the wall, then looked straight at my hand.

Tank went out for the blue box I'd left in the Mercedes and brought it to Marcellus. The old man held it closed as though there were something dead inside.

My phone rang before Rosa reached the table.

Rex Crown.

I nearly ignored him. Then I remembered the man hitting a guard from behind to get us out and answered.

“Captain,” Rex said.

“Busy.”

“Know. Your woman.”

I shut my eyes. “Everybody knows?”

“Still outside saying your name,” he said. “Saw you bring those people out.”

“Need her. Not my name.”

“Give me somewhere to bring myself, then.”

I looked at Tank, at the cash, at my hand dripping onto the cloth Rosa had silently put beneath it.

“How bad you hurt?” I asked Rex.

“Pride's worse than my face.”

“Stay home with it.”

“Didn't call for a bedtime,” Rex said. “Could've let the same people who watched you beat me watch me die. Came back. Give me somewhere to put that.”

I looked toward the dark lake windows.

“Unfinished casino. Desert. Midnight.”

He repeated it, then said he would be there.

“Your friend with the gun—” I began.

“Not coming. Got better friends.”

I laughed once. It hurt enough to make Rosa look up.

“What?” she asked.

“Nigga making sense.”

Rex heard her. “Tell those women the check's good. Don't stop being a gentleman because somebody rearranged my evening.”

Rosa took the phone.

“Bring your gentleman ass ready to help,” she told him.

“Yes, ma'am.”

She ended the call and gave the phone back.

“Let them,” she said to me.

“Let who?”

“People who love you. Let them do something.”

She touched my good shoulder, then pointed toward the entrance.

“Which stupid motherfucker broke the door?”

Nobody answered. She looked at my hand.

“Of course.”

She took my wrist. Her fingers were shaking. Hadn't noticed that while she was walking around ordering us back into being people.

“Where is she?” Rosa asked.

“Red—”

“Know fucking Red. Where?”

“Bringing her to the casino. Midnight.”

“Then why you bleeding on furniture?”

I started answering. She tightened her grip.

“Get angry after she's here,” Rosa said. “Buy a mountain. Fight the whole mountain. Right now, stand up without falling down.”

“Don't talk to me like a kid.”

“Stop making it necessary.”

Tank turned his face away.

“Funny?” I asked him.

“No.”

“Good.”

Rosa sat me on the bench and went for a thicker towel. Marcellus stayed beside the money.

“Yours?” he asked.

“Hers.”

He nodded. Apparently that was all the accounting he needed.

***

My phone vibrated on the table as Rosa came back. She picked it up, saw the screen, and stopped.

Red had sent a photograph.

White chair. Shawna sitting in it. Blue dress torn at her shoulder. Hair over one side of her face. Red behind her with his hand beneath her chin and his ring turned toward the camera.

I stood so fast the bench fell over.

Rosa caught my shirt.

“Aiden.”

“Going to kill him.”

“Look at her,” Rosa said.

“Am looking.”

“Her.”

I forced my eyes off his hand. Shawna faced the lens. Her mouth was swollen on one side. Her eyes were open and furious. He'd gotten her into the chair. Hadn't gotten her to look away.

Below it, he'd written: *She remembers this room.*

I called him and turned on speaker so Rosa could hear.

“Touch her again—” I began.

“Sitting right here,” Red said.

“Put her on.”

“Ask nice.”

I couldn't get enough air. Rosa squeezed the back of my neck. I felt every finger.

“Please.”

Red laughed softly. I held the phone and listened to him enjoy the word.

Then Shawna was there.

“Aiden?”

“Baby.”

“Are you hurt?” she asked.

“No.”

“Don't lie to me.”

“Here,” I told her. “I'm here.”

I heard fabric move and a breath stop too early. Couldn't see why. Almost worse than the picture, having to invent what every sound meant.

“Tell Rosa I ruined her dress,” Shawna said.

Rosa pressed a fist to her mouth.

“Doesn't give a fuck about the dress, baby.”

“Does,” Shawna said. “Tell her I'll pay.”

Rosa took the phone.

“Owe me an earring too, bitch.”

Shawna laughed once.

That little laugh held the whole room still. For a second she was with us in a way Red's picture couldn't stop.

Then he took the line back.

“Midnight,” Red said. “Money, what you stole, and an apology.”

I took the phone from Rosa.

“Want me on my knees?” I asked.

“Now you're listening.”

“Keep her alive. See me any way you want.”

He hung up.

Tank righted the bench behind me. I stayed standing, holding a call that had ended.

Marcellus put out his hand.

“Photograph,” he said. “Let me see.”

I gave him the phone. I'd run out of things I could break that Red would feel.

The old man enlarged the picture. His thumb stopped over the ring.

“There you are,” he whispered.

I looked up. Marcellus had begun smiling, and there wasn't a happy thing in it.

## 14. What She Told Me After

Shawna told me this part later, sitting beside me with her bare feet tucked under my leg. She stopped when she wanted water. Started again wherever she could. I kept asking the same questions until she said the next one would cost me a glass against the forehead.

Some words she remembered exactly. Some things she wasn't giving anybody, and I had to learn that loving her didn't buy me admission to every bad minute she'd survived.

What follows is what she told me. That night, I was at home looking at a photograph. She was still inside the Saint.

***

Red took her through the back corridors with two men following and his hand locked around her arm. The party was coming apart around them. Alarms sounded. A few guests still held drinks, reluctant to abandon anything they'd paid for without a personal announcement.

A woman beside the elevators lifted a phone.

“Get my good side,” Shawna told her.

Red pulled harder. The woman lowered the phone.

“Always have to do that?” Red asked.

“Look good?”

“Make a fucking scene.”

“Dragged me out of one,” Shawna said. “Thought you liked them.”

He took her into a private room she remembered. White chairs, white carpet, a painting of a horse coming out of the ocean. Red had once spent twenty minutes explaining that horse while she tried to calculate whether she could leave without him noticing.

She said the horse looked surprised to be swimming. The artist had made a very expensive animal wonder how its night had gone wrong.

This time there was somebody's blood on the carpet.

One guard brought Shawna's purse from the office. Red took it and put it on the table. Then he sent the men outside and closed the door.

That click was when she got cold.

She told me it hadn't happened at the gunfire or while he dragged her through the corridor. It happened when she understood he wanted a room where nobody else could hear him talk.

“Sit,” Red said.

She remained standing.

He said her name, slower, as if that would make a chair appear beneath her.

“Give me a cigarette,” Shawna told him.

He took one from a heavy silver box, lit it, and offered it like a man bringing flowers. She took it. Her hand was steady enough then.

“Could've killed him,” Red said.

“Tried,” she answered.

“Ran.”

“Coming back,” Shawna said. “My nigga's coming back.”

Red laughed and reached for a bottle.

“Always come back for money.”

“Put me outside,” she told him. “Find out.”

He poured too fast and spilled beside the glass. Then he wiped it with his own sleeve.

Shawna noticed. She said Red usually waited for somebody to cross a whole room and pick up something beside his shoe. Angry enough, he'd forgotten the job description he'd assigned himself.

“Think he loves you?” Red asked.

She smoked, looking at the horse.

“Asked you something.”

“Heard it,” she said.

“Loves winning,” Red told her. “You're what he stole. Wait until you're just a woman in his house.”

She kept her eyes on the painting. He came closer.

“Dante begged, you know,” Red said. “Begged us to look after you.”

Shawna turned.

“Us?” she asked.

The bottle stopped halfway back to the table.

“You were there?” she asked him.

“Didn't say that.”

“Who told you?”

Red put the bottle down carefully.

“Everybody knew.”

“I didn't,” Shawna said.

He looked toward the door. She watched him do it. When she told me, I asked if that meant he'd been there with T and Valentino. She said she still couldn't prove that part. He had claimed too much, then hidden inside what other people supposedly knew.

She wanted the old order in her hands. Wanted to lay it in his lap and make him touch the words. All she had was Dante's watch. She turned it around her wrist.

“What did he call me?” Shawna asked Red.

“What?”

“When he begged. Which name?”

“Your fucking name.”

“Which one?”

“Don't remember every—”

“Trouble,” Shawna interrupted. “Called me Trouble.”

Red stared at her.

“Miss Trouble when he was happy,” she said. “Mrs. Trouble when I was mad. Come home saying, ‘Mrs. Trouble, I fucked up,’ and I'd know to sit down before the rest.”

She laughed.

When she told me, she laughed again. Dante had borrowed a car for their honeymoon and brought its bumper upstairs after knocking it off. The whole bumper. Carried it in both hands because, apparently, leaving any of the damage behind would have been disrespectful.

“Like flowers,” Shawna told me from the couch. “Nigga carried it like flowers.”

I hated him a little for making her laugh from three years away. Wanted her to tell it again anyway. That's where I was, baby. Improving without becoming convenient.

Back in Red's white room, she'd wiped her eyes with the back of her wrist.

“Don't know how he talked to me,” Shawna told Red.

“Know what he was.”

“Talk about that, then. Your dead man. Don't get to talk about mine.”

He came close enough that she could smell his drink.

“Keep making him a saint,” Red said.

“Remembering my husband,” she answered. “Stole money. Lied badly. Left socks in the bed. Loved him. Think telling me he was bad changes something?”

She touched the watch again.

“Tell me something I don't know.”

Red pointed at her with his glass.

“Dante said the same things. House, different life, music. Buy you a microphone and call himself a husband.”

“Was my husband,” Shawna said.

“Paid for the wedding.”

“Paid for a room.”

“Paid for everything.”

“Should've kept the receipts,” she told him. “Maybe the preacher would've married you.”

Red threw the glass.

It broke against the wall beside the horse. Shawna didn't move. She'd been proud of that until she explained it to me: her legs had gone stiff. Courage wasn't the only reason somebody could stand still.

He came toward her.

“Used to know how to talk to me,” he said.

“Used to need you to stop.”

Red pushed her into the white chair, caught her face in one hand, and called a guard inside. Told him to take a picture.

That's the photograph they sent me. Shawna wasn't posing in a chair because she had run out of fight. He was holding her there while someone found the camera button.

She stared into the phone.

“He'll see you touching me,” she told Red.

“Point of it,” Red said.

“Know.”

The man took the picture and stepped away. Red's ring scraped her jaw as he released her.

She looked at his hand.

“Wearing it now?” Shawna asked.

His fist closed. “Wearing what?”

“All that money. Still need somebody to notice.”

He put his hand in his pocket.

***

A telephone rang outside the room. The guard at the door said a name, and Shawna saw Red's expression change.

“Frost?” she asked.

She knew the name from old penthouse conversations. Red used it whenever he wanted her to believe the city would become reasonable on request.

The guard whispered more. Red took the telephone so hard the man stepped backward.

“What do you mean arrested?” Red demanded.

When Shawna told me this part later, she made me listen twice. Said the first time my smile had interrupted my ears.

Red stood over the chair, listening.

“Knox said what?” he asked into the phone.

Shawna lifted her cigarette.

“My captain?” she asked.

Red covered the mouthpiece. “Shut up.”

“Wondering who finally made your policeman nervous,” she said.

He went into the hallway to finish the call. The door stayed open a few inches. The guard remained inside.

His name was Arlo. Thirty-eight, she told me, with gray beginning in his beard. She knew him too. Once he'd carried groceries upstairs when the elevator broke, then apologized for asking if she was all right because Red had heard him ask.

Arlo stood between her and the door.

“Don't have to stay in here,” Shawna told him.

He glanced toward the hallway. “Job.”

“Turning into a funeral,” she said.

He didn't answer.

She laid her cigarette across the lip of the ashtray and stood. He stepped toward her. She lifted one hand as though asking him to give her room, then reached past him with the other for the heavy silver cigarette box.

She threw it into his face.

Arlo hit the wall. Shawna ran through the door, past Red with the phone at his ear, and into the corridor before either man had caught up with the change in her attitude.

Red shouted her name.

She kept running.

The borrowed gown caught the corner of a table. She pulled until a strip of blue silk tore away. At the end of the corridor stood open service doors. Light shone from the stairwell. She could hear radios below and somebody crying.

She hit the door with her shoulder and nearly fell over a waiter sitting on the landing. He held his wrist. Blood had reached his white cuff. Beside him stood a young-looking woman who told Shawna she was twenty-four when Shawna asked if she could help lift him.

“Outside door?” Shawna asked her.

“Two floors. Won't let us past—”

“Get him up,” Shawna said.

They lifted him together. Shawna took his good side; the other woman supported the injured one without pulling his wrist. They started down, three people crowded into a staircase that had probably been designed for workers to move discreetly and never bleed on the paying guests.

Her dress dragged through dust and dropped flowers. The waiter tried to apologize for slowing them. She told him to save his breath for getting outside.

Red came through the service door above.

“Shawna!” he shouted.

She didn't look back.

“Want to help, get his fucking feet,” she called.

He caught them on the next landing. The woman supporting the waiter stopped, staring at him. Red pointed down the stairs.

“You two. Go,” he said.

Neither moved.

“Go!” Red shouted.

Shawna held the waiter until the woman had him steady. Then she let them pass. She watched them reach the lower door, watched the woman look back once, and waited until both disappeared through it.

Red grabbed her arm.

“Made me run,” he said.

“Try it more,” Shawna told him.

He pulled her toward him.

“Think you're Aiden now?”

She looked down at his hand, then into his face.

“Know exactly who I am. That's what's bothering you.”

He tried to haul her up the stairs. She planted her feet and struck the wrist holding her with the hard case of Dante's watch. Red swore and loosened his grip. She drove an elbow into his chest, got free, and ran toward the lower door.

Arlo stood in it.

One eye was swelling. Blood ran from his nose. He looked at her, then at Red behind her. She told me that for one second she thought he might step aside.

He raised his pistol instead.

She stopped.

Arlo couldn't look her in the face while he aimed it.

“Sorry,” he said.

“Get better at it,” Shawna told him.

Red pulled her back up the stairs. Her shoulder struck the rail. She bit the inside of her cheek because she didn't want him enjoying the noise she'd nearly made.

He put her into the white chair again.

The cigarette box lay on the floor where she'd thrown it. The patch of table it had occupied looked absurdly clean among the glass, blood, and spilled drink. She remembered that clean little rectangle clearly enough to describe it to me twice.

Red bent over her.

“One more fucking idea—” he began.

“Several,” Shawna interrupted. “Going to need a longer night.”

He stepped back.

Her hands were shaking now. She tucked them beneath her thighs so he wouldn't see. She had failed to get out. Two other people had succeeded. She held on to the difference while he paced.

When she told me, I asked what she'd been thinking on the stairs. She pushed her feet farther beneath my leg and gave me a look like the answer was written across my forehead.

“What my man would've done,” she said. “Better dress.”

I kissed her before I asked another question. Wasn't improving that answer.

***

At the lake house that night, I hadn't heard any of this yet. Marcellus was still holding my phone under the dining-room lamp, enlarging Red's ring until Shawna's face moved out of the picture.

I wanted him to put her back.

“The mounting,” he said. “There.”

“Tell me you're sure.”

“About my work, yes.”

“Were sure at the party,” I told him.

“You were,” Marcellus said.

I wanted to shake him. Wanted him right almost as badly as I wanted to hear her voice.

“Show me.”

He opened the decoy's box on the table. It still looked expensive enough to ruin several sensible lives. Its best contribution so far had been hitting a gunman in the head.

Marcellus laid the phone beside it.

“Changed what surrounds the other stone,” he explained. “Dark stones at the edges. Makes the blue look smaller. Kept this piece underneath.”

He pointed to a curved strip of metal in the photograph.

“Saw that shoulder at the gala. Only from the side. This gives me the underside.”

Tank leaned over him.

“Kept it because it's gold?” Tank asked.

“Kept it because he's an idiot,” Marcellus said.

Rosa exhaled. “Finally. Good fucking news.”

Marcellus showed us the curve beneath the stone. No map, no secret number. A shape he'd made with his hands. He recognized it the way I recognized Shawna's steps coming down our stairs.

“Both stones fit the mounting,” he reminded us. “This one here is the copy. Ring's where I'd look for the original.”

“Can read forty million through my phone?” I asked.

“Can recognize work he hid badly. Need the diamond in my hand to swear to it. Need its flaw. Picture won't give me that.”

I looked at Red's fingers under Shawna's chin. He might have been touching her with the thing Dante died trying to find.

“We'll get it,” I said.

Marcellus looked up.

“Say the whole promise.”

“What?”

“Says her or the diamond?”

“Her.”

Didn't wait to find out how much that hurt him.

“Wants the letter burned?” he asked.

“Burn it.”

“Proof of what he did?”

“Gets it. Want my promise? Bringing her home. Everything else finds a way to come with us.”

Marcellus nodded. His mouth tightened once.

“Dante said something very much like that.”

I caught the table's edge.

“Not Dante.”

“Know,” he said.

“Stop putting him between me and every fucking thing I do.”

Rosa said my name. I shook my head.

“Love her now. Me. Standing here bleeding. Going out there. Remember him all you want. When her phone rings, want her to know who's calling.”

My voice shook. I hated letting the three of them hear how much of me was still fighting a man who couldn't answer.

Marcellus took the gala folder from the end of the table. He set the records beside Celeste's letter. Rosa handed him the envelope containing the original order from Dante's watch. He kept them separate: a dead woman's words, a dead man's warning, the books Red had kept on people he could dispose of.

I'd have burned every page to get her through the door. I meant that. Being ashamed of a thing didn't always make me stop meaning it.

Marcellus slid my phone back.

“Give her something to answer,” he said.

I opened Shawna's messages.

My last one before the gala read: *You look good as fuck.*

She had replied with one eye, half a forehead, and a middle finger. I had expected a photograph of the dress. Got a report on the phone's performance instead.

I stared until it blurred.

Rosa stood beside me.

“Sent me six,” she said quietly. “Bathroom ceiling in one. Whole gorgeous ceiling, bitch nowhere in sight.”

I wiped my face with my good hand. Started typing.

*I'm coming. Wear whatever you want.*

Then I deleted the second sentence. She couldn't pick the dress or the chair or the room. Red might read it first. I wanted his head for standing between us even inside a fucking telephone.

I sent the two words that remained.

*I'm coming.*

***

Shawna told me later how he showed her that message. This is the last part of her account before he brought her out to the desert.

Red held the screen where she could see it.

“Thinks he's in a love song,” he said.

“Don't stand in the chorus, then,” Shawna told him.

He took the phone away.

She asked for her purse. He laughed. She asked for water and another cigarette instead. Those he brought, apparently still willing to audition for the part of a man who brought women things. He set the silver box back on the table, lit the cigarette, and gave it to her. She rested it across the box's open lid while she drank.

“Tell me you were lying to him,” Red said.

Shawna drank.

“About what?”

“All of it.”

“Lot of lying,” she said.

“Lied to Valentino.”

She put the glass down. “Yeah.”

“Lied to me.”

“Yeah.”

“Tell me you lied to Aiden.”

He waited. She told me that was the ugliest part: after hurting and trapping her, he still wanted her to make his heart feel better. She knew how. Knew the softness to put into her voice. Could have called him baby and watched his shoulders loosen.

For a moment, she nearly did it to buy herself room.

Then she looked at him.

“Told Aiden I wasn't scared,” Shawna said.

Red leaned nearer. “There.”

“Told him I didn't think about Dante when we were together.”

Red smiled. “And?”

“Told him I didn't like that stupid fucking cross.”

“See?” Red said. “See what I'm saying?”

She picked up the water again. Looked straight at him over it.

“Told him I could live without him.”

Red's smile went away.

She used his name next. Wanted him certain he was the one hearing her.

“Red. That was a lie.”

He knocked the glass out of her hand. Water struck the front of her dress. For a second she thought he would hit her again. Instead he stood there breathing, staring at the wet silk, then picked up his jacket.

“Get up,” Red told her.

“Where?”

“Want to see him so badly, let's go.”

He opened her purse and took out the little silver pistol. Turned it once in his hand, looked at her, and put it inside his jacket.

“This comes too,” he said.

Before he pulled her into the hallway, she left the cigarette burning in the silver box he'd put back on the table. She insisted I remember that. Everything he'd taken from her, and he had forgotten to make her put the cigarette out.

I remember, baby.

***

Back at our house, Rosa was finishing the wrap around my cut hand when Red's next message arrived.

Ten thirty-seven.

*On our way. Don't disappoint her.*

I looked around the dining room. Tank. Rosa. Marcellus. Three million dollars that had already cost too much.

“Call everybody,” I told Tank.

He picked up his phone.

“Everybody?”

“Everybody who ever called me brother.”

## 15. Ten Toes, Thirty Engines

By eleven, the street looked like every bad idea in Vegas had found my address.

Headlights up the drive. Doors slamming. Men calling to each other through the dark. Some wore leather, some silk, and one had arrived in pajamas covered with little white moons. His gold chain was caught between the buttons. Another man still had shaving foam behind his ear.

Tank looked at the pajamas.

“You were asleep?” he asked.

“Participating in my relationship,” the man said.

“Relationship's open at the bottom.”

The man buttoned himself without slowing down.

“Where she at?” he asked me.

“Red has her.”

His hands stopped on the shirt.

“Aight,” he said. “What we need?”

That was why I'd called. I wasn't under the impression every man on my porch was good. Some would've asked Red the same question if his invitation had arrived first. A few had carried T's coffin. Some owed the women at home answers to messages they'd had time to read six fucking times.

But they came. I could work with came.

The man with shaving foam saw the door.

“Red did that?” he asked.

“I did.”

“Okay. Was about to get separately mad.”

“Stay with the original mad,” I told him. “Deadline.”

He wiped his ear on his sleeve and went in. I watched a man I'd once fought move a chair so Marcellus could sit more comfortably. Somebody else picked glass out of Rosa's path before her shoe found it.

My girl was missing. These were the people who answered.

The world could interview better men in the morning.

***

Tank opened both money bags on the dining table. He waited for the room to fill, then spoke without looking at me first.

“This is from the motel,” he said. “I took it.”

The room changed its sound. Men stopped talking and started listening to each other stop.

A man beside the piano leaned forward.

“You took what?” he asked.

“All of it,” Tank said. “Let Aiden think it was gone. Red knew. Tried using it against him tonight.”

“Tried?” a man near the windows asked.

“Tried.”

The man by the piano pointed at Tank and looked at me.

“Still breathing?” he asked.

I stepped between them.

“Look at me when you say that.”

He did. Lowered his hand too.

“Tank stands with me,” I told him.

“Just saying—”

“Say he stole it. He did. Say he's a lying motherfucker. He was. Say you're touching him tonight, find out how fast I forget your name.”

Tank stood behind me without saying a word. I could feel him there. Had spent half the night wishing that were still a thing I could count on. Was choosing to count on it now.

The man by the piano lifted his hands.

“Your house,” he said.

“Yeah. My house. Money on my table. My girl with Red. Any of that bother you, door's already broke. Won't hurt its feelings leaving.”

Nobody moved toward it.

Rosa watched from the staircase in the borrowed coat. Marcellus sat behind her with the blue box on his knees and the papers inside his jacket. The old man looked exhausted. When I glanced at the stairs as if to offer him a bed, he shook his head. Apparently I was the third person that evening who needed to stop deciding where other people belonged.

I put my hand on the money.

“Some came because you owe me,” I told the room. “Some because Red owes you. Some heard there was money.”

“All three,” said a man at the back.

I looked at him. He stood by it.

“Good,” I said. “Knows why he came.”

Then I started paying them.

I didn't throw it in the air. Every man came to the table. Every man looked at me while I put the weight into his hands. I wanted them to remember whose money it had been before they arrived to talk about brotherhood. Shawna had carried it out of a room full of killers. Now she needed something more useful than a room full of men saying they were dangerous.

Bundles disappeared into coats. Into pockets. Into the pajama shirt, where the little moons began straining to escape.

“Get a bag,” Tank told that man.

“I'm good.”

“Look pregnant.”

“I'm good,” the man repeated, holding himself closed.

Some counted immediately. Some laughed. One just held the cash in both hands. I remembered seeing him in a hospital corridor with his head bowed and nothing to say. Tonight he kissed the stack before putting it in his coat.

I didn't ask which part of his life I'd helped. Had one woman to bring home. The rest of the city could bring its problems along if it brought its heart too.

Tank and I had separated the bundles by their bands. When two million had gone into hands or sat aside for the people still waiting, I stopped. The final million remained together in one bag.

I pulled the zipper closed.

“Hers,” I said.

Nobody argued.

Tank hadn't taken his share from the money we'd already set aside. I picked it up and held it toward him.

He shook his head. “No.”

“Take it before I love you louder.”

“Aiden.”

“Poor and guilty? I'm allowing one bad feeling. Pick.”

He looked at the men around us.

“Don't want it.”

“Spend it on somebody who does,” I told him.

I pressed it against his chest until he accepted it. He looked down, then set the whole share beside the closed bag.

“For her,” Tank said.

I left it there. Wasn't going to turn the thing he'd finally given willingly into another argument.

Rosa stepped away from the stairs.

“Before you start singing about brotherhood,” she told the room, “she's my friend.”

Men straightened. The one holding his pajamas stopped smiling.

“See her, bring her,” Rosa said. “Don't stop to tell Red how hard you are. Don't explain a grudge from 2018. You bring her.”

The man who'd arrived with shaving foam nodded.

“We got you, Rosa.”

“Better have her, baby.”

I pushed Rosa's share across the table. It came from the two million we'd separated. The million in the closed bag stayed untouched.

She looked down at it.

“For what?” she asked.

“Whatever you want.”

“Paying me to stay home?”

“Because she loves you,” I said.

Rosa looked up sharply.

“Don't do that right now.”

“When she gets out of the car, I want somebody here she knew before every room had me in it.”

Her fingers tightened around the borrowed coat. I could finally see how tired she was. One earring missing, dress dirty, holding herself straight because we'd all fallen apart whenever she stopped supervising.

“Bring her,” Rosa told me.

I nodded.

She slapped my cheek.

“With your mouth, Aiden.”

Nobody laughed. I swallowed.

“I'm bringing her.”

Rosa kissed the same cheek.

“Go, baby.”

I turned to the room.

“Anybody think Red owns Vegas?”

“Fuck Red,” somebody by the door said.

“Anybody think he owns my wife?”

Rosa made a little noise behind me.

I turned halfway back. “What?”

“Asked her yet?”

“Going to.”

“Quit inviting these men before me.”

The room broke open. Men laughed, slapped shoulders, discussed suits and weddings and who had to be kept away from an open bar. For a few seconds, we had a future large enough to crowd the furniture.

I let them have it. Needed some myself.

Then I raised my hand.

“Listen. I'm not asking you to die because I fell in love. Asking you to stand with me because Red thinks seeing something makes it his. Took a man's wife. Took another man's stone. Had Dante left in the desert. Took Shawna tonight.”

I closed my hand over the cross beneath my shirt.

“Keeps calling it a dead man's jewelry. Fine. Let him look while I come get my girl.”

Tank stepped beside me.

“Ten toes,” he called.

“Down,” the room answered.

“Ten toes.”

“Down.”

The broken door rattled in its frame. I felt their voices against my chest and wanted every penthouse in Vegas to hear them. Every little room where somebody was waiting for a man to come home different.

I kissed the cross and went outside.

***

Tank drove the Mercedes. I took the front passenger seat because I couldn't lean back comfortably and because I wanted to be the first person Red saw.

Marcellus opened the rear door.

“Sure about this?” I asked him.

“No.”

“Can stay with Rosa.”

“Waited enough,” he said, and shut himself in before I could start managing his life again.

The blue box rested beside him. The letters and records stayed inside his coat. Tank put the closed money bag in the back, adding his returned share first. One million plus Tank's part. Red had demanded three million. He was getting what remained after I paid the men coming to take his fucking kingdom.

Rosa came to my open window. Her share was still on the dining table.

“Gardenias,” she said.

“What?”

“Wedding. She likes gardenias. Will lie and tell you roses because men always buy roses.”

I gripped the door. “Gardenias.”

“And don't have that old man give her away. I'll do it.”

Marcellus leaned forward behind me.

“Hadn't volunteered,” he said.

Rosa pointed at him. “Keep it that way.”

She stepped back. Tank started down the drive.

The cars stretched along the street until it looked like somebody had called a funeral and forgotten to agree who was dying. Thirty engines, maybe more. Rex Crown sat in a black coupe. Little Saint rode beside him on a motorcycle with an unpainted tank.

“Borrowed it,” Saint called through my window.

I nodded. The owner had either been very generous or badly informed about our return policy.

Mercedes waved from the green Bentley beside Porsche. Bell was driving my returned police Lamborghini at the front, with Knox beside her. The chief lifted two fingers toward me before they pulled away.

Headlights filled both our mirrors.

For several blocks, Tank said nothing. The towers became signs, the signs became gas stations, and beyond those the road went black.

“Doctor after,” Tank said eventually.

“After.”

“Saying it now.”

“I heard you.”

He nodded and kept both hands on the wheel.

I looked down at the cross.

“When you went back to the pool,” I asked, “T look like himself?”

Tank drove a few seconds before answering.

“No.”

“Good.”

His fingers tightened.

“Don't have to say that,” he told me.

“Know.”

The dashboard lit his face. I remembered him behind T that first night, big as a wall, waiting for another man's decision. Now he had made his own and was driving me toward it.

“Glad you're here,” I said.

His jaw moved.

“Yeah.”

“Don't make me say it twice.”

“Heard you,” Tank said.

Marcellus turned the box between his hands in the back.

“Celeste wore blue the last time I took her dancing,” he said. “I was late. She'd danced with three other men.”

I turned halfway around. “Three?”

“Counted them for me.”

Tank glanced in the mirror.

“Did something to those men?” he asked.

“Asked for the fourth dance,” Marcellus said.

I looked back at the road.

“Could've done that myself.”

“Didn't,” the old man said gently.

We drove the next stretch without speaking. I had a whole convoy behind me, but that answer fit in the car beside me better than all their guns.

***

At eleven fifty-four, Red called.

I put him on speaker.

“Coming?” he asked.

“Hear the cars?”

“Told you to come ready to apologize.”

“I am.”

Tank glanced over.

“Told her I'd never let you touch her again,” I said. “Got plenty to apologize for.”

Red breathed into the phone.

“Think all those men love you?”

“Enough to leave the house.”

“That's money.”

“Should've paid better.”

He went quiet. I heard Shawna behind him. Not the words, just her voice before he moved the phone away.

“Let me talk to her,” I said.

“See her soon.”

“Let me—”

The call ended. I gripped the phone so hard Tank took it before I could break that too. He set it in the console.

Past the last gas station, headlights shone across the highway instead of down it.

Two trucks. Men beside them, no uniforms. Burning barrels between the trucks. Red had arranged the road the way he arranged a dining room: everybody would stop where he told them and appreciate the view.

Tank slowed. Thirty engines behind us changed their voices.

Knox called. I answered on speaker.

“See them,” she said. “Stay in the convoy.”

“Convoy is me.”

“Then don't make me explain a road accident to your wife before you get there.”

A shot hit our hood. Tank pulled left. Another made a white star in the windshield directly in front of him.

Marcellus dropped below the rear windows, pulling his coat over the papers and taking the box down with him. I ducked as Bell brought the Lamborghini alongside us. Knox leaned forward in the passenger seat, staring at the trucks.

The siren rose.

One man stepped backward. Another raised his gun anyway.

“Hardheaded motherfucker,” I said.

Tank put his foot down.

The Mercedes left the road in a sheet of dust. We dropped into a shallow ditch and came out hard enough to throw my head against the roof. My seat belt caught the rest of me. I tasted blood.

Behind us, the convoy spread to both sides of the trucks. Headlights bounced over scrub and stone. Red's men had expected a line.

Got a fucking horizon.

Rex's coupe came through on our right. Little Saint rode beside it, up off his seat over the rough ground. The green Bentley emerged from the dust with its roof down. Mercedes held her hair in one hand and a pistol in the other.

“Keep the old man down!” she shouted as they passed.

“Old man is down,” Marcellus called from the back. “Thoroughly fucking down.”

I laughed. Bullet hole in the glass, girl waiting inside a nightmare, and that old man was still correcting the way my people talked to him. I loved him for giving me a sound Red hadn't put in my mouth.

Then the nearer truck moved.

Its driver turned toward the cars passing through the desert. Rex braked. Saint swerved. One of the truck's front wheels climbed a ditch, bringing its nose across the coupe's escape route.

I saw Rex look up through the windshield. Saw Little Saint turn his bike back.

“Tank.”

“See it.”

“Believe you this time.”

Tank drove straight into the truck's rear corner.

Metal struck metal. Our car jumped sideways. My shoulder hit the door, and every stitch in my side tried leaving without me. The truck's rear swung through the dirt, pulling its nose away long enough for Rex to get clear.

Little Saint shot through the gap and raised a fist.

“Aiden!” he shouted.

“Tank!” I shouted back.

Tank looked at me. “What?”

“Giving you your fucking credit. Enjoy it.”

He grinned through the blood on his lip.

The truck stopped against a low rise. Its driver jumped out with a gun, took one look at the cars surrounding him, and dropped it.

Bell braked beside him. Knox got out with her weapon raised. Tank had slowed close enough that I heard her through my open window.

“Blocked my captain's road,” Knox told him.

The man stared at her uniform.

“Your captain?”

She pointed at me. “That one.”

He looked over. His face fell so completely I almost respected the honesty.

“Didn't say it was him,” he told Knox.

“What part of thirty cars looked like a small mistake?” I shouted.

Rex pulled up beside us with his window down. The cut on his eyebrow had opened again.

“Three times,” he told me.

“That was Tank.”

Rex looked at my driver. “Owe the family, then.”

Tank raised two fingers from the wheel. Didn't bother trying to look modest. Would've annoyed me if he had.

Knox's officers moved around the trucks. The second crew had run before the first finished moving. I could see four flashlights bouncing through the desert, trying to become somebody else's problem.

Bell helped secure the driver while Knox directed the others. Then they returned to the Lamborghini as our cars found the highway. Tank counted headlights coming through the dust: coupe, Bentley, borrowed bike, everybody else, scraped and louder but still moving.

“All back,” he said.

The police car took the lead. Cruisers stayed to hold the roadblock. Bell gave the siren one short burst, and horns answered along the line.

Marcellus sat up carefully.

“Finished doing that?” he asked.

“For now.”

He looked down at his collar.

“Tie gave up.”

At the next slow bend, I turned and straightened it with my good hand.

“Meeting forty million,” I told him. “Look ready.”

He looked at the cross on my chest.

“Damn, boy,” Marcellus said.

I smiled. “Magnificent.”

He nodded like the last repair of the evening had finally been completed.

***

At eleven fifty-nine, the unfinished casino rose out of the dark.

White columns beneath floodlights. Empty windows. Plastic moving behind them. A dry fountain beyond the cars parked across the entrance. Men standing between those cars.

Above it all, a balcony ran beneath an unfinished crown of stone.

I saw blue at the railing.

Shawna.

My whole body knew before my eyes finished crossing the distance. Her hair blew across her face. Red stood beside her and lifted the hand with the ring, waving like he'd invited us to dinner.

Tank stopped. Behind us, one engine after another went quiet.

I opened the door. My side hurt. My hand hurt. Everything in me that knew how to be scared was awake and screaming.

I put both feet on the ground anyway.

Shawna stepped toward the railing. Red caught her arm.

“Aiden!” she called.

I looked straight up at her.

“Here, baby,” I said. “I'm here.”

## 16. The Last King of Vegas

Red stood with my girl beneath a chandelier that had never been turned on.

Motherfucker hadn't finished building the place where he wanted to die.

Tank came around the Mercedes and stood beside me. Behind us, our headlights shone through the dust. Men got out wearing blue at their wrists, blue at their throats, guns where nobody had to guess at them. Some I'd known for years. Some had been calling me boss for eleven days. None had come to make peace.

The building was marble on one wall, naked concrete on the next. Empty windows. Plastic moving in the night wind. A staircase wide enough for a king led down into construction dust, and Red had put Shawna above it like she was the last expensive thing the place needed.

I raised my good hand. She found me between the headlights and held my eyes.

That was the world. Everything else was stuff I had paid for or planned to. Could've burned all of it while I watched if they let her come down.

Red wore white from his shoes to his collar. The ring on his right hand caught the floodlights when he put that hand on her shoulder.

“Brought everybody,” he called.

“Told them there was a dead man here,” I said.

Tank looked at me. I knew the look. Don't let this man move you before you decide to move. Don't give him anything else just because you're angry.

I was already leaning toward the stairs.

“Inside,” Red said. “You and Tank. Nobody else.”

I started walking. Tank caught my sleeve.

“We get her and leave,” he said.

“We get her.”

He looked at me another second, then let go.

***

Thirty men waited among roulette tables covered in plastic. I counted because a man should know how many people he's being stupid in front of. Some hid their guns under jackets. Others had decided there was no point preserving the surprise.

My badge showed inside the ruined white coat. A few men studied it longer than they studied my pistol. Outside, a siren sounded once. Through the entrance I could see Knox and Bell putting officers around the parked cars.

No uniform was coming to make Red feel better this time.

He brought Shawna down by the elbow. Every time his fingers tightened, I wanted to tear his arm out of its sleeve.

“Let her walk,” I told him.

“Walking.”

“To me. Let my woman walk to me.”

Red stopped halfway down.

“Haven't paid for anything yet.”

Tank stepped beside me.

“Brought your money,” Tank said.

Red laughed. “Still think this is three million?”

“Kept asking for it.”

“Asked for respect,” Red said. “Money was the easy part.”

He turned back to me.

“How much did she tell you about that first night?”

Shawna closed her eyes.

“Enough,” I said.

“Was supposed to get into a car with whoever T sent. You. Tank. Anybody with a pulse and somewhere to hide the money.”

“Don't,” Shawna told him.

Red leaned near her.

“Being honest,” he said. “What lovers want, right?”

I could hear Tank breathing beside me.

Red lifted her wrist. Dante's watch slid down against her hand.

“Got the house, cars, army,” Red told me. “Whole parking lot thinks you're the next big thing. Still wearing his watch.”

I had looked at that watch every morning. At breakfast. In bed. When she touched my cheek. I'd acted as if it didn't bother me because apparently a man who could afford a lake wasn't allowed to admit a wristwatch hurt his feelings.

It did. I was jealous of somebody in the ground, and ashamed enough to hide it badly.

Red saw my face. He held her wrist up a little higher.

“Tell him whose chain brought you over.”

Shawna looked at me.

“Dante's,” she said.

Tank turned toward me. I didn't look away from her.

“First time I kissed you, I was looking at his cross,” Shawna said. “Wanted something of him back. Didn't know what I was doing.”

Red smiled. Watched my hand beside the holster, waiting for it to fall away. Wanted me to turn against her while he stood there holding the evidence of another man's life.

My throat hurt.

“Now?” I asked.

Shawna swallowed. Blood marked the corner of her mouth.

“Looking at you,” she said.

Red's smile went away.

“Don't give him that shit.”

She kept her eyes on mine.

“You, Aiden.”
